# Decorative Marbled Endpaper

This page features a full-spread example of **marbled paper** <note>A decorative technique where colored pigments are floated on a liquid "size" and manipulated with tools before being transferred to paper.</note>, a hallmark of traditional bookbinding. 

The design is executed in a **combed pattern** <term>nonpareil: a refined marbling style characterized by tight, uniform arches created by drawing a fine-toothed comb through the pigments</term>. This specific "nonpareil" style was highly prized for its intricate, rhythmic detail. 

The color palette is a classic combination from the late 18th to mid-19th century, featuring:
*   **Vibrant vermilion red** as the primary background color.
*   **Royal blue** and **golden yellow** accents.
*   **White** highlights that provide contrast and depth.

The pattern consists of dense, repeating rows of small, sharp-peaked arches or crescent shapes that create a textured, wave-like effect across the entire surface. Such papers were used as **endpapers** <note>The sheets of paper pasted to the inside of the book boards to hide the raw edges of the binding material and provide a decorative transition to the written content.</note> both for their beauty and their ability to hide the wear and tear of the book's hinges over time.

<summary>A decorative nonpareil marbled endpaper in red, blue, and yellow, typical of 18th or 19th-century French bookbinding. It serves as a protective and aesthetic transition between the cover and the manuscript pages.</summary>
<keywords>marbled paper, bookbinding, nonpareil pattern, endpaper, 18th-century decorative arts, vermilion red, stationery history</keywords>

# FOREWORD.

Two years have passed since we undertook this work: painful concerns <note>Wolowski likely refers to the personal or political distractions of the early 1860s.</note> have delayed its completion; but, in the interval, we have neglected nothing to make this publication as complete as possible.

The *French* manuscript of the *Treatise* <note>original: "Traictie", an archaic spelling of "Traité".</note> of Nicole Oresme, which we have used, belongs to the Imperial Library (1); those found in the Public Library of Poitiers and the Burgundy Library in Brussels are both in Latin, as are the various known editions, with the exception of the one by Colard Mansion <note>Colard Mansion (active 1474–1484) was a famous Flemish printer and colleague of William Caxton.</note>.

This manuscript, featuring a neat script dating back to the fifteenth century, provides the titles and summaries in red; the initials are in blue. It bears the coat of arms of its first owner: Azure [blue] with a ribbon of Gules [red], placed in a diagonal band and accompanied by two gold dragons, whose tails form a second, smaller head, outlined in Gules: the whole design rests in the corner on a tree with gold fruit. It is a folio of 46 leaves; it comes from the Library of the Church of Paris <note>The library of the Cathedral of Notre-Dame de Paris.</note>.

A precious annotation reveals its origin; one reads, in fact, this line: *Cl. Joly <note>Claude Joly (1607–1700) was a prominent canon of Notre-Dame and a notable book collector.</note>, in September 1664, — given to me by Mr. Berthier, canon of Chaumont in Bassigni.* The binding, in sheepskin <term>basane: a type of sheepskin leather often used in bookbinding</term>, is remarkable; it also appears to date from the fifteenth century and is enhanced by finely executed designs. The inscription reads: *Nicol. Oresme. On Money.* <note>original: "Des monnoies".</note>

This manuscript is published here for the first time; we have preceded it with variations borrowed from the edition by Colard Mansion, of which the Imperial Library possesses the only copy preserved to this day. We have also included in this volume the Latin text, carefully revised.

The work of Oresme had been almost forgotten: the same is true—

---

(1) A kind communication from Mr. Léopold Delisle <note>Léopold Delisle (1826–1910) was a renowned historian and librarian at the Bibliothèque nationale.</note> has made known to us a second manuscript, slightly earlier but conforming to the one we used; they are evidently two copies of the same original text.

<summary>In this foreword, the editor describes the 15th-century French manuscript used for the edition, its heraldic markings, and its provenance from the collection of Claude Joly. He also mentions the inclusion of the Latin version and references to the rare Colard Mansion edition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the editor’s foreword by Louis Wolowski. Having described the physical manuscripts of Nicole Oresme’s work on the previous page, he now introduces a second related work included in the volume: a treatise on money by the astronomer Nicolaus Copernicus.</meta>

of another work, no less curious, which we are adding to this volume. The *Treatise on Money* <note>original: "Traité de la monnoie"; this refers to Copernicus’s "Monetae cudendae ratio".</note> written by Copernicus was generally ignored until recently. The illustrious Tadeusz Czacki <note>A prominent Polish historian and education reformer (1765–1813).</note> makes no mention of it in his great work on the laws of Poland and Lithuania (1), nor in his specific dissertation on the monetary question (2).

One of our learned colleagues, Mr. Bertrand <note>Joseph Bertrand (1822–1900), a renowned French mathematician.</note>, a member of the Academy of Sciences, has just published a very interesting notice under the title: *Copernicus and his Works* (3). In it, he speaks of the *Treatise on Money*, highlighting its eminent merit (4). We are publishing Copernicus’s work exactly as he wrote it in Latin; our English translation is placed alongside it. The existence of such a work by the great astronomer would be enough to awaken a legitimate curiosity; this curiosity is fully justified by the elevation and soundness of mind that characterizes this dissertation.

Copernicus’s views on money are very close to those of Nicole Oresme; they share the same healthy and vigorous insights, the same understanding of the importance of maintaining the instrument of exchange <term>droit de titre et de poids: correct in its fineness (purity of the metal) and weight</term>, and the same judgment regarding the nature of the prince's power concerning the regulation of monetary value. Bringing together the insights provided by these two great minds results in a study as productive as it is attractive.

May this volume, by facilitating such study, bring the attention of economists back to the field of historical investigations—a field still little explored from this perspective. This would be our greatest reward.

->Paris, June 24, 1864.<-

(3) *Journal of Scholars* <note>original: "Journal des savants"</note>, February 1864.
(4) *Ibid.*, p. 84, 85 and 86.

# NICOLE ORESME

<vocab>Part One, Nicole Oresme</vocab>

...of another work, no less curious, which we are adding to this volume. The *Treatise on Money* <note>original: "Traité de la monnoie"; this refers to Copernicus’s "Monetae cudendae ratio" (On the Minting of Coin), written in 1526.</note> written by Copernicus was generally ignored until recently. The illustrious Tadeusz Czacki <note>A prominent Polish historian and education reformer (1765–1813) known for his foundational work on the legal history of Poland and Lithuania.</note> makes no mention of it in his great work on the laws of Poland and Lithuania (1), nor in his specific dissertation on the monetary question (2).

One of our learned colleagues, Mr. Bertrand <note>Joseph Bertrand (1822–1900), a renowned French mathematician and historian of science who served as the Permanent Secretary of the Academy of Sciences.</note>, a member of the Academy of Sciences, has just published a very interesting notice under the title: *Copernicus and his Works* (3). In it, he speaks of the *Treatise on Money*, highlighting its eminent merit (4). We are publishing Copernicus’s work exactly as he wrote it in Latin; our English translation <note>The original French text refers to a French translation included in the 1864 edition; this version has been updated to English for the current reader.</note> is placed alongside it. The existence of such a work by the great astronomer would be enough to awaken a legitimate curiosity; this curiosity is fully justified by the elevation and soundness of mind that characterizes this dissertation.

Copernicus’s views on money are very close to those of Nicole Oresme; they share the same healthy and vigorous insights, the same understanding of the importance of maintaining the instrument of exchange <term>standards of fineness and weight: the legal requirements ensuring a coin contains the correct purity of precious metal and the correct mass</term> <note>original: "droit de titre et de poids"</note>, and the same judgment regarding the nature of the prince's power concerning the regulation of monetary value. Bringing together the insights provided by these two great minds results in a study as productive as it is attractive.

# INTRODUCTION.

---

Our learned friend, Mr. Roscher <note>Wilhelm Roscher (1817–1894), a founding father of the Historical School of Economics in Germany.</note>, professor of political economy at the University of Leipzig, was kind enough to communicate to us, in July 1862 <note>original: "En langue allemande"; the communication was in German.</note>, a work of great interest regarding his discovery of a writing by Nicole Oresme <note>Nicole Oresme (c. 1320–1382) was a philosopher, mathematician, and the Bishop of Lisieux. He was a key counselor to King Charles V of France.</note>, Bishop of Lisieux, *concerning money*; at the same time, he asked us to complete his research and to verify the "new" status he attributed to this literary exhumation.

Regarding this last point, we found it necessary to dispel an illusion. The work of which Mr. Roscher spoke was already known in France; it had notably been described and appraised in the *Essay on the Life and Works of Nicole Oresme*, published in 1857 by Mr. Francis Meunier. Mr. Lecointre-Dupont also mentions it in his *Letters on the Monetary History of Normandy and Perche* (Paris, 1846, octavo <note>A book size where the printed sheet is folded to create eight leaves.</note>, p. 49). However, no one had yet highlighted its scientific character in such a striking manner; no one, before our learned correspondent, had subjected it to a thorough examination from the perspective of political economy.

The name and the works of Nicole Oresme were not foreign to us, but Mr. Roscher’s communication revealed their full importance. To respond to the desire of our...

<summary>Wolowski discusses how German economist Wilhelm Roscher brought Nicole Oresme's medieval treatise on money to his attention, noting that while the manuscript was known to French historians, its significance as a foundational text of political economy had been overlooked.</summary>
<keywords>Nicole Oresme, Wilhelm Roscher, political economy, monetary history, monetary theory, Francis Meunier, Lecointre-Dupont, medieval economics</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Louis Wolowski's introduction. Following his discussion of the initial correspondence with Wilhelm Roscher, Wolowski now explains how his own research expanded upon Roscher's findings by utilizing a rare French manuscript of Nicole Oresme's work found in the Imperial Library.</meta>

...learned friend, we engaged in new investigations; they led us to dedicate a *Study* to the *Treatise on Money* <note>original: "Traité des monnaies"</note> by this ignored fourteenth-century economist—a work received with kind attention by the Academy of Moral and Political Sciences (1).

Alongside the copy printed in Latin, which Mr. Roscher had known, we took advantage of the unique manuscript in the Imperial Library <note>The *Bibliothèque impériale*, now the *Bibliothèque nationale de France* in Paris.</note> containing the French translation of Nicole Oresme’s work. He produced this translation himself for King Charles V; it offers a more complete text, which gains a new level of interest from these circumstances <note>It was common for medieval scholars to write in Latin for peers and translate into the "vernacular" (French) for the royal court.</note>.

Our *Study* leaves to Mr. Roscher's work all the merit of the discovery and all the appeal that comes from original insights, as well as the proven expertise of one of the economists most justly esteemed beyond the Rhine <note>A common 19th-century French way of referring to Germany.</note>. We did not hesitate to translate our learned friend's writing, not only out of a natural sense of gratitude but also because of the work's utility. One cannot pay enough tribute to the author's insight, refinement of judgment, and precise erudition. While we differ in opinion on some secondary points—notably regarding certain circumstances of Nicole Oresme’s life—we are pleased to note the conformity of doctrine that already led us, seven years ago, to introduce Mr. Wilhelm Roscher’s *Principles of Political Economy* to France. Our study on Nicole Oresme’s *Treatise on Money* would be incomplete without the successful contribution brought to it by the work of the eminent professor from Leipzig. We shall strive to reproduce it with fidelity.

---

(1) We are publishing it following this *Introduction*.

## A GREAT FRENCH ECONOMIST OF THE FOURTEENTH CENTURY

->*Communication made by Mr. WILHELM ROSCHER, professor at the University of Leipzig, correspondent of the Institute of France (Academy of Moral and Political Sciences) (1).*<-

In order to fully understand the present state of any science and to grasp its future, it is indispensable to know its past. Furthermore, when one succeeds in ascending higher toward the unnoticed sources of some truth, one experiences a satisfaction almost equal to that provided by the merit of expanding its course.

Such is the feeling that leads me to communicate to the Academy of Moral and Political Sciences (which has kindly honored me with the title of correspondent) a *find* <note>original: "trouvaille"</note> of a certain importance for the history of political economy and the very development of the genius of France. It is a *find*, for I am far from laying claim to having made a discovery. A happy accident led me to encounter, on a little-explored path, this precious stone buried in the dust. My entire merit is limited to pointing out a diamond of the purest water, unrecognized for so many years, and which should have long ago occupied the place belonging to it in the scientific crown of France.

Occupied with research on the history of political economy in Germany (2), I found, in writings from the end

of the seventeenth century, the mention of a treatise by Nicole Oresme: *On the Origin and Law, and also on the Alterations of Money* (1) <note>original: "De origine et jure, nec non et de mutationibus monetarum"</note>. This mention was made in a somewhat indifferent manner; thus I only approached the reading of this short work, composed of twenty-three chapters, as a task beyond what was required <note>original: "opus supererogatorium," a Latin term for work done above and beyond the call of duty</note>, for the sake of a clear conscience. What was my surprise when I found myself in the presence of a theory of money, developed in the fourteenth century, which still remains perfectly correct today, under the rule of principles recognized in the nineteenth century, and this with a brevity, a precision, a clarity, and a simplicity of language, which testify loudly to the superior genius of the author. The whole departs so far from the usual idea one has of the financial barbarity of the Middle Ages, that one would be inclined to suppose some trickery, if such a suspicion could be supported by the slightest appearance of truth, and if the appearance of such a remarkable work had not been of a nature to excite almost equal sur-

page xiij

prise at the beginning of the sixteenth century than at the fourteenth.

Nicole Oresme, whom the *Universal Biography* (Paris, 1822) calls "one of the foremost writers of the fourteenth century," was likely born in Caen; at the very least, he was always classified within the Norman nation <note>At the medieval University of Paris, students and faculty were organized into four "nations" based on their place of birth; the Norman nation was one of these groups.</note> at the University of Paris. After obtaining his doctorate in theology, he became the Grand Master of the College of Navarre in 1355, where he had been educated. Successively Archdeacon of Bayeux, Dean of the Chapter of Rouen, and Treasurer of the Sainte-Chapelle in Paris, he was chosen by King John (1360) as the tutor <note>original: "précepteur"</note> for his son, who later reigned under the name of Charles V (1). In 1377,

(1) Mr. Francis Meunier appears to have fully demonstrated (*Essay on the Life and Works of Nicole Oresme*, pp. 24-28) that this indication was erroneous. Oresme never took the title of tutor to Charles V, any more than that of Bishop and Archdeacon of Bayeux, or member of the clergy of the Sainte-Chapelle of Paris. No manuscript, no edition of his works, no historian, nor any author from the end of the fourteenth century to the beginning of the sixteenth attributes it to him. One must go down as far as du Haillan—that is, to 1576—and Lacroix du Maine (1584) to find him called "instructor" by the one and "tutor" by the other to Charles V. He instructed him through his writings, but the date of 1360, indicated by Huet as the start of Oresme’s tutorship, is the date of King John’s return and the Peace of Brétigny <note>The 1360 treaty between King Edward III of England and King John II of France, which ended the first phase of the Hundred Years' War.</note>. Charles V was then twenty-three years old; he had been Regent of France since the disastrous Battle of Poitiers (1356); he had been shaped in the school of experience and adversity. — It must be said, however, that the error corrected by Mr. Francis Meunier was shared by almost all writers who have concerned themselves with Nicole Oresme. One of the most complete biographical notices is found in the history of the Bishop-Counts of Lisieux, published by Richard Séguin in 1832. This small volume, printed in Vire, is very rare today. The author drew from good sources, but he was wrong not to subject Huet’s indications to a critical examination. These are still reproduced in the *History of Lisieux* by Mr. Louis Dubois (2 octavo <note>original: "in-8º," referring to the size of the book formed by folding a sheet of paper into eight leaves.</note> volumes, 1845) without being supported by any proof. <meta>This page continues the biography of Nicole Oresme, beginning with the completion of his name from the final word of the previous page's footnote.</meta>

page xiv

me was named Bishop of Lisieux: he died there on July 11, 1382. In 1363, at Avignon, in the presence of Pope Urban V and the entire Sacred College <note>The College of Cardinals.</note>, Oresme delivered an energetic speech regarding the misconduct of the princes of the Church; this boldness led to him being accused of heresy. He translated Aristotle's *Ethics*, *Politics*, and the treatises *On the Heavens and the Earth*, as well as Petrarch's book: *On the Remedies of Both Fortunes, Prosperous and Adverse* (1), and perhaps also the Holy Bible. He was at least commissioned for this work by Charles V, who wished to oppose this version in the common tongue <note>original: "langue vulgaire"</note> to that of the Waldensians <note>A medieval Christian movement often persecuted as heretical; they were known for translating the Bible into local dialects.</note>. His original works are for the most part dedicated to theology (2): among their number are the *Treatise on the Alteration of Money* <note>original: "Tractatus de mutatione monetarum"</note>, and several mathematical writings on the sphere and against astrologers, which Pico della Mirandola honored with his recommendation.

At the beginning of the short work to which this communication is dedicated, the author announces that he will strive, supported by the philosophy of Aristotle, to resolve the much-debated question—

(Francis Meunier, p. 139.)

<meta>This page continues the sentence from page xiv, concluding the "much-debated question" regarding the prince’s power over currency. It outlines Nicole Oresme’s foundational theories on the nature and function of money.</meta>

page XV

whether the prince can alter the currency in circulation at his own pleasure and for his own benefit.

The progress of civilization led to the establishment of money in order to overcome the difficulties of simple barter <note>Oresme is discussing the transition from "pure and simple exchange"—trading goods for goods—to a monetary system.</note>; it cannot serve to directly satisfy the needs of existence: as the story of Midas teaches us, one can die of hunger alongside the wealthiest of treasures (Chapter I). This is why money is called **artificial wealth**; it is a tool artfully invented to facilitate the exchange of **natural wealth** (1). Natural wealth refers to those things that directly meet human needs (2). The material used to create such a commercial instrument must be easy to handle <note>original: "attrectabile et palpabile"</note> and easy to transport; for a small portion of this material, one must be able to obtain a large amount of natural wealth (3); it must, therefore, be a precious and valuable material <note>original: "materia preciosa et cara"</note>, such as gold, for example. However, it must be found in sufficient quantity; otherwise, it would be necessary to move from gold to silver, and from silver to other simple or composite metals. Furthermore, the use of gold and silver for other purposes should be prohibited if they were to become scarce in the form of coinage. But it is in no way useful to the State for the material that constitutes money to become too abundant, for it would not be able to maintain the same value. This is the reason why copper coinage was abandoned in the past, and it is undoubtedly for

---

(1) An instrument artificially invented for the easy exchange of natural wealth. <note>original Latin: "Instrumentum artificialiter adinventum pro naturalibus divitiis leviter permutandis."</note>

(2) Those things which by themselves naturally assist human necessity. <note>original Latin: "Quibus per se subvenitur naturaliter humanæ necessitati."</note>

(3) And for a modest portion of it, natural wealth may be held in greater quantity. <note>original Latin: "Et pro modica ejus portione habeantur divitiæ naturales in quantitate majori."</note>

<summary>This page defines the philosophical distinction between "natural wealth" (goods that sustain life) and "artificial wealth" (money), arguing that currency is a tool for exchange that must maintain its value and scarcity.</summary>
<keywords>Nicole Oresme, artificial wealth, natural wealth, monetary theory, Midas, gold and silver, barter, debasement of currency</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the material nature of money from page xv, specifically explaining why gold and silver are the chosen metals and the practical necessity of smaller denominations.</meta>

page xvj

this reason that it has been granted to the human race to only encounter gold and silver—the two materials best suited for the function of currency—with great difficulty and never in vast quantities, and that alchemy cannot produce them (Chapter II).

The simultaneous use of gold, silver, and <term>billon<gloss>a mixture of a precious metal, usually silver, with a larger amount of base metal like copper</gloss></term> coinage stems from the varying importance of commercial transactions. It was useful to have a high-value currency that could be more easily transported and accounted for in large negotiations. Similarly, a less costly silver currency serves for the purchase of cheaper goods. Since it frequently happens that a country does not possess a sufficient quantity of silver relative to its total <term>natural wealth<gloss>tangible goods like food or fuel that directly meet human needs</gloss></term> (1), and because a silver coin small enough to be exchanged for a loaf of bread, for instance, would be impractical to handle, the idea arose to add an inferior alloy to the silver. From this comes billon currency (<note>original: *moneta nigra*, or "black money"</note>), which is used for the smallest transactions. The author is careful to insist on a warning here against a possible abuse of power: 

> "Any alloy of this nature is suspicious in itself, and one cannot easily distinguish the substance and quality of the gold <note>Oresme uses "gold" here as a general term for precious value; he argues that debasing high-value metals with alloys makes their true value hard to identify</note>. Therefore, one should only resort to it in cases of well-recognized necessity. Where, for example, gold and silver species are used, one should not strike any gold billon, but only silver billon"

(Chapter III). This thought is very sound, for the purpose of a "gold billon" <note>An alloyed, low-value gold coin</note> is already fulfilled in such cases by the silver currency.

---

(1) <note>original: *Non satis competenter habetur de argento secundum portionem divitiarum naturalium.* ("A sufficient amount of silver is not held in proportion to the natural wealth.")</note>

<summary>This page explains the practical need for a multi-tiered currency system (gold, silver, and billon) to facilitate different scales of trade and warns against the potential for fraud when using metal alloys.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Nicolas Oresme’s "Treatise on the Origin, Nature, Law, and Alterations of Money." Having discussed the choice of metals on the previous page, the text now moves to the transition from raw weight to official coinage and the legal relationship between the sovereign, the community, and the currency.</meta>

page xvij

After the introduction of monetary exchange, silver, copper, and other metals began by being delivered and received by weight. But, later, the inconveniences of weighing and <term>assaying<gloss>the chemical process of testing a metal to determine its purity and quality</gloss></term> led to the adoption of a stamp: "It was ordained that pieces of money should be made of a specific material and a defined weight, and that an image should be stamped upon them which, being familiar to everyone, would indicate the quality of the metal and the accuracy of the weight; thus, with doubt removed, the value of the money could be recognized without effort." <note>original Latin: *Provisum est quod portiones monetæ fierent de certa materia et denominati ponderis et quod in eis imprimeretur figura, quæ cunctis notior significaret qualitatem materiæ numismatis, et ponderis veritatem, ut amota suspicione posset valor monetæ sine labore cognosci.*</note> Consequently, not every precious object is equally suited to serve as money: one cannot use precious stones in this way, nor spices (chap. IV). To avoid fraud, it has not been permitted since remote antiquity for any private individual to strike the gold and silver they possess into the form of money themselves; instead, this manufacturing is reserved for one or several persons invested with public authority. Money was by its very nature (<note>original: *de natura sua*</note>) invented and introduced for the benefit of the community (<note>original: *communitatis*</note>), and as the prince is, to the highest degree, a public person invested with the greatest dignity, it naturally follows that he is charged with having the coins struck for the use of society. The imprint must be finely executed and difficult to imitate. Counterfeiting committed by a foreign sovereign is a legitimate cause for war (chap. V). But the prince, though master of the <term>minting<gloss>the process of manufacturing coins</gloss></term>, is in no way the owner of the coins that circulate with his image; the words of Christ: “Render unto Caesar what is Caesar’s,” apply not to the currency itself, but to taxes (chap. VII).

Regarding the coinage, Oresme says with his usual precision: “Since money belongs to the community, it must be struck at the community’s expense.” It is not that he intends to have the community pay the costs incurred by the min-

<summary>This page explains the historical transition from weighing raw metal to the creation of official coinage, emphasizing that money is a public tool created for the community's benefit rather than the prince's private property.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Nicolas Oresme’s medieval economic theory. The previous page established that money was created for the common good and its manufacture reserved for public authority to ensure trust. This page now addresses how mintage costs should be handled and the strict conditions under which a ruler may alter the currency.</meta>

page xviij

mintage costs from the public treasury; far from it: it is necessary, he says, to divide the mass of metal into as many pieces of money as required, not only to cover all the expenses, but also to leave a certain profit; but this profit must be very moderate (chap. vii).

One must only allow monetary variations in cases of absolute necessity, in the presence of an undisputed general utility. “The circulation of money in the State must be like an invariable law and rule,” if only because such a great number of salaries and annual incomes are fixed according to the value of money—that is to say, according to a determined number of <term>livres<gloss>the primary unit of account in medieval France</gloss></term> and <term>sous<gloss>a fractional unit of currency; 20 sous typically made one livre</gloss></term> (chap. viii). One distinguishes five types of monetary variations: 1st **shape** <note>original Latin: *figuræ*</note>, 2nd **proportion** <note>original Latin: *proportionis*</note>, 3rd **denomination** <note>original Latin: *appellationis*</note>, 4th **weight** <note>original Latin: *ponderis*</note>, 5th **material** <note>original Latin: *materiæ*</note>. Oresme only approves a change in the form of coins—with the old ones being taken out of circulation—in two cases: when the coins are worn out by prolonged use, or when their circulation is hindered by frequent counterfeiting of the stamp by forgers. In these two cases, a change of the stamp allows one to easily distinguish the new, good money from the old (chap. ix). The ratio of value between different coins, for example between gold and silver species, must follow the natural ratio of the price of gold and silver <note>original Latin: *naturalem habitudinem auri ad argentum in preciositate*, or the natural relationship of gold to silver in terms of value</note>. It should only be modified for real motives, resulting from a change in the price of the material itself <note>original Latin: *propter causam realem et variationem ex parte ipsius materiæ*</note>: for example, when the mining of gold decreases significantly, making it much more expensive compared to silver. Arbitrarily intervening in these relationships would be...

<summary>Oresme argues that mintage should cover its own costs with a small profit and that the value of money should remain stable to protect fixed incomes, only allowing for changes in weight, material, or ratio when dictated by natural market shifts.</summary>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Nicolas Oresme’s medieval economic theory, specifically focusing on the ethics of currency manipulation. The previous page introduced the five types of monetary variations: shape, proportion, denomination, weight, and material. This page examines the final three in detail, characterizing unauthorized changes as acts of tyranny.</meta>

to commit an odious exaction, and to practice a true tyranny (chap. x). The simple change of the **denomination** of coins cannot be tolerated either: for example, if one wishes to call *a livre* <gloss>the primary unit of account in medieval France</gloss> that which is not a livre, or if one modifies the accepted proportion between several known <term>species<gloss>referring to different types of coins, such as gold vs. silver</gloss></term>. Indeed, salaries or incomes fixed in money are, by means of these changes in denomination, increased or decreased without valid reason or justice, and to the detriment of a great number of persons. The prince, above all, must in no case embolden himself to such an act <note>original Latin: *hoc attentare*, meaning "to attempt this"</note> (chap. xi).

The same must be said of the alteration of the **weight** of a coin, while its name and price remain invariable: this is exactly the same as if one were to falsify the measure of wheat or wine. From the moment the inscription on the coin designates the quantity of the weight and the purity of the material, who could express energetically enough how unjust and odious it would be, especially on the part of the prince, to diminish the weight while keeping the same sign (chap. xii)? A change of **material** may become necessary if that which has been used for minting becomes too rare or too abundant. But if one deteriorates the alloy of the metals that constitute the currency, it is a fraud even more culpable than the reduction of weight, "for it is more veiled, less easy to notice, and therefore more harmful." The prince who commits such a fraud makes himself guilty of a true perjury and a sacrilege when the imprint of the coin bears a cross, or the name of God, the Holy Virgin, or a saint (chap. xiii).

In any case, every modification of the currency, whether it falls purely and simply into one of the five types

He specifically notes that when a ruler places holy symbols on a debased coin, the act becomes a sacrilegious breach of public trust.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Nicolas Oresme’s De Moneta, moving from the physical description of currency alterations to the legal and moral authority required to make such changes. It establishes that the right to change money belongs to the community, not the prince alone.</meta>

— XX —

mentioned above, or whether it combines several of them, could never emanate from the sole injunction of the prince; it must always be accomplished **by the community itself** <note>original Latin: *per ipsam communitatem*</note> (chap. xiv). When the community transfers its right to the prince for specific cases, he does not exercise them as the **principal actor** <note>original Latin: *principalis actor*</note>, but as the **executor of public order** <note>original Latin: *ordinationis publicæ executor*</note> (chap. xv). Oresme absolutely condemns any profit arising from the change of currencies. "Sometimes, to avoid a greater evil and prevent scandal, establishments contrary to honesty and virtue are tolerated in the State (1). Sometimes also necessity or utility allows for a simple manipulation, such as exchange, or even a bad convention, such as <term>usury<gloss>the practice of lending money at interest, which was historically considered a sin or a crime</gloss></term>. But regarding the alteration of currencies, done in view of an illegitimate profit, there exists no reason in the world that obliges or allows it to be tolerated. Outrages of this nature seem to have precipitated the fall of the Roman Empire" (chap. xvi). How could a prince not feel enough shame if he committed an act that he would have to punish in others with the most infamous capital penalty (chap. xvii). Oresme rightly remarks that the people do not realize this **fiscal exaction** <note>meaning a forced or unjust tax</note>, brought about by the alteration of currencies, as quickly as they do the burden of a tax; but it is the same with many chronic diseases, which are all the more perilous the longer it takes to notice them. This **monetary disorder** leads, among other things, to the export of precious metals, which no prohibition could prevent, to the melting down and counterfeiting of coins abroad, which results in foreigners seizing—

---

(1) *For example, brothels.*

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Nicolas Oresme’s "De Moneta," specifically focusing on the disastrous social and economic consequences of a prince unilaterally altering the value of currency. It transitions from the legal theory of the previous page to the practical impacts on trade and social class.</meta>

-> xxj <-

of the profit that the sovereign claimed to secure for himself. Let us also add the most violent disturbances of both domestic and foreign trade: as long as this **monetary revolution** <note>Oresme uses this term to describe the chaos caused by sudden changes in currency value</note> lasts, it is impossible to accurately estimate income, salaries, rents, or interest; nor can one lend money with any security (chap. xviii). "Many men, often the least honest, grow rich by speculating on the variation of <term>species<gloss>referring to different types of gold and silver coins</gloss></term>; many others, and among the best, fall into poverty, and this double movement operates in the opposite direction of right and the legitimate course of natural relations." What a fertile mine of disputes and lawsuits (chap. xix)! Thus, even in cases of absolute necessity, the prince must never resort to an **alteration of currencies** to seize the resources of his subjects; he must appeal to them through a loan, which allows everything to be restored later to its original state by means of a restitution (chap. xxi).

Among the principles—partly political, partly belonging to **political economy**—professed by Oresme, there are some that deserve to be particularly highlighted; he distinguishes between honorable professions, useful to the State, and degrading professions. To the former belong those who increase the mass of actual goods or who favor their development in accordance with the needs of society, such as the clergy, judges, soldiers, farmers, merchants, artisans, etc. He relegates to the second category the <term>money-changers<gloss>original Latin: campsores</gloss></term>, the <term>money-traders or bullion-dealers<gloss>original Latin: mercatores monetæ seu billonatores</gloss></term>, and those who handle money, who limit themselves to increasing their own wealth by a meager profit <note>Oresme views these middle-men as parasitic because they do not produce goods, but rather profit from the manipulation of currency</note> (chap. xix).

With all the great economists, Oresme is an adver—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nicolas Oresme’s social and political theories. It transitions from the classification of professions (ending with money-changers) to his staunch opposition to arbitrary power and his belief in the stability of laws and currency as the foundation of a healthy state.</meta>

-> xxij <-

declared adversary of **arbitrary power**. He regards it as the cornerstone of all statesmanship (*above all, it must be known* <note>original Latin: *ante omnia sciendum est*</note>) never to change—without an evident necessity—the laws, customs, statutes, etc., which concern the community. One must not replace an ancient law, even with a better law, unless there is a very notable improvement, for these changes shake the respect for legality, especially when they become frequent. This often results in scandal, murmurs, and the danger of disobedience (chap. VIII). Oresme reminds us above all, and with pride, of his former office as the <term>tutor<gloss>French: précepteur</gloss></term> to a monarch, by multiplying the most energetic warnings against the danger of **monarchic absolutism**, which was already threatening France at that time. The general interest, if well-advised, will never place the entire right of <term>coinage<gloss>French: monnayage; the sovereign right to mint and regulate money</gloss></term> into the hands of the prince alone; men, free by nature, will never consent to become slaves, or to submit to the yoke of a tyrannical power. If it should happen that the community, misled by error or dominated by threat and violence, had transferred an arbitrary power to the monarch regarding the currency, it could withdraw this concession; for one could no more seriously grant the monarch the power to dispose of the value of <term>specie<gloss>French: espèces; physical gold or silver coins</gloss></term> at his whim, than one could surrender to him the right to abuse the wives of his subjects. (Could Oresme have known the intimate link that connects private property and monogamy—*table and bed; commerce and marriage* <note>original Latin: *mensa et torus ; commercium et connubium*</note>?) The duty of the prince is to maintain, by means of public revenue, a *magnificent and most honorable state* <note>original Latin: *magnificum et honestissimum statum*</note>. This revenue may rest in part on the right of coinage, but only in a manner consistent with a firmly established law. The author rises with energy against the flatterers, who distill sophisms...

He argues that the right to control money belongs to the community and compares the prince's potential abuse of currency to an assault on the private lives and families of his subjects.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Nicolas Oresme’s treatise. It follows the argument that a monarch’s power over the currency is not absolute and that any attempt to claim such power as a royal right is a betrayal of the state. It concludes the section on Oresme's political theory and begins an analysis of his contributions to economic science.</meta>

-> xxiij <-

deceitful arguments, and against the so-called politicians who betray the State by presenting this limitation of power as a kind of disinheritance of authority, or as a species of <term>lese-majesty<gloss>from the Latin "laesa maiestas"; a crime against the dignity of the sovereign or the state</gloss></term>. He also finds blameworthy any payment that the sovereign might demand in exchange for renouncing such an abusive exaction (chap. XXI). Tyranny, compared to monarchy, appears to him like a man whose head has grown so large that it can no longer be carried by the rest of the body. It is less stable than monarchy; thus **Theopompus** <note>King of Sparta in the 8th century BCE, credited with establishing the Ephors to limit royal power.</note> could rightly answer those who reproached him for leaving his children a throne less rich in revenue than he had inherited from his father: "I leave it more durable." — "Divine oracle!" cries Oresme, "words of the greatest weight, which should be inscribed in letters of gold in the palaces of kings: *I leave it more durable*"; it is as if he had said: "By moderating my power, I have increased it more in duration than I have diminished it in extent." This is a wisdom higher than that of Solomon (chap. XXII).

Oresme says expressly that the servitude imposed on the French people cannot last. Whatever the power of tyranny, it strikes against the free aspirations of the hearts of the subjects, and it weakens itself in the face of the foreigner (chap. XXIII).

A few more words on the place Oresme holds in science.

Truth is, in a certain sense, older than error; this can easily be shown regarding the study of wealth and monetary relations. The first generation that gradually became accustomed to using a common commodity, easily accepted by all, as money, was perfectly aware of the

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Nicolas Oresme’s economic and political thought, transitioning from his theories on royal power to his place in the history of economic science. It discusses the early understanding of money as a commodity before the dominance of the mercantile system.</meta>

<page-num>xxiv</page-num>

<margin>
(1) *Memoirs of the Royal Society of Saxony*, history and philology class, III, p. 22 and following. <note>Original title: "Abhandlungen der Königlich Sächsischen Gesellschaft der Wissenschaften."</note>
(2) See the memoir by Roscher <note>Wilhelm Roscher (1817–1894), a founder of the Historical School of Economics in Germany.</note>, inserted in the *Memoirs of the Royal Society of Saxony*, December 12, 1861 (history and philology class).
</margin>

nature of money, of its quality as a <term>commodity<gloss>In this context, an item of trade that has its own intrinsic value, rather than just being a token of exchange.</gloss></term>, endowed with a special aptitude for circulation. The mystic dreams, which claimed to find in the precious metal the very essence of wealth, were hardly possible then. But, it must be recognized, the exaggeration of the role of money and of circulation by the <term>mercantile system<gloss>An economic theory (mercantilism) that emphasized accumulating gold and silver and maintaining a positive trade balance.</gloss></term> originated, among most peoples, from conceptions that were not lacking in a certain truth. I have shown elsewhere (1) that in England, towards the end of the sixteenth century and at the beginning of the seventeenth, the notion of public wealth and the indication of its sources were formulated in terms as correct as in our own day. This was the work of the founders of England's colonial power, the leaders of American emigration: they were the principal intellectual representatives of the English people of that era. In the middle of the seventeenth century, there still flourished in England an economic triumvirate that may be pointed out as the gathering of the precursors of the school of **Adam Smith**, a triumvirate formed by **Petty**, **Locke**, and **North**; it was engaged in the struggle against the mercantile system, whose definitive arrival dates from the reign of **William of Orange** <note>William III (1650–1702), who became King of England in 1689.</note>. — Germany also offers, in the sixteenth century, the noblest traces of a perfect understanding of the relationship between money and wealth and of the benefits of economic freedom (2). One saw from then on developing, alongside these truths, the erroneous teachings of the mercantile system, which grew little by little and which ended

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of economic thought, moving from the developments in England to the French context. It specifically addresses how early economic ideas were often embedded within religious and moral frameworks, such as the Scholastic study of penance, before evolving into the "Colbertism" of the 17th century.</meta>

<page-num>XXV</page-num>

<sig>b</sig>

...ended up dominating during the second half of the seventeenth century. — In France, as we have said, ideas followed the same path, but their development began much earlier. It would not be accurate to regard the transition from the conceptions of a thinker like Oresme to those of <term>Colbertism<gloss>An economic policy named after Jean-Baptiste Colbert, minister to Louis XIV, which emphasized state regulation, manufacturing, and a favorable balance of trade.</gloss></term> as a simple step backward. Many practical pieces of advice originating from the <term>mercantile system<gloss>Also known as mercantilism; an economic theory that wealth is generated by accumulating gold and silver and maximizing exports.</gloss></term> corresponded to the needs of the time; it is even permissible to add that certain theorems had their roots in the social relations of the era. Even when dealing with an absolute error—such as the failure to recognize that money is itself a <term>commodity<gloss>An item produced for trade that has its own inherent value, rather than just being a symbol of value.</gloss></term>—it arose from the natural confusion of the human mind. At that time, man's horizon had suddenly expanded, and he had not yet sufficiently mastered the multitude of new impressions assailing him. If the proverb, "Truth emerges more quickly from error than from confusion," <note>original: *Citius ex errore veritas emergit, quam ex confusione*</note> is true, then the formal structure given to the errors of the mercantile system served as a stepping stone toward more accurate economic concepts.

The striking position held by Oresme regarding the priority of economic ideas is not, moreover, something that should cause surprise when one examines the facts closely. The <term>Scholastics<gloss>Medieval scholars who used a method of critical thought and logic to reconcile Christian theology with classical philosophy.</gloss></term>, and especially <term>Duns Scotus<gloss>John Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), one of the most important philosopher-theologians of the High Middle Ages.</gloss></term>, explored the path of economic knowledge much more than people are usually inclined to believe; it is true, however, that they often did so in a singular form. Within their massive dogmatic <term>folios<gloss>Very large books made from sheets of paper folded only once; typically used for significant academic or religious texts.</gloss></term>, they preferably devoted to this branch of study the section dealing with the sacraments, and notably the sacrament of penance. In these texts, they researched the conditions that must be imposed on a repentant sinner when he asks for absolution—specifically, to what extent he must be held responsible for the <insert>financial or material</insert> reparation of the harm he caused. One is thus led...

<summary>This page explores the transition from medieval economic thought to mercantilism in France, arguing that even the errors of mercantilism were a necessary step toward modern science. It highlights how Scholastic theologians like Duns Scotus laid the groundwork for economics by discussing restitution and justice within the sacrament of penance.</summary>

<keywords>Nicole Oresme, Colbertism, mercantilism, economic history, Scholasticism, Duns Scotus, sacrament of penance, restitution, money as commodity</keywords>

...by examining sins related to the economy, to scrutinize the very nature of economic institutions. Gabriel Biel, a famous professor at <term>Tübingen<gloss>A prominent university town in Germany; Biel taught there in the late 1400s.</gloss></term> at the end of the fifteenth century—who has been nicknamed "the last of the <term>Scholastics<gloss>Medieval thinkers who used a system of logic and philosophy to reconcile Christian theology with classical reason.</gloss></term>"—was also an economist of this kind. We can point to <term>Oresme<gloss>Nicole Oresme (c. 1320–1382), a French philosopher, scientist, and economist who wrote a famous treatise on money.</gloss></term> as the greatest Scholastic economist from a dual perspective: because of the accuracy and clarity of his ideas, and because he clearly knew how to free himself from pseudo-theological systematization in general, and from pseudo-philosophical deduction in detail.

The era in which Oresme lived belongs to the saddest and most tormented times in the history of France. But it is in critical moments, when the entire social organism threatens to dissolve, that the observer gathers the most instructive lessons—just as the study of physiology advances more rapidly by the sickbed and at the dissection table than it can in a sculptor's studio. The long struggles that marked the rise of the <term>House of Valois<gloss>The royal house that succeeded the House of Capet to the French throne, ruling from 1328.</gloss></term>; the fortunate return of so many great <term>fiefs<gloss>Feudal estates or territories held by lords.</gloss></term>, which became difficult to grant anew; the absolutism almost entirely established under Philip VI and the rebirth of the power of the <term>Estates<gloss>The "Estates General," a representative assembly of the three "estates" or orders of French society.</gloss></term> under his successors; the deplorable setbacks of foreign war, and the victory that eventually crowned them; the splendor of chivalry, already struck at the heart by an incurable weakness as depicted by <term>Froissart<gloss>Jean Froissart, the primary chronicler of the first half of the Hundred Years' War.</gloss></term>; the uprisings of Paris under Étienne Marcel, and the terrible *Jacquerie* <note>A violent peasant revolt in northern France in 1358.</note>—all of this was a collection of trials suited to laying bare the most intimate organs and the sequence of vital phenomena of the social body. At the same time, the Church was troubled by the question of the residence of the <term>Holy See<gloss>The central administration of the Catholic Church and the office of the Pope.</gloss></term>, raised between Avignon and Rome, by the...

<summary>This page describes how the 14th-century economic and social crises in France, including the Hundred Years' War and the Jacquerie, allowed thinkers like Nicole Oresme to analyze the "vital organs" of the state and develop early economic theories. It frames these social upheavals as a "dissection table" that revealed the inner workings of society more clearly than peaceful times ever could.</summary>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the chaotic historical context of 14th-century France, shifting from the general social unrest mentioned on the previous page to the specific economic disasters—plague, famine, and extreme currency instability—that shaped Nicole Oresme’s economic theories and his influence on King Charles V.</meta>

<page-num>xxvii</page-num>

the disputes of Emperor **Louis** <note>Louis IV, Holy Roman Emperor (reigned 1314–1347), who was famously embroiled in a long conflict with the Papacy.</note> with the pope, and the movements of the **Lollards**, the **Wycliffites** <note>Followers of John Wycliffe, whose calls for reform prefigured the Protestant Reformation.</note>, and others like them. How much must a man in a high position, gifted with such penetrating insight and a depth of mind free from mere empty rhetoric as **Oresme** was, have learned from such a spectacle! To speak only of purely economic facts, one need only recall the terrible plagues of the years 1348 and 1361 to find the material for a serious study of the question of population! And what lessons were to be found in the great upheaval of capital brought about by the expulsion of the **Jews** and the **Lombards** <term>Lombards<gloss>Italian merchants and bankers from Northern Italy who dominated European finance during the Middle Ages.</gloss></term>, the numerous famines, and the crises of high grain prices caused by war, sedition, and poor harvests! Confining ourselves to the main subject of this report: the exhaustion of finances, transformed into a chronic illness by the extravagances of the court and the disasters of war, led to constant <term>currency debasement<gloss>The practice of lowering the value of currency, often by reducing the amount of precious metal in coins.</gloss></term>, sometimes weakening and sometimes increasing the standard of the coins, depending on whether the Crown was looking at its expenses or its receipts. During the single year of 1348, there were no fewer than eleven variations in the monetary rate; nine the following year; eighteen in 1351; thirteen in 1353; and eighteen again in 1355; such that in the course of a single year, the minting value of a <term>silver mark<gloss>A traditional unit of weight for precious metals, roughly 8 ounces or 244 grams.</gloss></term> rose from 4 <term>livres<gloss>The primary currency of France before the decimal system was introduced; literally "pounds."</gloss></term> to 17 and 2/5 livres, only to fall back later to 4 and 3/5 livres (1). At times, these measures were combined with a forced price control on all commercial goods, as in 1330 (2). **Oresme's** pupil, **Charles V**, knew how to ward off many of these evils when he succeeded to the crown, and it is above all for this reason that he earned the nickname **Charles the Wise**!

<meta>This page transitions from the historical analysis of Nicole Oresme’s 14th-century environment to a 19th-century epilogue. The author describes a personal journey to Lisieux to locate Oresme’s final resting place, correcting historical misconceptions about the destruction of his tomb.</meta>

### EPILOGUE.

---

After sharing with the **Academy of Moral and Political Sciences** <note>The *Académie des sciences morales et politiques* is a prestigious French learned society founded to study the humanities and social sciences.</note> this translation of the report by Mr. **Guillaume Roscher**, we informed them of some new research we conducted regarding the forgotten author of the *Treatise on Money* <note>original: "Traité des monnaies"</note>. Eager to discover the specific details related to the life of **Oresme**, we visited **Lisieux**; there, we hoped to find his tomb, located in the cathedral. Our search was in vain. The memory of Oresme barely survives in the minds of a few archaeologists; as for his tomb, some accounts claim it disappeared during the chaos of the **Revolution**; however, in this instance, as in many others, people have tried to blame the Revolution for events that were completely unrelated to it. A distinguished resident of Lisieux, **Mr. Pannier**, who pursues archaeological studies with enlightened zeal, possesses a copy of an old manuscript found a few years ago by a notary in Lisieux within an inventory. This document covers a period of forty-one years, from 1676 to 1717; it contains interesting information on the history of that city. In it, one finds proof that **Nicole Oresme's** tomb was destroyed in 1677, during the episcopacy of **Léonor II de Matignon**. Here is the passage pointed out to us by Mr. Pannier:

> "In this year 1617 <note>Though the OCR reads 1617, the context of the manuscript (1676–1717) and the previous paragraph indicate this event actually occurred in 1677.</note>, the Lord Bishop had the entire cathedral church newly repaired, and for this purpose, all the tombs that were in the nave and in the

<summary>The author details a visit to Lisieux to find Nicole Oresme’s tomb, discovering through a local manuscript that it was destroyed during church renovations in 1677 rather than during the French Revolution.</summary>
<keywords>Nicole Oresme, Lisieux Cathedral, tomb, Léonor II de Matignon, Mr. Pannier, historical research, preservation</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the excerpt from a late 17th-century manuscript detailing changes made to the Lisieux Cathedral under Bishop Léonor II de Matignon. It concludes the epilogue regarding the fate of Nicole Oresme's tomb.</meta>

wings, some were of beautiful stone and others of copper. *Likewise, the stone and copper tombs of several bishops who were in the choir have been removed.* »

Léonor II de Matignon founded numerous public utility establishments in Lisieux and built the most modern part of the former episcopal palace, which was considered one of the most beautiful and richest in the kingdom. But he sacrificed to the poor taste of the era by committing true acts of vandalism within the Church of Saint-Pierre. Thus, he had the ancient stained glass windows, which adorned the windows of the nave, replaced by plain white glass, and destroyed a magnificent stone <term>rood screen</term> <note>original: "jubé"; an ornate screen, often carved from wood or stone, that separated the chancel or choir from the nave.</note> at the entrance to the choir.

The tomb of Nicole Oresme was located in the first bay of the choir, on the left (the Gospel side <note>The Gospel side of a church is the north side, to the left of the congregation facing the altar.</note>), near the door. This part of the building, like the nave and the transept, dates from the end of the twelfth century; the final two bays of the choir and the apse were constructed in the thirteenth century.

It was therefore in the middle of the eighteenth century <note>While the text says "middle of the eighteenth century," the previous page notes the destruction began in 1677, and Bishop Matignon died in 1714; the author likely refers to the broader era of "enlightened" renovations that swept through French cathedrals.</note> that a bishop of Lisieux, showing little interest in artistic matters and suppressing ancient stained glass for being "too dark," also had the funerary monuments placed inside the cathedral removed. Oresme’s tomb was included in this measure, which preceded the Revolution of 1789 by more than a century. How many similar desecrations have been committed since? It is thus in the very places where the memory of famous men should be piously preserved that forgetful posterity destroys the monuments of past times and the glories of old!

L. W. <note>Louis Wolowski (1810–1876), the Polish-French economist and historian who edited this edition of Oresme's work.</note>

<summary>The author identifies Bishop Léonor II de Matignon as the figure responsible for destroying Nicole Oresme's tomb and other medieval treasures in Lisieux Cathedral during the late 17th century, long before the French Revolution. The text laments the loss of historical monuments to the "bad taste" of modernizing clergy.</summary>

# BIOGRAPHICAL NOTES.

---

To complete these indications, we believe it useful to reproduce the literal copy of a biographical notice extracted from the *History of the Bishop-Counts of Lisieux*, published by Richard Séguin in 1832.

## NICOLAS ORESME.

### 33rd bishop, 19th count of Lisieux, in 1377.

> « Nicolas Oresme was a Bocain-Norman <note>Referring to the "Bocage," a region in Normandy characterized by woods and pastures.</note>, from the diocese of Bayeux; Mr. Huet, the famous bishop of Avranches, even believes he was from Caen, without daring to state it with certainty. At the time that Mr. Halley, professor of eloquence at the University of Caen, was printing his poetry, there were families of this name in Vaucelles and in the parish of Clinchamps; what is certain, adds Mr. Huet in his *Origins of Caen*, is that Nicolas Oresme was Norman, and that as long as he was at the University of Paris, he was always considered part of the Norman nation <note>original: "nation normande"; at medieval universities, students and masters were grouped into "nations" based on their place of birth.</note>. This learned bishop, after completing his studies in his own country, went to Paris; he was grand master of the College of Navarre; he earned such a great reputation there that King John <note>John II, known as John the Good (reigned 1350–1364).</note> named him tutor to Charles, his eldest son; he was worthy of it, for he was the most skilled in the sciences and fine arts in the University of Paris. This grateful prince <note>The future Charles V, known as "The Wise."</note> gave him marks of his gratitude. Oresme was made grand master of the College of Navarre, archdeacon of Bayeux, treasurer of the Sainte-Chapelle in Paris, dean of Rouen, and finally bishop of Lisieux.
>
> « Oresme composed several works: by order of the king, he worked on a translation of the Bible in order, says Mr. Huet in his *Origins* (chapter **XXIV**), to prevent the alterations that the Waldensians <note>original: "Vaudois"; a medieval Christian movement that was declared heretical by the Catholic Church.</note> and other heretics were making to the sacred books. The king sent him on an embassy to Pope Urban V in Avignon; there he delivered a vehement speech against the disorders of the clergy, which the Protestants have falsely interpreted in their favor. Jacques Gauthier, in his *Chronographical Table*, printed in 1637, and Father Pierre de Saint-Bomard, a Feuillant <note>A member of a reformed Cistercian monastic order.</note>, in his *Chronological and Historical Treasure*, have wrongly placed this great bishop among the heretics of the fourteenth century; but he has been de-

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Nicolas Oresme, focusing on his intellectual reputation, his diplomatic missions for the French Crown, and the early years of his episcopate in Lisieux.</meta>

...defended by Thomas Basin, who was one of his successors and a great enemy of sectarians; Basin notes that the venerable Oresme, one of his predecessors, was considered highly skilled in astrology and had composed a book on the subject. He was once again sent by the King to Avignon in 1366 to dissuade the Pope and the cardinals from moving the Holy See back to Rome. According to Monsieur Fleury <note>Claude Fleury (1640–1723), a famous church historian.</note>, he delivered a very dull speech, burdened with citations and poor arguments; indeed, it was very difficult to find good reasons for such a position. Some attribute to this prelate, though without evidence, a book titled *The Dream of the Orchard* (*Le Songe du Verger*), which is a treatise on ecclesiastical and civil power written as a dialogue between a cleric and a knight. It is now thought more likely that this work is by Raoul de Presles. Around 1370, Oresme translated portions of the works of Aristotle and Petrarch.

« He composed a treatise on the alteration of money <note>original: "changement des monnaies"; this work, *De Moneta*, is famous today as one of the first scientific treatises on economics, arguing that a king does not "own" the currency and shouldn't devalue it.</note>, which is found in volume XXVI of the *Library of the Fathers*, page 226; a work on the Immaculate Conception of the Blessed Virgin, cited by Monsieur Huet; one hundred and fifteen sermons; *The Art of Preaching*; a treatise against mendicants <note>Referring to the "Mendicant Orders" like the Franciscans or Dominicans, who lived by begging.</note>; a work on the sphere; three works against judicial astrology <note>Judicial astrology involved predicting the future and human destiny via the stars, which Oresme criticized in favor of natural science.</note>, which were praised by Thomas Basin and Pico della Mirandola; and a Latin treatise on the Antichrist, which is printed in volume IX of the great collection of the Fathers by Martenne and Durand of the Congregation of Saint-Maur.

« Finally, King Charles V, wishing to honor the merit of his former tutor, had him elected Bishop of Lisieux on November 16, 1377; he was consecrated on January 26 of that same year, which we would count today as 1378 <note>In the medieval French calendar, the new year often began at Easter; therefore, January 1377 was what we now call January 1378.</note>. The King honored him with his presence and presented him with two gold rings, set with precious stones, costing 390 gold livres—a very considerable sum at the time. We still possess the monarch's order to the receiver general of subsidies to pay this sum. In that same month of January, this prelate was sent to Saint-Denis to meet the Emperor Charles and his son Wenceslaus, who had come to work toward peace between France and England alongside the Prince of the Bocains <note>A reference to the King of Navarre, Charles the Bad, who held lands in the Norman Bocage.</note>, the King of Navarre. In the following February, Oresme endowed a mass for the funeral ceremony of the Queen of France, the wife of King Charles V, who died on the 6th of that month in 1377 (1378), and he took his oath to his metropolitan <note>The Archbishop of Rouen, who had authority over the Bishop of Lisieux.</note> on June 18. He had a dispute with his canons regarding the maintenance fund (*fabrique*) <term>fabrique: the fund or administrative body responsible for the upkeep of a church's building and ornaments</term> for the cathedral, which they claimed was entirely his responsibility; arbitrators were appointed, who decided that the damage caused by the wars should not be borne by the bishop-count, but should be taken from the offerings of the faithful. This learned prelate, after

Mr. Louis Dubois, in his *History of Lisieux* (2 volumes in-8°, 1845), claims that Nicolas Oresme was not from Bayeux, as asserted by the *Gallia Christiana* <note>original: "Gallia christiana"; a massive documentary history of the Catholic Church in France.</note>, but from the commune of Allemagne <note>Today known as Fleury-sur-Orne; it was renamed in 1916 to avoid the association with Germany (*Allemagne*) during World War I.</note>, near Caen. The same historian adds that Halley—whose opinion is shared by the learned Huet—is also incorrect in stating he was born in the city of Caen.

> "Oresme was considered," says Mr. Louis Dubois, "one of the most learned men of his century, as much in philosophy and mathematics as in theology. In 1360, he was chosen by King John to be the tutor of his son, who, upon becoming king, obtained and above all deserved the fine name of **Charles the Wise**. By 1356, having achieved his doctorate, Oresme was named Grand Master of the College of Navarre <note>A prestigious college of the University of Paris.</note>, where he taught theology, and was commissioned by his pupil, who had ascended the throne, to translate the Bible into the French language. Dean of the Church of Rouen in 1361, and canon of the Sainte-Chapelle in Paris, he left the College of Navarre where he had brought about a revival of high-quality studies, according to de Launoy (*History of the College of Navarre*). In 1363, he was sent to Pope Urban V in Avignon; there, before the pontiff and the cardinals, he delivered a speech as eloquent as it was bold against the ever-increasing excesses of the court of Rome, and predicted with much reason the unfortunate events for the Church that could not fail to break out—events which, indeed, a century later, dealt blows to this power from which it has never recovered. One finds this discourse in the work of Flaccius Illyricus (**Matthias Francowitz**), on the *Witnesses of the Truth* <note>original: "Témoins de la vérité" / *Catalogus testium veritatis*; a work by a Lutheran reformer identifying medieval figures who criticized the Papacy.</note>. Such were the ignorance and bad faith of those still-barbarous times that Oresme was accused of heresy by Jacques Gauthier in his *Chronographic Tables*, and in the *Chronological Tables* of Pierre de Saint-Romuald. Thomas Basin, successor and worthy admirer of Oresme, vindicated him of these accusations, which were so perilous at the time. The King, who had great confidence in the enlig—

> "Oresme was magnificently rewarded for his works. In 1377, he was appointed to the bishopric of Lisieux. At the beginning of 1378, he was sent to meet Emperor Charles IV, who had traveled to Saint-Denis. On April 3, 1381, he confirmed ten pounds of annual income for the canons of the church of Saint-Cande-le-Vieux in Rouen, whose privileges he defended against the archbishop."

In the biographical section of the *History of Lisieux* (Vol. II, pp. 238 and 239), Mr. Louis Dubois cites the works of this learned bishop.

The courageous speech he delivered in 1363 at Avignon against the excesses of the high clergy, before the Sacred College <note>The assembly of cardinals who advise the Pope.</note>, was printed in the *Catalogue of Witnesses to the Truth* <note>original: "Catalogus testium veritatis"; a collection of historical texts used by Protestant reformers to show that there had always been voices calling for church reform.</note>, published under the pseudonym Flaccius Illyricus by Matthias Flacius <note>original: "Francowitz"; a Lutheran reformer and scholar.</note> in 1556, and in subsequent editions of this collection directed against clerical abuses; it appeared separately in 1604, in Wittenberg, through the efforts of Salomon Gessner. The principal works of Oresme, printed after his death (for the printing press had not yet been discovered), are translations, such as Aristotle's *Ethics* <note>original: "Morale d'Aristote"</note> (1488, in folio), the *Politics* by the same philosopher (1488, 2 volumes in folio), and the books *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: "du Ciel et du Monde"</note>. Oresme composed, in Latin, a book on the <term>communication of properties</term> <note>original: "communication des idiomes"; a theological doctrine concerning how the human and divine attributes of Jesus Christ relate to one another.</note>, and one hundred and fifteen sermons, etc. As for the Latin book against the Antichrist, attributed to him by Martène and Durand who printed it in their "Very Ample Collection," it is now recognized, Mr. Louis Dubois adds, that it is not by our prelate, any more than the translation of the Bible, which appears to be by Raoul de Presle (see the *Mercure de France* of October 1750, and the entry for Oresme in the *Universal Biography* <note>original: "Biographie universelle"</note>, written by Mr. Pannier).

King John chose him, in 1360, to be the tutor of his son Charles V, who rewarded him with the bishopric of Lisieux in 1377. He possessed a very extensive knowledge, being a great theologian, philosopher, mathematician, and humanist. He translated from Latin into French, by the command of the

Mézeray <note>François Eudes de Mézeray, a prominent 17th-century French historian.</note> speaks of our author in these terms:

> "Charles V commanded Nicolas Oresme, formerly his tutor, to write against Jean Teramo and other ultramontanes <note>Those who supported the absolute authority of the Pope over national governments—literally those from "beyond the mountains" (the Alps).</note> who wanted to abolish the temporal power of princes; and it was then, according to tradition, that he composed the *Dream of the Orchard* <note>original: "Songe du verger"; a famous 14th-century political dialogue.</note>, which is not a daydream, but a powerful argument where he introduces the cleric and the gentleman disputing the authority of the pope and that of princes."

In his esteemed letters on the *Monetary History of Normandy and Perche*, Mr. Lecointre-Dupont says (1):

> "Nicolas Oresme, born in Bayeux, one of the most famous theologians of the University of Paris, had been the tutor of Charles V. Elevated in 1377 to the episcopal see of Lisieux, which he occupied until his death in 1382, he composed during this interval a very remarkable philosophical treatise on the changes in the exchange rate of coins, to demonstrate that a prince cannot, by his private authority, arbitrarily change the money in circulation in his States, regulate its value at his whim, and derive an unlimited profit from its manufacture." — And he adds in a note:
> "Nicolas Oresme's treatise exists in manuscript at the public library of Poitiers (XCV of the Manuscripts, no. 2), and it was printed in the *Great Library of the Fathers* <note>original: "Magna bibliotheca Patrum"</note>, Vol. IX, Paris edition. According to the authors of the *New Christian Gaul* <note>original: "Nova Gallia christiana"; a monumental history of the French dioceses.</note>, it was only written under the pontificate of Clement VII in Avignon, that is, in 1378, or rather when Oresme was already Bishop of Lisieux. One could, however, infer from the title it bears in the Poitiers manuscript that its composition predates Oresme's elevation to the episcopate. Indeed, here is its title: *Treatise on the Alteration of Monies, published by Master Nicolas Oresme, professor of sacred theology.* <note>original Latin: "Tractatus de mutationibus monetarum editus a Mag. Nicholao Oresme, sacre theologie professore." The title "Professor" suggests it was written while he was still teaching, before becoming a Bishop.</note>"

We believe that the *Christian Gaul* <note>original: "Gallia christiana"</note> has committed an error, and that this latter version is more accurate. Oresme's work dates from before 1373.

---

(1) Paris, published by Dumoulin, 1846, p. 49.

## STUDY

->ON<-

# THE TREATISE ON MONEY

## BY NICOLE ORESME (1).

---

There is no subject at once more important and more simple than that of money, and there is none that the "spirit of system" <note>This refers to the tendency to build rigid, abstract theories (systems) that may ignore practical reality or natural laws.</note> has more disturbed, to the great detriment of public economy. The most disastrous errors have been the result of a too common misconception, which has confused currency <term>specie; the physical coins in circulation</term> with wealth, and which sees, in gold and silver coins, only a conventional sign of exchange, subject to the will of the sovereign. From the moment where precious metals are taken for the sole element of fortune, nations are condemned to dispute over the fractions of a necessarily limited treasure. From the moment where currency presents only a conventional sign, depending on authority, the power that created it can modify and vary it; it can also replace it with other signs, by putting an end to what has been so strangely named the

(1) We read this *Memoir* in the annual public session of the five Academies of the Imperial Institute of France on August 14, 1862.
