<keywords>Nicole Oresme, monetary theory, currency, precious metals, public economy, sovereign authority, wealth, Imperial Institute of France</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the study of Nicole Oresme’s treatise. It completes the thought from the previous page regarding the "usurped royalty of gold," transitioning from the dangers of financial mismanagement to the classical definitions of money found in the works of Aristotle and the Roman jurist Paul.</meta>

usurped royalty of gold. Permanent hostility between nations, trade conflicts, the debasement of currency <note>original: "altération des espèces"; refers to the practice of reducing the amount of precious metal in coins while maintaining their face value.</note>, disguised bankruptcies, assignats <term>paper money issued by the French Revolutionary government from 1789 to 1796</term>, paper money, hatred of capital, and chimerical plans for financial renewal—such is the sad legacy of a false idea regarding money.

To overcome these perils and to dispel these phantoms, it is enough to consult the nature of things, to study the essence of money and the role it is called upon to fulfill.

Known in the ancient world, the truth seemed to vanish during centuries of decline, without ever being entirely extinguished; it left a luminous trail in France during an era that one might suppose was foreign to sound economic notions.

Aristotle, that powerful genius from whom no fertile thought seems to have escaped, says, when speaking of money:

> "It was agreed to give and receive, in exchanges, a material which, useful in itself, was easily manageable in the habitual uses of life; this was iron, for example, silver, or some other similar substance, of which the size and weight were first determined, and which finally—to free oneself from the burden of continual measurements—was marked with a specific stamp, a sign of its value."

Among the Romans, the great legal scholar Paul <note>Julius Paulus, one of the most influential Roman jurists, who wrote during the 2nd and 3rd centuries AD.</note> confirms and completes this beautiful definition: it is the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of money, completing a quotation from the Roman jurist Paul. It transitions from the theoretical benefits of standardized currency to the historical reality of how monarchs abused the power of minting through currency debasement.</meta>

metals "whose public and permanent certification made it possible to overcome the common difficulties of exchange (1)."

Aristotle and Paul speak of the stamp—a sign of value—which reveals and guarantees the intrinsic composition of coins.

Authority, as the guardian of public trust, was called upon to verify and declare the composition and weight of metallic currency. This protective measure soon became the occasion and the pretext for the most shameful frauds and the most serious debasements <term>the practice of lowering the value of a currency by reducing the amount of precious metal in coins</term>. The trust placed in the stamp gave rise to the temptation to abuse it; the prince, to whom the minting of money was entrusted—since he was presumed to watch over the public wealth like a father—allowed himself to be persuaded, or pretended to believe, that the value of money came from the effigy <note>the image or portrait of the ruler on the coin</note>, which was simply intended to certify its integrity. Money, called upon to serve as a regulator for agreements and

---

(1) Here is the admirable passage by Paul <note>Julius Paulus, a 3rd-century Roman jurist</note> (Digest, book XVIII, title I, 1):
> "*Sale* began with exchange; formerly there was no money, and nothing distinguished the *merchandise* from the *price*. Each person, according to the needs of the time and circumstances, swapped what was useless to them for what might be useful, for we often see that what one possesses in excess, another lacks. But as it did not always happen easily that one possessed what the other desired, and vice versa, a material was chosen whose public and permanent certification made it possible to overcome the common difficulties of exchange through a shared valuation: this material, marked with an official stamp, no longer bears the name of merchandise, but that of *price*."

<summary>This page defines money as a certified medium of exchange based on the theories of Aristotle and the jurist Paul, while highlighting how historical rulers abused the minting process to debase currency. It explains the shift from barter to a system where an official "stamp" guarantees a coin's value, a trust that was often betrayed by the state.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the corruption of currency. The previous page established how the state's role as the guarantor of a coin's weight and purity was eventually exploited by rulers who realized they could profit by reducing the precious metal content while maintaining the coin's face value.</meta>

page xxxviii

as a guarantee for exchanges, lost its essential character of purity and stability by suffering the impact of fraud.

This abuse was not unknown in Greece; however, she strove to remain faithful to the reputation of her monetary system (1). This was not the case in Rome, where the Republic set an example that the emperors would later exploit to fund their passions and extravagances. According to Vopiscus (2), there is no more certain symptom of the state's decline than the corruption of the currency, and the degrees of this decline are marked by the successive debasement <term>the reduction of precious metal in a coin's composition</term> of the money. One could almost judge the character of emperors by the more or less pure ring made by the coins struck during their reign (3) (4).

page xxxix

The infinite diversity of fragmented powers during the feudal era further increased this disorder: every lord struck his own coins, and every monarch committed debasements <term>the reduction of precious metal content in a coin while maintaining its face value</term> of varying severity to disguise, by means of these sophistications, actual bankruptcies. It was ordered that payments be made every six months, or every month, in the current coin (1), and, depending on whether the prince was a debtor or a creditor, he would weaken or raise the standard of the currency (2). These sad misdeeds did not even reach the...

---

...of the prince, whatever its standard was. *Digest* <note>The Digest, or Pandects, a massive compendium of Roman law compiled under Justinian I</note>, V, 25-1. — By a noble contrast, Theodoric the Goth <note>The first King of Italy (r. 493–526) who sought to preserve Roman culture</note> made these beautiful words heard: "The integrity of the coinage must be entirely protected where our face is impressed; what will be safe if our image is sinned against?" <note>original: "Omnino monetæ integritas debet tueri ubi vultus noster imprimitur; quodnam erit tutum si in nostra peccetur effigie."</note>

We see by the text of a capitulary <term>a series of legislative or administrative acts from the Frankish period</term> of 744, under Childeric III, that the government of the Frankish kings wanted to restrain, through severe penalties, the widespread industry of counterfeiting. "Concerning false money, we command that whoever is proven to have struck it shall have his hand amputated. And whoever consented to this, if he is a free man, shall pay sixty solidi <term>a gold coin originally introduced by Constantine</term>; if he is a servant, let him receive sixty lashes." <note>original: "De falsa moneta jubemus ut qui eam percussisse comprobatus fuerit manus ei amputatur. Et qui hoc consensit si liber est, sexaginta solidas comparat; si servus sexaginta ictus accipiat."</note> (Baluze, vol. I, p. 184-188.)

The chivalrous monarch to whom history—it is not known why—preserves the name of *John the Good*, because he embodied within himself the wanderings of an era fatal to France, tormented the value of the currency more than any of his predecessors. From 1351 to 1360, the <term>livre tournois|The "Tours pound," a standard unit of account in medieval France</term> changed value seventy-one times; the years 1359 and 1360 alone account for sixteen and seventeen <term>mutations|Official changes to the legal value or metal content of coins</term> respectively (2). The harm was all the more serious because, instead of a progressive alteration, changes occurred in opposite directions, and an increase in value succeeded an equal number of different decreases eleven times. It was "law in a state of madness" (3). But

When one attentively studies this page of our history, one recognizes that never were revolutions more frequent, nor more fertile in crises and reactions of all kinds, to the point that it would perhaps be difficult to decide whether the <term>assignat|Paper currency issued during the French Revolution, which eventually became nearly worthless due to hyperinflation</term> system was more disastrous for France than the deplorable practices of King John regarding money (2). The harm then was so great that it is impossible to measure its extent or calculate all its consequences.

From this arose the power and wealth of the *money-changers*, who withdrew strong currency <note>original: "monnaie forte," referring to coins with high precious metal content</note> to constantly substitute it in circulation with weak currency <note>original: "monnaie faible," coins debased with cheaper metals</note>, part of which was minted abroad. The prince thus found himself deceived in his greedy calculations; he ended up suffering like the people, whose misery grew ever worse. What could become of industry and commerce when the compass of exchange was driven mad?

All the sufferings, all the disasters, all the calamities seemed to have united to weigh upon France at the time when Charles V was called to take the crown. The dismemberment of the territory and the exhaustion of the treasury, the sedition of the cities and the <term>Jacquerie|A bloody peasant uprising in 1358, named after the stereotypical name for a peasant, "Jacques Bonhomme"</term> of the countryside, the banditry of the *tard-venus* <note>original: "late-comers," mercenary bands who pillaged the countryside after the Treaty of Brétigny</note> and the <term>Great Companies|Large bands of mercenary soldiers who, when unemployed during truces in the Hundred Years' War, lived off the land through plunder and ransom</term>, the Black Death and famine, the destruction of labor and the payment of the royal ransom <note>The enormous sum of 3 million gold crowns required by the English to release King John II</note>—this is what this poor country had to endure; it seemed to have hit rock bottom. Suddenly, in the midst of the shining armor that crushed with its weight men astonished at no longer having the strength to carry it—when the prowess of chivalry had resulted only in a shameful rout—there appears a pale and sickly figure, a hand incapable of carrying a sword,

a body, weakened by suffering, which refused the hardships of war. But there was within it a steadfast soul and an enlightened mind; and as if God had wished to show, in the midst of those times which valued only strength, the sovereign power of thought, Charles V, *the Wise* <note>original: "le Sage"</note>, quickly established a well-ordered State in place of true chaos. He knew how to revive prosperity alongside confidence, and to resume productive occupations by protecting the integrity of transactions and the security of individuals. The bands <note>Referring to the "Great Companies" of mercenaries mentioned on the previous page.</note> that ravaged the countryside became instruments of victory; everything took on a new appearance. A strict economy <note>In this context, "économie" refers to the careful management of state finances and resources.</note> was able to meet every need and fill the treasury; increased military forces restored the greatness of France, while agriculture, commerce, and industry—revived by the hope of a better future—reopened the true source of abundance and wealth. Nothing was left to chance; everything was subjected to calculation. An active and persevering thought, enlightened by setbacks no less than emboldened by success, exercised an influence that the ignorance of the era might have deemed supernatural, witnessing the <term>legal scholars|légistes; experts in Roman law who helped centralize royal power</term>, the scientists, the artists, the philosophers, and the astrologers who surrounded the king and inspired his designs.

It was not the least glory nor the least service of Charles V to put an end to the debasement of the currency <note>original: "altérations des monnaies," the practice of reducing the precious metal content in coins while maintaining their face value</note>. As Regent of the kingdom, he had not

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Charles V’s efforts to stabilize France. Having established political order, the text now focuses on his most significant economic achievement: the reform and stabilization of the French currency, reversing the damaging devaluations of his predecessors.</meta>

page xlvj

in this regard, escaped the contagion of bad example. The great ordinance of 1255, obtained by the estates assembled in Paris (1), promised henceforth a good and stable currency for the entire kingdom, such that the <term>mark of silver|marc d’argent; a medieval unit of weight for precious metals, approximately 244 grams</term> would never produce more than six <term>livres tournois|the "Tours pound," a standard unit of account in medieval France</term> (2). Duke Charles <note>Referring to the future Charles V during his period as Regent while his father, King John II, was a prisoner in England.</note> raised it as high as twelve livres; this was the signal for the great uprising in Paris (3). This memory, and perhaps even more the fortunate influence of one of his advisors, Nicole Oresme (4), led King Charles to take great care in the wise administration of the coinage (5).

During his reign, the standard <note>original: "le pied"; the fixed base value or ratio used for minting</note> for gold remained invariable, and that of silver experienced only slight modifications. The stability of the currency restored the regularity of transactions; it provided an active fuel for public prosperity.

***

(1) The debates of this assembly contain remarkable insights into political economy. Nicole Oresme elevated the ideas that had emerged there, in a somewhat instinctive manner, to the level of a doctrine.

(2) Henri Martin, V, 141.

The introduction to our work contains the information we were able to gather regarding Oresme's life.

Nicole Oresme, Bishop of Lisieux, former <term>grand master|grand maître; the administrative and academic head</term> of the College of Navarre and dean of the church of Rouen, is generally described as the <term>tutor|précepteur; a private teacher or mentor</term> of Charles V (1). We are inclined to believe, along with Mr. Francis Meunier (2)—the author of an interesting essay dedicated to highlighting the life and works of this learned man—that this was a misunderstanding (3), arising from an ambiguity. He was likely only the advisor to the wise monarch, who benefited from the assistance of his insights and drew precious lessons from him, especially regarding the matter of currency.

Nicole Oresme is known as one of the most renowned scholars of the reign of Charles V; we owe to him the first French translation of the *Ethics*, *Politics*, and *Economics* of Aristotle (4) and many of the—

This time, Oresme's work was in the hands of a competent judge, who immediately recognized its importance and scope (1); possessing the manuscript of the French work, we have tried to complete a study which seems to present both a historical interest and a scientific interest:

An historical interest, because Oresme’s treatise coincides by its date with the wise measures taken by **Charles V** <note>Charles V (1338–1380), known as "the Wise," was a King of France who successfully restored the value of French currency after years of instability</note>, in order to stop the currency <term>mutations|The practice of debasing or changing the metal content and face value of coins, often used by medieval monarchs to generate quick revenue, which led to inflation</term>;

A scientific interest, because it allows us to claim for France the honor of having preceded Italy, as well as England, in the sound exposition

(1) **Mr. Roscher** transmitted to us, in German, under the title: *A Great French Economist in the Fourteenth Century* <note>original: "Un grand économiste français au quatorzième siècle"</note>, the communication intended for the Academy of Moral and Political Sciences that we have reproduced (See above, p. xi). Mr. Roscher wrote this communication under the impression of the charm he felt upon reading a work he believed to be completely unknown: he applied the finesse of analysis, the sagacious erudition, and the height of insight that distinguish him. Our *Memoir*, for which we initially retained the title of **Mr. Roscher’s** work—as it was he who suggested the idea to us—treats the same subject, but views it from a different aspect. Its purpose is to utilize the manuscript of the French version, written by **Oresme** himself, a manuscript that is more complete than the Latin edition upon which **Mr. Roscher** based his work.

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Nicole Oresme’s 14th-century treatise on money. It transitions from discussing the historical rediscovery of the text by Wilhelm Roscher to explaining Oresme’s intellectual lineage—specifically his connection to Aristotle—and the lasting impact of his economic theories on currency stability.</meta>

of true principles, regarding one of the most serious questions of political economy.

A student of Aristotle, Nicole Oresme drew from the great Greek philosopher the doctrine of which he became the energetic interpreter (1). Informed by the sad experience of the misfortunes caused by the debasement of the currency <term>numéraire|The total stock of coins or legal tender in circulation</term>, he explained its origin and true nature; he defined the character and role of money with a clarity and force that have not been surpassed since.

When one looks back to the era from which Oresme’s work dates, one does not know whether to be more surprised by the vigor of this demonstration, or by the oblivion into which such clearly deduced principles fell following the death of **Charles V**. It is not until the sixteenth century that we see these principles reclaimed in Poland, Italy, and France, and in the seventeenth century in England.

The French manuscript contains twenty-six chapters (three more than the Latin edition); we shall attempt to preserve its language, which is both firm and natural <note>French: *naïf*. In this context, it refers to the direct and unadorned style of Middle French, rather than "naive" in the modern sense</note>, reminiscent of the style of **Froissart** <note>Jean Froissart (c. 1337–c. 1405) was one of the most important chroniclers of medieval France; his prose is celebrated for its vividness</note>.

> "It seems to many," (*says the translator's prologue*), "that any king or prince may, by his own authority, right, or privilege, freely change the cur—"

(1-10.) — "As it appears in the treatise on the *Changes of Money*." (1-12.)

<meta>This page concludes the list of chapters from Nicole Oresme’s treatise (begun on the previous page) and continues the editor's analysis of Oresme’s work, emphasizing his systematic approach to economics and his bold advice to King Charles V.</meta>

The plan is broadly mapped out: to remedy the abuses under which the nation groaned without fully understanding their enormity, and to prevent their return, Nicole Oresme courageously probes the wound; he studies the seat of the illness, and, after making known the nature of money and the laws of circulation, he points out the remedy. His language, at once modest and resolute, is worthy of the wise monarch <note>King Charles V of France (reigned 1364–1380), known as "The Wise," who was a patron of Oresme</note> whom he addresses in these terms in the *Translator’s Conclusion*: “The things premised above are spoken without assertion or affirmation and subject to the correction of wise and prudent men, and especially of You, my *very dear and honored Lord*, who in the majority of these matters are knowledgeable and expert.”

Nothing is simpler or more true than the opening of the subject; Oresme shows how every man and every region “overflowed in one thing of which in another he had a great lack (1). Men... began to communicate and exchange their riches in—

->XXIV. The principal conclusion. — XXV. That the Tyrannical prince cannot long endure. — XXVI. How taking profit, by reason of the alterations <note>original: "mutations." This refers to the practice of "debasement," where a ruler reduces the amount of precious metal in coins while maintaining their face value to pay off debts.</note> of money, prejudices the entire royal power.<-

Thus, need having engaged man to join another man, society was established for the purpose of mutual assistance. Yes; but one does not communicate to another what one has, to receive what one does not have, except because one believes it to be to one’s advantage.”</note>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Nicole Oresme’s economic theories, specifically focusing on his definition of money as a tool for exchange rather than intrinsic wealth, following the previous page's introduction to his address to King Charles V.</meta>

page liv

—what seemed, without money... But since in this manner of permutation <note>The act of bartering or exchanging one thing for another.</note> and exchange of things many difficulties and controversies arose among them, clever men found a more convenient practice, which is to say, the creation of money, which would be an instrument to exchange and trade their natural riches with one another.”

He does not at all confuse money with wealth, and recalls the fable of King Midas <note>In Greek mythology, King Midas was granted the power to turn everything he touched into gold, only to realize he could no longer eat or drink.</note>, to show that “someone abounding in it could still die of hunger... for with money one does not immediately relieve the poverty of human life, but it is an artificial instrument found for exchanging natural riches more easily.” After having indicated the purpose of money, he studies its conditions: “It was expedient that such an instrument be fit and convenient to handle and manage easily with the hands, light to carry, and that for a small portion of it, natural riches could [be] purchased and exchanged in a greater quantity.....”

> “It was therefore appropriate that money be made of precious material and small in quantity, such as gold... and silver... Furthermore, it is neither expedient nor political that such material—that is to say, gold and silver—be in too great abundance, for for that same cause copper money was abandoned and rejected.”

Turgot <note>Anne Robert Jacques Turgot (1727–1781) was a prominent French economist and statesman; the author is noting that Oresme’s 14th-century ideas were so advanced they match those of the Enlightenment.</note> would not have said it better.

It is a law of Providence: “That gold and silver

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nicole Oresme's economic treatise, transitioning from the ideal materials for money to the necessity of government-regulated minting and the prevention of counterfeiting through alchemy or debasement.</meta>

page lv

...which are very suitable for making money, cannot be easily obtained in great quantity or abundance, nor can men easily make them through alchemy <note>The medieval precursor to chemistry, which often attempted to create gold or silver from base metals.</note>, as some tempt and try to do. To whom I will say: thus nature rightly rebels and opposes anyone who, for nothing, strives to exceed and overcome her in her natural works."

Nicole Oresme explains with the same clarity of vision the utility of gold money, silver money, and the third, black *mixed* <note>Likely referring to "billon," a low-grade alloy of silver and copper used for small-value transactions.</note> money. No mixing <note>The practice of alloying or debasing precious metals.</note> should occur except for the less precious metal "from which it is customary to make small change... and for this reason, no mixing should occur in gold money."

The difficulties were great when it was necessary to constantly weigh and test the various pieces of metal used as money. To address this, it was agreed that "portions and pieces of money would be made of a certain material and a determined weight, upon which an image well-known and appropriate to everyone would be imprinted, which would signify the quality of the material and the truth of the weight of the coin <term>denier: the standard silver coin of the Middle Ages, derived from the Roman denarius</term>, so that, suspicion being set aside, the value of the money could be quickly known without labor or doubt; and it is true that such an impression was established on coins as a sign of the truth of the material and weight..."

Coins "must be of a shape and quantity easy to handle and count, and of a material suitable for minting,"

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Nicole Oresme's "Treatise on the Origin, Nature, Law, and Alterations of Money." It follows the discussion of why certain metals are chosen for coinage, moving into the prince's role as a guarantor of value and the revolutionary idea that money belongs to the public, not the crown.</meta>

page lvj

...and also soft enough to receive an impression and retain it. Because of this, not all precious things are suitable for making <term>deniers: the standard silver coin of the Middle Ages, often used here to refer to coins in general</term> or money; for precious stones, pepper <note>Pepper was highly valuable and sometimes used as a medium of exchange, but Oresme argues it lacks the physical properties—like malleability—required for minting.</note>, glass, and similar things are not naturally suitable for this, but only gold, silver, and copper, as mentioned above."

The prince has been called upon as a public person and the highest authority to mint money "and to mark it with an honest impression. This impression... must be subtle, and it must be very difficult to counterfeit or reproduce."

But money does not belong to the prince; it belongs to those who earn it: "for if someone gives their bread or the labor of their own body for money <term>pécune: money; a term derived from the Latin 'pecunia'</term>, when they receive it in this way, it is certainly purely theirs, just as their bread or the labor of their body was, which were in their free and frank power to make or give..."

> "The exchange rate and the value of coins must be in the kingdom like a law and a firm ordinance that must in no way be shifted or changed."

Such is the principle firmly established by Nicole Oresme: he examines its applications, pursues its consequences, and points out the inevitable danger one faces in violating it. His calm and lucid words become animated and elevated when he fights against the arbitrary claims of prin- <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with "princes" on the following page.</note>

-ces to dispose at their will of the fineness <term>titre: the purity or proportion of precious metal in a coin</term>, the weight, and the value of the money.

> "Nor does it appear in any way that the prince should be moved to make such an alteration... because he wants to mint more money so that, by this, he might have more gain... this manner is very evil and very ugly greed, which would be done to the prejudice and damage of the whole community."

The image of the prince must serve only as a guarantee:

> "one places on the coin <term>denier: the standard silver coin of the Middle Ages, often used here to refer to coins in general</term> the image and the inscription of the prince to signify and make known the certainty of the weight, quality, and goodness of the material.... Thus, if the truth did not correspond to the weight, quality, and goodness, it would immediately appear that it was a very vile falsehood and fraudulent deception.... Who then would have trust in the prince who had diminished the weight or goodness of the material, thus marked with his own sign?"

Oresme attributes to the term money <note>original: "monnoie"</note> an etymology that is, at the very least, ingenious:

> "Money is said to come from 'to admonish' <note>original: "ammonester"</note>, for it admonishes that fraud or deception should not be committed, neither in the metal nor in the weight of it (1)."

(1) The impression and figure of the money is the sign of the truth of the material and of this alloy <note>original: "mixtion"</note>, if it is an alloy, and thus to change that thing is to falsify the money; for these reasons, on some coins and even more, one writes the name of God or of some saint and the sign of the cross, which manner was found and anciently established as a witness to the truth of the money in terms of weight. If therefore a prince, under this inscription, changes the coins in weight or in composition, he is seen

<vocab>Oresme, money, alteration, weight, community, etymology, admonish, denier</vocab>

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that the prince's image on a coin is a sacred pledge of its weight and purity, not a license for the crown to profit from devaluing the currency. He suggests the word "money" itself is a warning against fraud, reinforced by the religious symbols often minted onto coins.</summary>
<keywords>Nicole Oresme, monetary ethics, coinage, debasement, etymology of money, prince's guarantee, public trust, medieval economics</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Nicole Oresme’s economic theories, specifically his comparison between the sin of usury and the prince’s practice of altering the currency. It follows the previous page’s discussion on the prince’s seal as a guarantee of honesty.</meta>

page lviij

The condemnation of lending at interest—which was then conflated with <term>usury: the practice of charging any interest on a loan, which was considered a sin in medieval law</term>—was natural for a disciple of Aristotle. Oresme, without freeing himself from this error common to the time in which he lived, nonetheless managed to singularly temper its impact. To show that "taking profit from the <term>mutation of money: the act of a ruler reducing the weight or purity of coins while keeping their face value the same</term> is worse than usury," he says:

> "The usurer gives his money <note>original: "peculne"</note> to the one who receives it voluntarily and of his own free will, and who afterwards can use it to help and succor his own needs; and that which he gives back to the lender, over and above what he received, *is part of a certain contract between them to which they have both consented*."

These are most remarkable words coming from the mouth of a theologian in the fourteenth century!

He further adds:

> "In this matter, then, where the prince receives profit from the alteration of money over and above the natural rate and usage, this acquisition is similar to usury; but it is even worse than usury, considering that it is less voluntary and is done against the will of the subjects, without it bringing them any profit or being of any necessity. For the profit of a usurer is not so excessive, nor so prejudicial or general to everyone, as is this alteration, which is imposed upon the whole community; I say that it is not only similar to usury, but is tyrannical and frau-

<note>The text here breaks off, likely continuing as "fraudulent" on the next page.</note>

> -festly being a liar, committing perjury and bearing false witness, and furthermore he is a transgressor and a despiser of that legal commandment of God, in which it is said: "Thou shalt not take the name of thy God in vain." (Chap. XIII.)

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that a ruler debasing currency is worse than a usurer because, while usury involves a voluntary contract between two people, currency alteration is a forced theft from the entire community. He further asserts that a prince who debases money violates the religious commandment against perjury, as the prince's seal on the coin is a false promise of its value.</summary>

<keywords>Nicole Oresme, usury, interest, currency debasement, Aristotle, economic ethics, tyranny, Ten Commandments, medieval theology</keywords>

<meta>This page completes the sentence from page lviii, continuing the analysis of Nicole Oresme’s 14th-century economic treatise. It focuses on the prince's responsibility toward the currency and how debasement disrupts trade, anticipating modern economic laws.</meta>

page lix

dulent, so much so that I doubt whether it should rather be called violent prey, or fraudulent extortion."

Oresme attacked the evil at its root by dispelling the common error regarding the power of the prince; he restored to money its character as a commodity, certified and guaranteed by authority: he anticipated *Turgot*, *Adam Smith*, and *Jean-Baptiste Say* <note>These are foundational figures in 18th and 19th-century economics; the author is highlighting Oresme as a "modern" thinker far ahead of his time</note>.

The <term>mutation of money: the sovereign’s act of changing the value, weight, or metal content of coins</term> is all the more perilous, "for it is not so quickly felt nor perceived by the people, as it would be by another <term>collection: here referring to a tax or levy</term>, and yet no such or similar thing could be more grievous or greater."

> ->"It is fitting and proper for a prince to condemn and punish counterfeiters and those who commit any falsification or theft regarding money. How then should he not feel great shame if there is found in him the very thing he ought to punish in another with a most ugly and infamous death."<-

Nicole Oresme had already formulated the principle that later made the name of Gresham famous in England <note>Known today as Gresham’s Law: "Bad money drives out good."</note>: good money disappears from any country where <term>debasements: the practice of lowering the value of currency, often by mixing precious metals with cheaper ones</term> are made. He painted in a single word the confusion born from such instability of the <term>specie: physical money or coinage</term>: "Furthermore, even in the land where such alterations take place, the business of trade is so troubled that merchants and craftsmen <note>original: "mechaniques," referring to those who work in manual trades or industry</note> do not know how to communicate with one another... and thus by such alterations the world is troubled."

We have said enough to make known the incontes-

<summary>Oresme argues that currency debasement is a form of state-sanctioned theft that is more dangerous than taxes because it is hidden. He identifies that debased currency ruins trade and drives good money out of circulation, centuries before modern economists formalized these theories.</summary>
<keywords>Nicole Oresme, currency debasement, Gresham's Law, monetary policy, counterfeiting, economic history, medieval trade</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nicole Oresme’s economic theories from page lix, moving from the technical aspects of currency debasement to the political and moral rights of the sovereign versus the community.</meta>

page lx

...establishes the priority belonging to Nicole Oresme regarding the sound doctrine of money: one would need to quote his *Treatise* <note>Original: *Traictie*, referring to Oresme's influential 14th-century work *De Moneta* (On Money)</note> in its entirety to reproduce all the serious lessons brought to light there. It remains for us to show his elevated views on matters of government, and his upright understanding of the ideas of liberty and independence.

It is not only the prince who lacks the right to alter the currency: in principle, the community possesses it no more, even if it were "for war or the redemption of its captured prince." <note>This likely refers to the massive ransom required for King John the Good of France, captured by the English in 1356, which led to severe currency devaluations.</note> Unless there is an exceptional necessity, the community and the prince must resort to subsidies or borrowing.

It is argued that "the community to which the money belongs and is, can strip itself of its right, and give it entirely to the prince." Oresme rejects this thought: "A community of citizens, which is naturally free and tends toward liberty, would never knowingly submit to servitude or lower itself to the yoke of tyrannical power... Just as, therefore, the community cannot grant the prince the power and authority to abuse the wives of its citizens at his will, and whichever ones he pleases, similarly it cannot give him the privilege of doing as he pleases with the coinage." The resources of the State "must be assigned elsewhere and taken in another manner than by such undue <term>mutations: the practice of changing the legal value or metal content of coins</term>."

The prince has no title to have paid to him a pre-

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that neither the king nor the community has the right to debase currency, comparing the king's control over money to an act of tyranny equivalent to violating the personal rights of citizens.</summary>
<keywords>Nicole Oresme, De Moneta, monetary policy, tyranny, citizenship, social contract, currency debasement, medieval economics</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page lx, completing the sentence regarding the sovereign's lack of right to claim ownership over the currency and transitioning into Nicole Oresme's broader critique of tyrannical governance.</meta>

...supposed abandonment of the right to debase the currency. "To deny him this thing is not to disinherit him or to go against the royal majesty, as some liars, flatterers, and forgers—traitors to the public good <note>Original: *chose publicque*, referring to the commonwealth or the collective interest of the people</note>—tell him and make him believe."

An enemy of tyranny, along with all economists worthy of the name, Oresme devotes a chapter to demonstrating that "the tyrannical prince cannot long endure."

Tyranny appears to him as a "monster in nature... like a body whose head is so large that the rest of it is so weak that it cannot support it." <note>Oresme uses this vivid anatomical metaphor to illustrate how a ruler who takes too much from his subjects weakens the very society that must support him.</note>

> "God forbid," he cries, "that the free hearts of the French should be so debased <note>Original: *abastardyz*, meaning to lose their noble or natural character</note> that they would voluntarily become serfs; for the servitude imposed upon them could not long endure, for however great the power of tyrants may be, it is nevertheless violent in the hearts of the free children of future subjects, and ineffective against foreigners. Whoever, therefore, would seek in any way to attract and induce the lords of France to this tyrannical regime would certainly expose the kingdom to great discredit and shame, and prepare it for its end. For the most noble succession <note>Original: *sequelle*, referring to the lineage or dynasty</note> of the kings of France never learned to tyrannize, nor did the Gallican <note>Refers to the French people, derived from the Latin term for the inhabitants of Gaul</note> people ever accustom themselves to servile subjection. And for this reason, if the royal succession of France deviates from its first..."

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that currency manipulation is a form of tyranny that contradicts the "free heart" of the French people. He uses anatomical metaphors to explain how a tyrant weakens the state and warns that the French monarchy must not deviate from its noble origins by enslaving its subjects.</summary>
<keywords>Nicole Oresme, tyranny, currency debasement, French monarchy, liberty, Gallican people, public good, political philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion regarding Oresme’s warnings to the French monarchy and transitions into a historical assessment of his intellectual legacy and the rediscovery of his economic principles during the Renaissance.</meta>

> "...virtue, without any doubt she will lose her kingdom and it will be transferred into another hand."

In Oresme’s work, the sentiments of the citizen rise to the same height as the enlightenment of the scholar.

The monarch to whom he addressed these counsels was capable of understanding and following them; thus, history has preserved for him the name **Charles the Wise** <note>Charles V of France (reigned 1364–1380), known for his patronage of the arts and his efforts to stabilize the French economy during the Hundred Years' War</note>. But with him, the power and prosperity of the country were destined to disappear, abandoned once again to the heartbreak of civil war and the shame of foreign invasion.

The true principles regarding <term>currency</term> <note>The management and valuation of metallic money</note>, taught by Nicole Oresme and practiced by Charles the Wise, were buried in the common ruin of the fatherland. They were lost to such an extent that they were regarded as "daring novelties" when Jean Bodin became their mouthpiece in the sixteenth century (1). These principles, which inspired the best works on money in Italy, were not professed there until the same period; the *discourse* of Count Scaruffi bears the date of 1582 (2). We know that Rice Vaug—

(2) A similar doctrine was developed by the great Copernicus, in the *Treatise on the Minting of Money* <note>Original Latin title: *Monete Cudende Ratio*</note>, which dates from 1526. We provide this important work in the second part of this volume.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of 16th and 17th-century economic thinkers across Europe, specifically picking up mid-name from the previous page's mention of the English writer Rice Vaughan.</meta>

[Vaug]han(1), Cotton, Petty, North, and Locke, who popularized the same doctrine in England, belong to the seventeenth century.

To ancient France, therefore, belongs the indisputable honor of having first formulated the doctrine of money in the writings of Nicole Oresme, whom we may, along with our learned friend Mr. Roscher <note>Wilhelm Roscher (1817–1894), a founding father of the German Historical School of Economics</note>, salute by the title of a great economist; yet modern France alone has reaped the profit from it. The debasement of <term>currency</term> <note>The practice of reducing the precious metal content of coins while maintaining their face value, often used by monarchs to pay off debts</note>, after having resumed its course under Charles VI, did not cease until the Great Revolution. In 1789, the <term>livre</term> <note>The standard currency unit of France until 1795</note> represented only 1/86th of the fine silver it contained during the time of Charlemagne, and only 1/10th of the quantity prescribed by Charles V (2).

---

(1) The Political Economy Club of London has published, under the title of *Tracts on Money*, an interesting collection of the oldest writings dedicated, in England, to this serious matter. At the head of this collection is the work of Rice Vaughan: "*A discourse of Coin and Coinage: the first invention, use, matter, forms, proportions and differences, ancient and modern.*" In the preface (p. 6), we see that Mr. McCulloch <note>John Ramsay McCulloch (1789–1864), a prominent Scottish economist and statistician</note> places this writing between 1630 and 1635. *He states:*

> "It is the earliest Work in the English language that give a general view of the origin of money, the materials of which it has been formed, its uses, and the abuses to which it has been subjected." <note>original English: "It is the earliest Work in the English language that give a general view of the origin of money..."</note>

In his 1626 discourse, Cotton combats the "heresy that the value of Coins was to a considerable extent dependent on the stamp by which they were impressed." <note>original English: "heresy that the value of Coins was to a considerable extent dependent on the stamp by which they were impressed."</note>

(2) This fact is general across Europe; in this regard, we shall cite an instructive passage from Storch <note>Heinrich Friedrich von Storch (1766–1835), a German-Russian economist who wrote in French</note>, Vol. II, Book V, Chap. III, p. 131.

> "Most currencies today are no longer what they once were..."

Today the franc is invariably equivalent to four and a half grams of fine silver, at a <term>fineness</term> <note>The proportion of pure precious metal (silver or gold) contained in a coin compared to the alloy used to harden it</note> of nine-tenths; it therefore weighs five grams.

But regarding doctrine, let us not be too proud nor too disdainful of the ignorance of our ancestors. Let us humbly admit that much remains to be done so that the truth might penetrate the minds of many prejudiced people, who persist in assuming that money is not at all a natural production, but that it is a sovereign creation of governments and societies. History is there to show where such a mistake leads, and for a long time the *Treatise* <note>original: "Traictie"</note> of Nicole Oresme <note>A 14th-century French bishop and scholar who wrote one of the first scientific treatises on money, arguing against its debasement by kings</note> should have cured the country of it.

---

> ...formerly, although they still bear the same names. Almost all governments, whether monarchical or republican, whether from antiquity or modern Europe, have employed a fraudulent means to excuse themselves from paying their debts. They have kept the same name for the coins <note>original: "espèces"</note> while altering their real value, their weight, or their fineness. Throughout Europe, the current currency was originally a <term>pound</term> <note>A "livre" or pound originally referred to a literal pound weight of silver</note> of silver weight; by depreciating the coins, they continued to call them pounds.

# VARIANTS AND CORRECTIONS.

The French translation of the *Treatise* <note>original: "Traictie"</note> by Nicole Oresme, which we reproduce based on a manuscript from the Imperial Library <note>Now the Bibliothèque nationale de France.</note> (1), was printed in Bruges by *Colard Mansion*.

Here is what Mr. Van Praet says on this subject in his *Notice on Colard Mansion, bookseller and printer of the city of Bruges in the fifteenth century* (2):

> “*Treatise on Money* <note>original: "Traité des Monnoies"</note>, translated from the Latin of Nicolas (3) Oresme into French, small folio.
> “Edition in old large *bâtarde* script <term>bâtarde script: A style of Gothic handwriting used in 15th-century manuscripts and early printing for vernacular texts.</term>, without page numbers, catchwords, signatures, or initials; in long lines numbering twenty-three to a full page, containing forty-four leaves.
> “An edition of the greatest rarity, which was previously unknown, and of which the only copy—sold for 670 francs at the sale of Mr. Haillet de Couronne’s books in 1811—is in the possession of the author of this notice. The typefaces alone indicate that it comes from the presses of Mansion, who employed his largest characters there; height 271 mm (10 p.).
> “This book contains the *anonymous* (4) French translation of the work titled *On the Alteration and Variation of Coins Made by Kings* <note>original Latin: "De mutatione monetarum ac variatione facta per reges"</note>, which Nicolas Oresme composed for Charles V, and which appeared

---

(1) F. Notre-Dame, no. 172.
(2) Paris, 1829, pp. 63, 64.
(3) We have explained why we have retained the name *Nicole* for Oresme, which he used himself in his translation of *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: "du Ciel et du Monde," Oresme's translation of Aristotle's "De Caelo."</note> (Imperial Library, ms. no. 7065).
(4) This translation was made by Oresme himself. To be convinced of this, one need only recall the *translator's conclusion* (see below, p. LXXXVI) addressed by Oresme to King Charles V.

<summary>This appendix provides bibliographic information regarding the rare 15th-century printed edition of Oresme's treatise by Colard Mansion and justifies the use of the name "Nicole" over "Nicolas."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the bibliographical discussion of early editions of Nicole Oresme’s work, moving from the description of the rare 15th-century French edition by Colard Mansion to a newly discovered 16th-century Latin edition by Thomas Keet.</meta>

printed for the first time in Paris, quarto, without date, by Thomas Keet, at the beginning of the sixteenth century.

> “The editors of Fontette's *Bibliothèque de France* (vol. III, p. 296, no. 32-924) only knew of the existence of the translation through the *Golden Codicil* <note>original: "Codicille d'Or"</note>, which cites it (pp. 8 and 22), and did not know that it had been printed.”

This copy, which appears to be unique, was bequeathed by Mr. Van Praet to the Imperial Library <note>Now the Bibliothèque nationale de France.</note>, along with the magnificent collection of *Colard Mansion* editions; it is marked E, 514, with this indication: “It was printed in Bruges during the fifteenth century.”

A kind communication <note>Likely referring to scholarly assistance or access granted by another librarian.</note> has allowed us to collate the text printed by Colard Mansion with the manuscript we are publishing here. In order to make this edition as complete and as accurate as possible, we have noted *the variants and corrections which frequently make the sense easier to grasp and give the expressions a more graceful turn*. It is possible that modifications were made to the original text, and that certain rectifications were introduced, as more than a century separated Oresme’s death from the publication made by Colard Mansion.

We must add that, in various passages, the manuscript from the Imperial Library proves to be more complete than the printed copy. The latter notably omits this fine thought (1): “Who then would be he who, in a prince who had diminished the weight or goodness of the material thus shaped with his own seal, *would have trust?*”

As for the Latin copy, printed by Thomas Keet, of which Mr. Van Praet speaks, we recently found it at the Imperial Library. It is a small quarto <term>quarto: A book size resulting from folding a sheet of paper twice to create four leaves (eight pages).</term> of 16 pages of very compact printing. A handwritten indication on the title bears these words: *Paris, Thomas Keet, circa 1511*; it is entitled: “A very brief treatise, yet filled with the most excellent maxims...” <note>original Latin: "Tractatus brevissimus optimis tamen sententiis refertissi-"</note>

<meta>This page continues the bibliographical analysis of Nicole Oresme’s "Treatise on Money." > “...[treatise], on the alteration of currencies and the variations made by kings or princes, published by the reverend father in Christ, Nicholas Oresme of Lisieux <note>original: "Lexoviensi"</note>, former bishop, both a theologian and a most sharp philosopher.” <note>original Latin: "...mus, de mutatione monetarum ac variatione facta per reges aut principes editus, a reverendo in Christo patre Nicolao Oresmio Lexoviensi, quondam antistite, theologo pariter ac philosopho acutissimo."</note>

This copy evidently served as the prototype for several later reprints; it may explain how various gaps continued to mar the original work. Thus, chapters XVI and XVII are missing, or at least their text is only reproduced in a very abbreviated form.

The edition we are publishing—which is the result of work performed on the various Latin manuscripts of Oresme’s work held by the Imperial Library <note>Now the Bibliothèque nationale de France.</note>—is therefore more faithful and more complete.

<meta>This page follows the errata section of the previous pages, transitioning from the textual corrections of the manuscript to the actual beginning of the work itself. ### *Here begins a small Treatise on the first*
### *Invention of money and the causes*
### *and manners thereof.*

For what purpose they were made. — How they should be used. — To whom it belongs to mint, debase, or change them, and what inconveniences may arise and spring from this. Gathered from several volumes and recently <note>original: "nagaires"</note> translated from Latin into French, in order to show the great fault and misuse that is practiced today regarding currency by merchants and commoners, and which the King and the Princes tolerate and permit, from which will follow many evils, inconveniences, and irreparable damages, if a quick provision and remedy is not applied, as will be specified in the following discourse.

And this saying I have brought to my purpose, because it (1)

<meta>This page concludes the introductory remarks and the Table of Contents. It serves as a transition from the list of twenty-six chapters to the main body of the treatise, with the author offering a humble justification for writing on such a critical economic and political matter.</meta>

Thus, through the introductions and chapters mentioned above, there appear, in part, the scandals <note>original: "esclandres"; in this context, it refers to public outrages or moral stumbling blocks caused by bad policy.</note>, damages, and disadvantages—though not yet all of those that may follow and are already beginning—in the kingdom and land where such abuses are tolerated and permitted regarding the currency and the noble metals from which it is and ought to be made. And although it does not belong to me to take up this cause <note>original: "querelle"; here meaning a formal complaint or a legal and moral argument taken up on behalf of others.</note>, considering that I am the least, the most ignorant, and the most unlearned of all, nevertheless let this warning be understood and accepted as coming from the good heart and the will I have for the universal good <note>original: "bien universel"; the medieval concept of the commonweal or the general welfare of the entire community.</note>; and I pray that those who read this do not attribute any rash reproach to me.

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the actual treatise. It transitions from the table of contents and preface to Chapter 1, which discusses the origins of trade through barter before the invention of money.</meta>

### *Here begins the Treatise on the first origin and nature of the laws of money; and first, for what reason money was first invented.*

> *When the Most High divided the nations, when he separated the sons of Adam, he set the boundaries of the peoples according to the number of the children [of Israel], etc.* <note>original: "Quando dividebat Altissimus gentes, quando separabat filios Adam, constituit terminos populorum juxta numerum filiorum, etc." This is a Latin quotation from the Bible (Deuteronomy 32:8) used to justify the division of private property as a divine or natural order.</note> When the most high and sovereign omnipotent God divided the peoples and separated the sons of Adam, he established boundaries for the nations according to the number of the sons of Israel; after which, men multiplied upon the earth and their possessions were divided and distributed among them, as was appropriate <note>original: "expédiant"; in this sense, it means what was useful or necessary for the functioning of society.</note>. From this it happened that one man had more of a thing in his possession than his needs required, and another had of that same thing little or nothing; but conversely, he abounded and had an excess of another thing, for which the first man had a great need. For instance, perhaps one man might have an abundance of sheep and other livestock, but had need of wheat and bread; and the laborer, conversely, had bread enough, 

<margin>The exchange of goods before money existed.</margin>
but he lacked livestock. Also, one region possessed an overabundance of one thing which was in great demand in another. Men, therefore, for this reason began to communicate and exchange <note>original: "eschanger"; the root of the modern word exchange, referring to the direct trade of goods.</note> their riches together, without using money, by one giving a sheep to another for wheat, and one gave his labor <note>original: "labouraige"; referring to manual work or services provided in trade.</note>

<vocab>Nicole Oresme, Treatise on Money, commutation, exchange, law of money</vocab>

<summary>Nicole Oresme explains that money was invented to solve the practical difficulties of barter, as the unequal distribution of resources among people and regions necessitated a system of exchange.</summary>
<keywords>barter, origins of money, private property, trade, divine order, resource distribution, Nicole Oresme</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the transition from a barter economy to a monetary one, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's explanation of the exchange of livestock for grain.</meta>

...for bread or for wool. And they did the same with all other things; a custom they followed for a long time in many cities and countries, as recorded by the historian Justin <note>original: "Justin, historiographe"; referring to Marcus Junianus Justinus, a Roman historian who summarized the works of Trogus Pompeius.</note> and other ancient authors. But, as this manner of barter <note>original: "permutacion"; the direct exchange of goods for goods without a medium of exchange.</note> and exchange of things gave rise to many difficulties and disputes among them, clever men found a more convenient practice: namely, the creation of money.

<margin>The first invention of making money as an instrument.</margin>This money was to be an instrument for valuing and trading natural riches <note>original: "naturelles richesses"; essential goods for survival like food and clothing.</note> with one another. Through money, one can more quickly provide for human needs. All forms of currency <note>original: "pecunes"; derived from the Latin *pecunia*, it refers here to money as a medium of exchange.</note> are called "artificial riches" and nothing else, considering that it could happen that someone possessing an abundance of it might still die of hunger beside it. 

This is exemplified in the history told by the philosopher Aristotle regarding a greedy King whom Ovid, in his book ***Metamorphoses***, calls Midas. He worshipped and prayed to the Gods that everything he touched might turn to gold. The Gods granted this foolish prayer, and thus he died of hunger next to his gold, as the poets imagined. For money does not immediately relieve the poverty of human life; rather, it is an artificial instrument invented to exchange natural riches more easily. Thus, without further proof, it clearly appears that money is very useful and necessary for the good of the public community—indeed, even essential.

<summary>This page explains the transition from barter to a monetary system, defining money as an "artificial" form of wealth that serves as a tool to facilitate the exchange of "natural" wealth (necessities).</summary>

### *The second chapter. Of what material money should be made.*

And because money is therefore the instrument for exchanging <term>natural riches<gloss>tangible goods necessary for life, such as food, clothing, or livestock</gloss></term> with one another, as was said in the previous chapter, it was appropriate that such an instrument be suitable and convenient to handle and manage easily with the hands, light to carry, and that for a small portion of it, natural riches could be bought and traded in greater quantity, along with several other conditions that will be seen and noted hereafter. It was necessary, then, that money be made of a precious material that is small in quantity, such as gold; however, there must be a sufficient abundance of such material in the country.
<margin>When metal must be mixed with gold and silver.</margin>
And when gold cannot suffice, money is also made of silver. Where these two metals cannot suffice or cannot be found in sufficient abundance, then a money should be made, either mixed or purely of another metal, <margin>Bronze money.</margin> which in ancient times was made of bronze or copper, as Ovid relates in the first book of the *Fasti*, where he says:

->They once gave bronze; now there is a better omen in gold, and the old money has yielded, conquered by the new. <note>original Latin: "Æra dabant olim, melius nunc omen in auro est, Victaque concessit prisca moneta novæ." From Ovid’s *Fasti*, Book I, lines 221-222.</note><-

Which means, in English <note>original: "en françois"; the author is translating the Latin verse into the vernacular for his readers</note>, that the ancients, in days of old, gave their money in copper, but now—

<summary>This chapter outlines the physical requirements for currency, explaining that it must be portable and made of precious materials like gold or silver. It notes that less valuable metals like bronze were used in antiquity when precious metals were scarce, citing the poet Ovid.</summary>

XII

—and more excellently, the moderns provide it in gold. And likewise, the ancient way of life has left to the new an example of sound coinage. Our Lord also promised a similar transformation through the prophet Isaiah, saying: *Instead of copper I will bring gold, and for iron I will give silver.* <note>Isaiah 60:17</note> These two metals are therefore very fitting and well-suited for money; and thus, as Cassiodorus says (1),

<margin>The first discovery of gold and silver.</margin>
the first two who are said to have discovered these two metals were Cutus, who found gold, and Indus, who found silver; both were Kings of Scythia <note>An ancient region in Central Eurasia</note>; and, with great praise, they gave them over to human use, for which they were considered divine by the peoples of that time.

<margin>Turning gold and silver into money.</margin>
And for this reason, one should not allow so much of these metals to be applied to other uses that the remainder is insufficient to make money.

<margin>Treasures from tombs brought out for the public good.</margin>
Seeing and considering this in former times, Theodoric, King of Italy, and being rightly informed of the gold and silver that, according to the custom of the ancient pagans, was placed in their tombs with the dead, commanded these to be removed and brought to be made into money, for the use and benefit of the <term>public good<gloss>The *res publica*, or the shared interests and welfare of the state and its citizens</gloss></term>, saying (2) that it is a kind of dishonest sin to leave things hidden uselessly in the tombs of the dead—

<margin>Not having gold and silver easily in abundance.</margin>
<margin>Alchemy.</margin>
Furthermore, it is not practical or sound policy that such materials—namely gold and silver—should be in too great an abundance. For perhaps it was for this very reason that copper coinage was discarded and rejected from human use, as <note>The Roman poet Ovid (43 BC – 17 AD), often cited in medieval texts for his descriptions of the "Golden Age" and subsequent decline of man.</note> Ovid says. This, too, perhaps, was why gold and silver were favored for human use: so that these metals, which are so well-suited for minting, could not be easily obtained in great quantities or abundance, and so that men could not easily produce them through <term>alchemy<gloss>The medieval practice of attempting to transform base metals into gold or silver through chemical and spiritual processes</gloss></term>, as some attempt and try to do. To those people, I will say: nature rightly rebels and sets herself against anyone who vainly strives to exceed and overcome her in her natural works.

— XIV —

### *The third chapter concerns the diversity of the materials*
### *of money and of alloying.*

Money, as was said in the first Chapter, is an instrument for trade (1), and because it belongs to and is a necessity for the community and for every individual to trade and conduct business—sometimes on a grand and large scale of great importance, and sometimes on a smaller scale, and most often for little things—for this reason it was appropriate and necessary to have gold money. Gold is precious and can be easily carried and moved; it is also more suitable for conducting and managing large-scale trade. It is also necessary to have silver money, which is less precious and is fit and convenient for making payments and balances through exchanges, and also for buying small goods of low price. And because sometimes in a region there is not a sufficiently competent supply of silver in proportion to the <term>natural wealth<gloss>The actual goods, land, and resources of a region, as opposed to its artificial monetary value</gloss></term>, the small portion of silver that should justly be given for a pound of bread, or other such things, would be so small that, due to its tininess, it would not be easily felt or handled. For this reason, a <term>mixture<gloss>The process of alloying a precious metal with a base metal, like copper, to create a coin that is physically large enough to handle but low in value</gloss></term> was made of a lesser material with silver in va- <note>The text likely continues as 'valeur' (value) on the following page.</note>

---

(1) <note>original: "Instrumentum mercaturæ"</note> "An instrument of commerce," says the Latin text.

<summary>This chapter explains that different metals—gold, silver, and alloys—are necessary to facilitate different scales of trade, from international commerce to buying a simple loaf of bread. It justifies the use of "mixture" (alloying) as a practical solution for low-value transactions where pure silver would be too small to use.</summary>

value; and from this originated **black money** (1), which is suitable for small-scale commerce. Thus, in regions where silver is not abundant, it is very appropriate to produce **mixed and compound money**.

<margin>Three types of materials for making money.</margin>
It should be known that there are three types of materials suitable and appropriate for making money: the first is gold, the second is silver, and the third is black mixed metal. However, it must be noted as a general rule that an <term>alloy<gloss>The process of mixing a base metal, usually copper, with a precious metal to create a more durable or lower-value coin</gloss></term> should never be created in coinage, except only in the less precious metals used for small currency. For instance, if a country used both gold and silver money, an alloy should never be introduced into the gold currency, provided the gold is of a nature that allows it to be minted in its pure state. (As for gold that is not suitable or fit for minting <term>florins<gloss>A gold coin first minted in Florence in 1252, which became a dominant international currency in Europe</gloss></term> because it contains an <term>alloy<gloss>Here referring to natural impurities or non-standard mixtures in raw gold</gloss></term>, let it be used to make rings or other such items [2].) The reason for this is that all such mixtures are inherently suspicious; it is not easy to determine the true substance or quantity of gold within such an alloy.

<margin>Mixed gold money.</margin>
Therefore, no alloying should occur in gold coinage except in cases of extreme necessity, as previously mentioned. If a mixture must be made, it should be done in the currency where there is the least risk of suspicion or deception—namely, in the less precious metal, which is silver.

— XVII —

<sig>2</sig>

->*The fourth chapter speaks of the form and mark of the Money.*<-

<margin>Masses of silver and copper that were taken by weight.</margin>
<margin>A figure ordained for money in order to know its quality.</margin>
When men first began to trade and buy merchandise and riches by means of money, there was not yet any impression of a figure or image upon it; rather, one simply gave a portion of copper or silver in exchange for food and drink; this portion was measured by weight. And because it was a tedious and troublesome thing to frequently resort to and go to the scales, and because in this manner money could not easily be matched to merchandise by weight alone; and furthermore, the seller could not, in many ways, know the substance of the metal of which the **Money** was mixed and composed; for this reason, it was prudently and wisely provided for by the wise men of that time—namely, that the portions and pieces of money should be made of a certain material and a determined **weight** <term>weight<gloss>In medieval economics, the value of a coin was strictly tied to its weight in precious metal. Official stamping was a guarantee that the weight was correct without needing a scale for every transaction</gloss></term>, on which would be imprinted a figure well-known and recognized by everyone, which would signify the quality of the material and the truth of the weight of the **denier** <term>denier<gloss>A standard medieval silver coin, originally modeled after the Roman denarius; here used as a general term for a piece of minted currency</gloss></term>, so that, suspicion being put aside, the value of the money could be quickly known without labor and without doubt; and whether such an impression was instituted upon the coins as a sign of the truth of the material and of the... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<meta>This page follows page XVIII, which discussed the physical properties and nomenclature of coins. <page-num>XIX</page-num>

## The fifth chapter: To whom it belongs to make the penny.

<margin>It is not lawful for everyone to make money.</margin>
<margin>The prince ordains the making of money.</margin>
<margin>The intricate impression.</margin>
<margin>Do not forge money of lesser value than the prince's.</margin>
<margin>Complaint of debasement. <note>original: "empirance." This refers to the lowering of the quality or value of the currency, often through debasement of the metal.</note></margin>
Furthermore, it was reasonably ordained in ancient times, to avoid deception, that it should not be lawful for every person to make money or to strike a figure or image upon their own gold and silver; but it was ordained that the characters and letters printed upon the money should be made by a public person, appointed by many members of the community. And because the prince of the region is the most public person and of the greatest authority, it is more fitting and appropriate that he, on behalf of the entire community, should have the money forged and marked with an honest impression, rather than anyone else. This impression, therefore, made by the prince and at his command, must be intricate <note>original: "subtile"; meaning complex or finely detailed to prevent copying.</note>, and it must be very difficult to counterfeit or print. It must also be forbidden, under penalty of death, for any vassal of his land or even a neighboring foreign prince to forge money similar in appearance or of lesser value than his own; otherwise, the common people would not be able to distinguish or discern between that foreign currency and that of the prince. Such a thing would cause very great harm; and immediately, if this were done, no person—not even a vassal—can or should have the privilege to do such a thing, for it would be a cause of great falsehood. Toward a foreign prince, such an act would be a just cause to attack him and challenge him in battle.

<vocab>money, prince, penny <note>original: "denier," the standard unit of account.</note>, community, debasement, authority</vocab>

<summary>This chapter establishes that the right to mint money belongs solely to the sovereign prince to ensure trust and prevent forgery. It argues that unauthorized minting by vassals or foreign powers is a deceptive act and a legitimate cause for war.</summary>
<keywords>sovereignty, minting rights, forgery, counterfeiting, debasement, legal authority, prince, community</keywords>

<page-num>XX</page-num>

## *The sixth chapter. To whom this money belongs and ought to belong.*

<margin>The equal instrument.</margin>
Although for the common utility, the prince has the authority to mark the money and also to forge it, as has been said, nevertheless it does not follow that this Lord and prince is or ought to be the owner and master of the money current in his principality and lordship: for money is the equal instrument <note>original Latin: "Æquivalens instrumentum." This implies that money is a tool that holds a value equal to the goods being traded.</note> for exchanging natural riches <note>In medieval philosophy, "natural riches" refers to essential goods like food, clothing, and tools, which satisfy primary human needs.</note> among men, as it appears in the first chapter. Therefore, money is the true possession of those to whom such and similar natural riches (of which we spoke before) belonged; for, if anyone gives his bread or the labor of his own body for <term>money (pécune)<gloss>Derived from the Latin "pecunia," referring here to minted coins or cash.</gloss></term>, when he receives it in such a manner, it is certainly purely his own, just as his bread or the labor of his body was, which were within his free and frank power to do or give <note>original Latin: "For if someone gives his bread, or the labor of his own body, for money, when he receives it, it is his own, just as was the bread or labor of the body, which was in his free power."</note>, assuming, of course, that he is not a serf; for God, at the beginning of His beautiful world, did not give to princes alone, that is to say,

---

(1) "Equivalent instrument." (Latin text.)
(2) "For if anyone gives his bread, or the labor of his own body, for money, when he receives it, it is his own, just as was the bread or the labor of the body, which was in his free power." (Latin text.)

<summary>This chapter argues that money is the private property of the individuals who earned it through labor or trade, rather than the property of the prince who minted it. It defines money as a tool of equivalent value used to facilitate the exchange of natural goods between free people.</summary>

<header>— XXI —</header>

<margin>To whom the coins are owed.</margin>
...to our first parents, liberty and lordship over things, but also to all their posterity and generation (1) <note>original Latin: "Deus enim a principio non dedit solis principibus libertatem ad dominium rerum, sed primis parentibus et toti posteritati." This translates as: "For God from the beginning did not give the liberty of dominion over things to princes alone, but to the first parents and to all posterity." The author argues that property rights are a natural human right, not a royal grant.</note>, as it is written in the book of Genesis. Thus, for this reason, money does not belong solely to the prince; but if anyone wished to oppose this opinion because Our Savior Jesus Christ, when shown a **coin**, questioned and asked the one presenting it: "Whose image and inscription is this?" He answered that it was Caesar's. Then Jesus spoke and decreed: "Render therefore unto Caesar that which is Caesar's, and unto God the things that are God's." It is as if he wished to say: "To Caesar belongs the coin or the money, seeing that his image is printed upon it"; however, looking at the order of the Gospel, it appears clearly that one should not say the coin belongs to Caesar simply because it was inscribed with his image, but rather because it was a **tribute** belonging to Caesar, and not otherwise; for, as the Apostle <note>A reference to Saint Paul in the Epistle to the Romans, 13:7.</note> says: "To whom tribute is due, let tribute be given; and to whom taxes <note>The original French uses "betaille," likely a transcription of "taille" (a specific tax) or "vectigal" (dues/tolls).</note> are due, let taxes be given." Jesus Christ, by this decree, gives us to understand to whom tribute is due, for

it is due to him who fights and battles for the **commonwealth** <note>original: "chose publicque." Derived from the Latin "res publica," meaning the state or the public interest.</note> and for the defense of the Realm and public utility; such a person may mint money, and thus, by these reasons alleged above, the money belongs to the commu- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "community" (communauté).</note>

---

(1) "For God from the beginning did not give the liberty of dominion over things to princes alone, but to the first parents and to all posterity." (Latin text.)

<page-num>XXII</page-num>

...munity and to private individuals; and this is stated and maintained by **Aristotle** in the seventh book of the *Politics* and by **Cicero** <note>The original text uses "Tule," a common medieval/early modern French shorthand for Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note>, near the end (1).
