### *The Seventh Chapter. Regarding the expenses at which money should be minted.*

<margin>Expenses for the work of coinage and upon whom they fall.</margin>
Just as the money belongs to the community, as has been said, it should likewise be made and forged at the community's expense; and this is done quite suitably if the expenses are taken from the money itself, in such a way that the coinable material—such as gold, when it is handed over for minting or requested for the making of money—is valued at a lower price than what can be produced from that material. This is based on a certain price fixed by the Lords and officials knowledgeable in this matter; for example, if from a mark <term>mark: a medieval unit of weight for precious metals, typically around 244 grams</term> of silver one can
<margin>Seigneurial right <note>The legal right of a lord or sovereign to take a small portion of the metal for the privilege of minting.</note>.</margin>
produce LXII <note>62</note> shillings <term>shillings: originally "solz" or "sous," these were units of currency value</term>, and if the labor of the workers necessary for this requires two shillings for each mark, then the uncoined mark of silver will be worth only LX <note>60</note> shillings, and the two shillings will be for the expenses of the minter. This portion thus fixed must be such that it suffices abundantly, at all times, for the minting of money. And if the money can be produced for a lower price, it is appropriate enough that the remainder be at the distribution of the Prince and at his command, or that of the master of the mint, and let there be some payment to the

lord; however, this portion must always be moderate and should be small enough, if the money <note>The text ends mid-sentence.</note>

<page-num>XXVI</page-num>
<header>— XXVI —</header>

### *The eighth chapter. On mutations in general.*

<margin>Do not change the ancient ordinances.</margin>
<margin>Do not make harmful changes.</margin>
<margin>Changes by necessity or utility.</margin>
Before all things, it must be known that the primary laws, statutes, customs, and ordinances concerning the community should never be changed without evident necessity. Rather, according to the philosopher Aristotle, in the second book of the *Politics* <note>original: "Politiques"</note>, ancient positive law <term>positive law: specific statutes and regulations enacted by a government, as opposed to natural or universal laws</term> is by no means to be abolished or erased for a new one, even if the new one were better, unless there were a very great and notable difference in the quality of it; for such kinds of changes <note>original: "mutacions"; here referring specifically to alterations in the legal weight, fineness, or value of money</note> diminish the authority and reverence of those laws, and even more so if they are made frequently. From such changes arise scandals and murmurs among the people and the danger of disobedience; and even more so if such changes were made for the worse, for then they would be intolerable and unjust. Now, therefore, it is certain that the rate and the price of coins in the Kingdom must be like a law and a firm ordinance that should not be altered or changed in any way. As a sign of this law and rate, all pensions and annual revenues are assessed at the price of the money—that is to say, at a certain number of pounds, shillings, and pence <note>original: "livres, solz et deniers"; the standard denominations of the French accounting system</note>—whereby it is clear that no change should ever be made unless, by chance, necessity compelled it, or there was evident utility for the entire community. On this subject, Aristotle says in his fif-

<page-num>XXVII</page-num>

...fifth book of the *Ethics* <note>original: "Ethiques"; referring to Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*, Book V, where he discusses the role of money in maintaining justice in exchange</note>, speaking of money <note>original: "monnoies"</note>:

*Certainly the thing which most firmly should remain in existence is money.*

<margin>Several ways of mutation.</margin>

A mutation <term>mutation: a technical term for the alteration of the coinage, often referring to debasement or changes in legal value</term>, therefore, of money, as I can generally understand it, can be imagined to be done in many ways: one way can be done in form and in figure <note>original: "figure"; the physical design or the stamp impressed upon the coin</note>; another way, in the proportion of metal <term>proportion: the "fineness" or ratio of precious metal (gold or silver) compared to the base metal alloy</term>; another way, in the price and the name of it; further, it can be changed in quantity and in weight; and finally, in the substance of the material <note>original: "substance de matière"; changing the metal itself, such as switching from silver to a lesser metal</note>. In each of these five ways aforementioned, either together or at different times, money can be changed. It is well to declare, while passing briefly through these ways, and to inquire through reason whether any of these can be just, and when, and by whom, and how, and for what cause.

<page-num>XXVIII</page-num>

## *The ninth chapter is about the mutation of money in design.*

The design <note>original: "figure"; the visual image or stamp impressed upon the coin</note> or character printed upon the money can be made and minted in two ways: first, <margin>The design can be changed.</margin> the design can be changed on the money without forbidding the circulation <note>original: "cours"; the legal status of the money as a medium of exchange</note> of the previous coins, such as if a prince, on the money made during his time, inscribed his name while still allowing and permitting the circulation of the earlier money. This action would not strictly be a "mutation," nor would there be much harm <note>original: "vice"; in this context, a moral or legal fault</note> if such a thing were done, provided that in doing so, no other mutation was made. Otherwise, a mutation of design can be made by creating new money and forbidding the circulation of the old; and this method is properly called <margin>When a mutation can be made.</margin> mutation. This can be justly done for one of the two following reasons:

The first is when some foreign Prince or certain counterfeiters <term>counterfeiters: "faulsaires," individuals who illegally manufacture fake currency to deceive the public</term> have maliciously erased or forged the forms and dies <term>dies: "coings," the engraved metal stamps used to strike designs onto blank metal discs to create coins</term> of the money, and they are found to have made sophisticated and false money, similar in size, color, and design. When there is no other way to remedy this, it would be expedient and very necessary to change the dies and the impression of the design on the money.

The other reason might be if, by chance, the old money was, through its ancient and long circulation...

<summary>This chapter discusses changes to the visual design of coins, distinguishing between simple updates (like adding a ruler's name) and "proper" mutations that replace old currency. It argues that changing a coin's design is only justified to prevent counterfeiting or to replace worn-out currency.</summary>

<margin>When the change is vain and ambitious.</margin>

[...too] greatly worsened and diminished in weight, then the circulation <note>original: "cours"; the legal status of the money as a medium of exchange</note> of it can and should be forbidden, and a new currency should be minted which is better and has a different design <note>original: "impression de figure"; the specific image or stamp on the coin</note>; and thus the common people would know, by that difference, how to distinguish between the good and the bad, the old and the new; but, it does not seem to me that the Prince can lawfully forbid the circulation of the first money without any of these causes; otherwise, such a change <note>original: "mutacion"</note> would be unnecessary, scandalous, and very damaging to the community. It does not appear in any way that the prince could be moved by any other path to make such a change, except for one of the two following reasons: namely, either because he wants his own image and name to be printed on every penny <term>penny: "denier," a common medieval silver coin that served as a basic unit of account</term>, and nothing else—which is an act of irreverence toward his predecessors, and is a vain and wasteful ambition—or because he wishes to mint more money so that he might thereby obtain more profit, as was mentioned in the seventh chapter; and this manner is a very evil and ugly greed <term>greed: "convoitise," an inordinate desire for wealth, often implying the exploitation of others</term> which would be carried out to the prejudice and damage of the whole community.

<page-num>XXX</page-num>
<header>— XXX —</header>

### *Chapter Ten. Of the change in the proportion of Money.*

<margin>The proportion of gold to silver.</margin>
Proportion is a comparison or relation <note>original: "habitude"; here referring to the relative standing or ratio between two things</note> made of one thing to another, just as in the proportion of gold money to silver money, there must be a certain relation and proportion in value and in weight; for as gold is by its nature more noble, more precious, and better than silver, and more difficult to find and obtain, it is certainly fitting and very reasonable that the same weight of gold should be worth much more and be held in more precious esteem, in a certain proportion, than silver. For example, perhaps a proportion of twenty to one, so that one pound of gold would be worth twenty pounds of silver, and one mark <term>mark: "marc," a traditional unit of weight for gold and silver, usually equaling eight ounces</term> of gold would be worth twenty marks of silver; and similarly from the large units to the small. It is also possible to establish another proportion, such as twenty-five to three, or some other similar valuation <note>original: "évaluacion"</note>; but nevertheless, this proportion must follow the natural relation or value of gold to silver in terms of preciousness. According to that natural value, this proportion must be instituted, and it is not permitted to change it voluntarily, nor to go against it, nor can it be justly varied, except for a reasonable cause and by the variation of that material in part, which <note>The sentence ends mid-thought, continuing on the next page to explain how the ratio should only change if the scarcity of the metals themselves changes.</note>

<vocab>Proportion, Gold money, Silver money, change, weight, mark of gold, pound of gold, preciousness, valuation</vocab>

<summary>This chapter explains that the value ratio between gold and silver coins (proportion) should not be set arbitrarily by a prince but must reflect the natural market scarcity and "nobility" of the metals. It argues that this ratio should remain stable unless the relative supply of the metals themselves changes significantly.</summary>

<page-num>XXXIV</page-num>

### *The eleventh chapter. Concerning the change of the name of the currency.*

<margin>Names of the currencies.</margin>
As it was said in the fourth chapter, there are some accidental qualities of currencies that are named and taken from the maker or the place where they are forged, and these have little or no impact on the base value; but others are more essential and appropriate to the currencies, that is to say, <margin>Pence, shillings, and pounds.</margin> pence <term>deniers: the smallest unit of currency, derived from the Roman denarius</term>, shillings <term>solz: or sous, derived from the Roman solidus; usually worth twelve pence</term>, pounds <term>livres: derived from the Roman libra, a unit of weight and currency</term> and similar names, which signify and denote the price, the weight, and the value of the currency; and which also were established by the ancients through high considerations and great mystery, of which Cassiodorus <note>Cassiodorus (c. 485 – c. 585 AD) was a Roman statesman and scholar whose works were highly influential in the Middle Ages.</note> says:

> "It is to be noted with what great reason these monies were gathered and named by the ancients, for they intended and said six thousand pence to be one shilling <note>original: "soult" (solidus). In medieval numerology, 6,000 often symbolized the total age of the world in years; the solidus was symbolically linked to the Sun (Sol) and the completion of time.</note>, so that the roundness of the noble metal—that is gold—like the radiant Sun, might conveniently enclose the age of the world within itself. And certainly, wise and learned antiquity does not call the penny 'perfect' without cause."

<margin>The ounce is the first degree.</margin>
The ancients called the ounce <term>once: a unit of weight</term> the first degree of measurement by twelve, following the likeness of the count of twelve months which together make the course of a year. O noble intention of prudent and wise men! <margin>The refined insight of the Philosophers.</margin> O thing foreseen and exquisite of the great Philosophers who divided and distinguished so many things necessary for human use, and which contain so many secret things in nature!

<summary>This chapter explains that while some currency names are arbitrary, others like the penny, shilling, and pound have deep symbolic and philosophical origins linked to the measurement of time and the nature of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>etymology of money, Cassiodorus, pence, shillings, pounds, ounce, medieval numerology, symbolism of gold</keywords>

with good reason, then, is the pound <term>livre: the unit of currency representing a "libra" or pound weight of silver</term> so called, which determines and contains within itself the consideration of so many things." Such are the words of the Philosopher Cassiodorus <note>The author concludes the quote from Cassiodorus, who linked the names of coins to both mathematical harmony and the weight of the Roman pound.</note>. Even if we use these names and coins in a different manner today, nevertheless the pieces should never be changed (in vain) <note>original: "frustra" (Latin). The author warns against "mutations," or arbitrary changes to currency value by the sovereign.</note>. Let there be, for example, three types of names: <margin>Penny, shilling, and pound.</margin> the first worth a penny <term>denier</term>, the second a shilling <term>solt</term>, and the third a pound <term>livre</term>. If, therefore, the name of one is changed and the others are not, the proportion will immediately be altered—as if someone were to call or make the first piece of money worth two pennies while the others remained unchanged. The proportion would be varied, which is a thing that ought not to be done, as it appears in the preceding chapter; except, perhaps, by chance and very rarely. And of that, for now, I take no account. It is necessary, then, that if the proportion is to remain unchanged and the penny changes its name, the others must also change proportionally: if the first penny is called "two pennies," then the second must be called "two shillings," and the third "two pounds." And if no other change were made, it would be necessary to buy or price merchandise at a proportionally higher price. And if such a change of names were done for nothing—which should never be done, for it would be a great scandal and the naming would be false—that thing would be called a "pound" which, in truth, would not be a

<page-num>XXXVI</page-num>

pound <note>original: "livre"</note>, which is an improper thing, as has been said; however, no other disadvantage would follow, except for those cases where pensions <term>pensions: fixed annual payments or annuities, often granted by a lord or the state</term> or other revenues were not assigned to a specific number of coins <note>original: "pécunes"; referring to the actual physical pieces of money</note>; where it also appears immediately, through the aforementioned disadvantages, that these types of revenues would, by such a mutation <term>mutacion: a change in the legal value, weight, or metal content of currency by royal decree</term>, proportionally increase or decrease unreasonably and unjustly to the detriment of many. ### The twelfth chapter. Concerning the mutation of the weight of money.

<margin>Creating smaller denominations.</margin>
If the weight of the money were changed, and along with this the price of its name <note>original: "pris de l'appellacion"; the face value assigned to a specific name like "shilling"</note> and its appearance were varied proportionally, it would be to create another kind of money—much like one who would make two half-pennies <term>oboles: small coins worth half a denier or penny</term> out of one penny <term>denier: a standard silver coin, often translated as a penny</term>, or some such thing—without loss or gain. This thing could, in some sense, be done lawfully, without any real transmutation in the material of the coinage, which can only happen rarely, as was said in the tenth chapter.

<margin>The Prince provides certainty for the coinage.</margin>

First, because the image and the inscription of the Prince are placed on the penny to signify and make known the certainty of the weight, quality, and goodness of the material, as was demonstrated in the fourth chapter. Through this method, the prince could acquire for himself foreign wealth <term>pecune: from the Latin pecunia, referring to money or assets</term>, nor by any other means could he be moved to make such an alteration <term>mutacion: the technical medieval term for debasing or changing the standard of the currency</term>; he would thus receive pennies <term>deniers: the standard silver currency units</term> of good weight, and from them he would forge pennies of a small or lesser weight, which he would then pay out. ## *The thirteenth chapter. On the change of the material of the currency.*

<margin>Ceasing the minting of coins.</margin>
The material of the penny <term>denier: the standard silver unit of medieval currency</term>, as stated above, is either pure or mixed. If it is pure, it can be abandoned due to a shortage of material; for instance, if little or no gold can be found, it would be necessary to stop minting it. If a sufficient abundance were to return later, then the minting of Money should resume, as has been done at times. Conversely, a material should also be abandoned for minting due to an excessive abundance found. 

<margin>Copper currency.</margin>
For this reason, copper currency ceased in the past and left common usage, as was stated above in the third chapter; but such causes have rarely occurred often. In no other way should the material of currencies, whether pure or simple, be abandoned or newly taken up again. 

<margin>Which material should be alloyed.</margin>
And if there is any mixture in such material, it should only be made with a less precious metal that is itself mintable <term>alayer: to alloy, or mix a base metal with a precious one to adjust its value or durability</term>, as was permitted in the said third chapter. 

<margin>Proportion of gold to silver.</margin>
And in "black money" <note>original: "noire monnoie"; coins made of billon, an alloy of silver and a high percentage of copper, which gave them a dark or "blackened" appearance compared to pure silver</note>, so that one may distinguish the pure and good from the mixed and composed, this mixture must also be according to a certain proportion—such as ten marks <note>a "mark" was a medieval unit of weight, usually around 244 grams</note> of silver against one of gold—as is expedient and ordained by wise lords knowledgeable in this matter. And this proportion can be changed for some reasonable

<page-num>XLIII</page-num>

->*The fourteenth chapter. Concerning the compound alteration of currencies.*<-

<margin>Causes for alterations are rare.</margin>
<margin>Alteration should be performed by the community.</margin>
<margin>Money is a thing of justice.</margin>
<margin>Shekels, an ancient currency.</margin>
The compound alteration <term>mutacion composée: Oresme refers here to the practice of changing multiple aspects of a coin at once—such as reducing its weight while simultaneously lowering the purity of its metal.</term> of currency occurs when several simple alterations are combined into one; for example, if one were to mix the proportions of the coins or the mixtures of the metal together, and along with that, the weight; or if the possible combinations of the five simple alterations mentioned previously <note>In previous chapters, Oresme identifies five ways to alter money: changing the name, the value, the weight, the material (purity), or the form (design).</note> were carried out in many different ways. For no simple alteration should ever be made to currency except for the real and natural causes already stated, which happen very infrequently and only by chance; and there has never been a true occasion to perform a compound alteration of currency. And if, by chance, the need for it should ever arise, for an even stronger reason than with a simple change, such a compound alteration must never be made by the prince alone, because of the dangers and inconveniences touched upon before; rather, it should be done by the community. For if so many abuses follow from simple alterations when they are done improperly, as has been said before, far greater and worse abuses would follow from a compound alteration. Currency, therefore, should be just and true in its substance and in its weight, just as is signified to us in Holy Scripture where it speaks of **Abraham**, how he bought a field for which he gave 400 shekels <note>A reference to Genesis 23:16, where Abraham buys a burial plot for Sarah. In the Middle Ages, this was cited as a foundational example of honest commerce.</note>

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that "compound alterations"—changing multiple aspects of a coin simultaneously—are even more dangerous and prone to abuse than simple changes, and must only be handled by the community rather than the prince. He concludes by citing the biblical example of Abraham to prove that currency must be a matter of truth and justice.</summary>

### *The fifteenth chapter. That the profit acquired by the prince through the alteration of currency is unjust.*

<margin>Taking profit from the alteration of money is a wicked and unjust acquisition.</margin>
<margin>Deception through currency alterations.</margin>

<page-num>XLVIII</page-num>

### *The sixteenth chapter. That profiting from the alteration of currency is against nature (1).*

Although all injustice is in some way against nature, nevertheless, taking profit from the <term>alteration: "mutacion," the practice of changing the weight or value of coinage for the prince's benefit</term> of money is an injustice and is, in a specific way, unnatural. It is a natural thing to multiply and gather certain natural <term>Riches: "Richesses," meaning tangible assets like land or food</term>, such as grains, corn, wheat, and fields, which when they are well-sown and well-cultivated, return a hundredfold grain, as Ovid <note>Publius Ovidius Naso, the Roman poet. Oresme refers to the classical idea of nature's inherent fertility.</note> says; but, it is a monstrous thing and against nature that a thing not fit for bearing should give birth, or that a thing sterile and dry of any species should bear fruit or multiply by itself, as is the case with <term>money: "pécune" or "monnoie"</term>. And how then can it be that this money, in and of itself, brings profit? For putting it forth and exchanging it for merchandise of natural riches or spending it for one's own use is a natural and lawful thing, but receiving or giving it for itself, such as changing one coin for another, or handing over one for another, such profit is vile, dishonest, and against nature. For this reason

<margin>(1) This entire chapter reflects Aristotle's erroneous <note>The commentator here notes the historical shift in economic thought, as modern economics recognizes the time-value of money, which Aristotle did not.</note> conception regarding the *sterility* of money; Oresme <note>Nicole Oresme (c. 1320–1382), the author, who was a physicist and counselor to King Charles V of France.</note> uses the same kind of arguments against the *exchange* of various types of money, which he denounces as the result of the alteration of the <term>coins: "espèces," referring to the physical currency units</term>.</margin>

Aristotle proves, in the first book of the *Politics*, that usury is a thing against nature, for the natural use of money is that it should be an instrument to exchange and purchase natural Riches, as has often been said; whoever then uses it in another way abuses it and acts against the natural institution of money, when he wants the <term>denier: a small silver coin, used here as a general term for a penny or a unit of currency</term> to give birth to and bring forth another denier, which is a thing against nature. Furthermore, in these <term>alterations: "mutacions," specifically the practice of debasing the coinage by reducing its precious metal content</term> where one takes a profit, it is necessary to call a "denier" that which, in truth, is not a denier, and a "pound" that which is not a pound, and so with other weights, as has been said before. It appears then that these alterations

For you cannot give the full integrity of the names and fail by diminishing them; thus, to erase such certain secrets of nature is nothing other than to cruelly desire and wish to confound the certain effects of truth; weight and measure appear first in proof,

<page-num>LI</page-num>

## The seventeenth chapter. Profit made by the alteration of money is worse than usury.

<margin>Three ways of profiting from money.</margin>
<margin>Alteration is called a toll or a tax-farming.</margin>
<margin>Saint Matthew did not return to money-changing.</margin>
<margin>Defilement of the body.</margin>
There are three ways, it seems to me, by which one can profit from money without putting it to its natural use: the first way is through the art of money-changing, either by guarding it or being a trader in it; the second is <term>usury: "usure," the practice of lending money at interest, which was widely condemned in the Middle Ages</term>; and the third is through the <term>alteration: "mutacion," the act of a ruler changing the weight, fineness, or value of coins</term> of money. The first way is vile. The second is bad, and the third is worse and very evil. Aristotle made mention of the first two but not of the third, because in his time such malice had not yet been invented. And that the first way is vile, dishonest, and blameworthy, Aristotle proves by the reasoning already alleged above in the preceding chapter, saying that this method is in some way making money "give birth," <note>Aristotle argued in his *Politics* that money is "sterile" by nature and should not produce more of itself (interest/profit).</note> and he calls this <term>money-weighing: "abolostaticon," a corruption of the Greek term *obolostatikē*, referring to the petty or sordid trade of money-changing</term>, which is commonly called a toll or custom duty. This is why the Apostle Saint Matthew, who had been a money-changer, did not return to his former work after the resurrection of Jesus Christ, as did Saint Peter, who had been a fisherman. And, in assigning the cause for such a thing, Saint Gregory says that it is one thing to seek one's living by fishing, and another to increase one's <term>wealth: "pecunes," derived from the Latin *pecunia*</term> through the profit of tolls and taxes. And he says further that there are several works which, without sinning at all, cannot

...[is] similar to and like <term>usury: "usure," the practice of lending money at interest, which was considered a sin in the Middle Ages</term>, but is even worse than usury, considering that it is less voluntary and against the will of the <term>subjects: "subgectz," the people living under the authority of a prince or ruler</term>, without it bringing them any profit or being of any necessity; for the gain of a usurer is not so excessive nor so prejudicial or general to anyone as this <term>alteration: "mutacion," the act of a sovereign changing the weight, fineness, or face value of coins</term>, which is imposed over and above the whole community. I say that it is not only similar to usury, but is tyrannical and fraudulent, so much so that I doubt whether it should rather be called violent plunder or fraudulent <term>exaction: the act of demanding or levying a payment through authority, often implying an abuse of power</term>.

### *The eighteenth chapter. That such changes of money, in and of themselves, are not to be permitted.*

<margin>Permission of vile things.</margin>
<margin>Disadvantages.</margin>
Sometimes, so that worse things do not happen and to avoid scandal, certain dishonest and evil things are permitted in the community, such as <term>public brothels: "bordeaulx publiques," licensed houses of prostitution which medieval authorities sometimes tolerated to prevent greater social disorder</term>. Sometimes also, due to certain necessities and opportunities, some vile trades are permitted, such as the art of money-changing, and even worse, such as <term>usury: "usure," the practice of lending money at interest, which was condemned by the medieval Church</term>; but regarding such a <term>change: "mutation," the alteration of the currency's value or metal content</term> of money to take gain from it, there appears to be nothing in the world necessary, or otherwise, for which this evil gain can or should be permitted; for by this practice one does not avoid scandal, but rather generates it—as was said in the eighth chapter—and many other disadvantages follow because of this, some of which have already been touched upon, and others will yet be seen hereafter. And there is no necessity or opportunity to do this, nor can it profit the <term>public good: "chose publicque," the common welfare of the state</term> in any way; a manifest sign of which is that such changes are newly invented <note>The author argues that because these practices have no long historical precedent in successful states, they are a dangerous modern corruption</note>, as was already mentioned in the preceding chapter.

<margin>Newly invented changes.</margin>
<margin>A change made by a king of Italy, reproved.</margin>
Never, in Cities or Kingdoms governed prosperously in the past or now, was this done, nor were histories ever found that mentioned this thing, except in a chapter written by <note>Cassiodorus (c. 485 – c. 585) was a Roman statesman and writer who served under Theodoric</note> Cassiodorus, concerning <note>Theodoric the Great (454–526), King of the Ostrogoths who ruled Italy</note> Theodoric, king of Italy, regarding a small change made to the weight of the

<summary>The author argues that unlike other tolerated social evils (like brothels or usury), currency debasement serves no public necessity and only creates scandal. He highlights the "novelty" of these practices, noting that historically prosperous states avoided them, with rare and criticized exceptions.</summary>

<page-num>LV</page-num>

Currency is rebuked in various ways and is effectively condemned, though a certain official <note>original: "Quidam Arcarius," referring to a treasurer or state official</note> had done so to pay certain <term>wages: "souldées," specifically the pay or "solde" given to soldiers</term>. Regarding this matter, the King, writing to <term>Boethius: Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), a Roman statesman and philosopher whose works on logic and music were foundational to medieval education</term>
<margin>A letter that condemns the change.</margin>
on this subject, said among several other things: 
> "Wherefore let your prudence, schooled by teachings and lessons, cast out most perverse falsehood from the company of truth; and let none among you be inclined or covetous to destroy, seduce, or devour that integrity, etc." 

And after several other things were inserted, he said again: 
> *A fixed thing given to laborers certainly should not be diminished or increased, but from those of whom loyal deeds are required, less than full compensation should not be given.* <note>The author argues that if a ruler expects loyalty from his subjects, he must not "mutilate" their pay through currency debasement.</note>

If therefore the Italians or Romans finally made such <term>changes: "mutacions," the practice of altering the weight or metal content of coins to the sovereign's profit</term>, as one still sees today in some old, bad money that is sometimes found in the fields, this was, perhaps, one of the causes
<margin>Cause of the destruction of the Italian lordship.</margin>
by which their noble <term>Lordship: "Seigneurie," referring here to the Roman Empire and its sovereign authority</term> came to nothing. And so it appears then that such changes are so evil that, by their nature, they are not to be permitted in any way.

# *Chapter Nineteen: Concerning certain disadvantages affecting the prince, which follow from the mutations of currencies.*

Very great disadvantages arise and are born, in several ways, from the <term>mutations of currencies: "mutacions des monnoies," the practice of a ruler changing the weight or metal content of coins to increase state revenue</term>; some of the most principal ones affect the prince, and others affect all the people of his Kingdom, as has been seen in the recent past within the Kingdom of France and its dependencies and neighboring countries; and some disadvantages have been mentioned before, which it is now expedient to recite. First, it is a very shameful thing for a prince to commit fraud by falsifying his currency, calling "gold" that which is not gold, and delivering that which is not a <term>pound: "livre," a unit of account based on a specific weight of silver</term> as if it were a known pound (1). It has been said, furthermore, that it is the proper duty of a Prince to condemn and punish <term>counterfeiters: "faulx monnoyers," those who illegally minted coins or altered their value</term> and those who commit any falsity or theft regarding the currency. How, then, should he not feel great shame if one finds in him the very thing that he ought to punish in another with a most foul and infamous death? Furthermore, it is a very great scandal

### *Chapter Twenty, Regarding the disadvantages affecting the whole community.*

Among many disadvantages arising from the <term>debasement: "mutacion," the alteration of the coin's weight or metal purity</term> of the money, which affect and concern the entire community, there is one that was touched upon in the fifteenth chapter; that is to say, the way in which princes could draw to themselves almost all the <term>wealth: "pecune," from the Latin 'pecunia,' meaning money or property</term> of the community, and thereby excessively impoverish their <term>subjects: "subgetz," the people living under the ruler's authority</term>. And just as some diseases are more contagious and more perilous than others because they are more perceptible and closer to the noble organs (1); so too such an evasion, as is this currency change, is all the more perilous and harmful the less it is noticed. For the grievance that comes from it is not as quickly felt or perceived by the people as it would be through another <term>tax: "cuillecte," a general collection or levy of money</term>, and yet no other tax, nor any similar burden, can be more grievous or greater. Furthermore, gold and silver, through such changes and <term>debasements: "empiremens," literally 'worsening,' referring to the lowering of the currency's value</term>, diminish and decrease within a Kingdom, and, notwithstanding <margin>Export of currencies.</margin> all the precautions and prohibitions that are made, the metal is transported abroad where it is valued at a higher price; for, perhaps, men more willingly carry their

---

(1) The Latin text says: "And just as certain chronic diseases are more dangerous than others and are less perceptible, so such an exaction, the less it is perceived, the more dangerously it is practiced." <note>original Latin: "Et quemadmodum quædam aegritudines chronicæ sunt aliis periculosiores et sunt minus sensibiles, ita talis exactio, quanto minus percipitur tanto periculosius exercetur."</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the negative social and economic consequences of "mutacions" (monetary alterations). While the previous page focused on the physical loss of bullion and the cessation of foreign trade, this page describes the internal collapse of trust, the inability to pay wages or rents fairly, and the decline of charitable works.</meta>

<page-num>LXI</page-num>

...and for this reason, while such <term>alterations: "mutacions," changes to the weight, value, or metal content of coins</term> last, the revenues of the prince and the nobles, as well as pensions and <term>annual wages: "gaiges annuelz"</term>, <term>levies: "lievaiges," taxes or duties</term>, <term>rents: "rentes"</term>, and similar things, cannot be properly or justly assessed or paid, as was done in the past and is done at present; and, what is worse, <term>money: "pecune," from the Latin 'pecunia,' meaning wealth or cash</term> and <term>coinage: "monnoie"</term> cannot be lent or entrusted from one person to another <note>The text suggests that when the value of money is constantly changing, credit and lending (the act of "believing" or "trusting" someone with money) become impossible.</note>; and thus, because of such alterations, the world is in turmoil, and even divine service and the <term>charitable alms: "aumosnes caritatives"</term> for the poor members of God are cooled and delayed <note>The "poor members of God" refers to the destitute and the sick who were seen as representing Christ on earth.</note>; and yet, a sufficiency of <term>coinable material: "nature monnoïable," the gold or silver bullion available for minting</term>, merchandise, and all the other things mentioned before are necessary and very useful to human nature, while the opposite is extremely harmful to the entire community.

<summary>This page explains how currency instability disrupts the payment of wages and taxes, destroys the system of credit and lending, and even hinders the performance of religious duties and charity. It concludes that a stable supply of precious metals and trade is essential for a healthy society.</summary>
<keywords>economic instability, social justice, medieval finance, charity, credit, currency debasement, wages</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Oresme’s critique of monetary debasement. While the previous page focused on the disruption of legal and social obligations like wages and rents, this page shifts to a sociological perspective, contrasting "productive" members of society with speculators and officials who profit from currency manipulation.</meta>

<page-num>LXII</page-num>

## *The twenty-first chapter. Concerning the harms that affect a part of the community because of these alterations.*

<margin>Money traders.</margin>
<margin>Abuses of merchants and receivers through currency alterations.</margin>
Some parts of the community are occupied with honorable labors, useful to the whole <term>commonwealth: "chose publicque," the public good or state</term>—such as acquiring <term>natural riches: "richesses naturelles"</term> <note>Oresme follows the Aristotelian tradition, distinguishing between "natural" wealth (food, land, tools) and "artificial" wealth (money).</note>, offering prayers and supplications according to divine law, upholding Justice, and managing certain affairs for the common utility and necessity. These include the clergy and the religious, judges, the knights of the land, merchants, laborers and those who till the soil, craftsmen, and the like. However, the other part of this community is occupied with nothing but growing and multiplying its <term>money: "pecune"</term> through no real production <note>The text suggests these individuals do not create "natural riches" but merely manipulate "artificial" currency.</note>, such as <term>money-changers: "changeurs"</term>, money traders, <term>cullers: "billonneurs"</term> <note>Specifically, those who sorted through coinage to pick out "strong" coins to melt down or sell at a premium when the currency was being debased.</note> and those like them; this practice of <term>alteration: "mutacion"</term> is quite shameful, as was stated in the eighteenth chapter. Among these people, then, who are essentially unnecessary to the commonwealth, some are tax-collectors and officials who handle and receive vast sums of money; such men take a very great portion of the profit and gain resulting from these currency alterations. They do so maliciously and perhaps even against God and justice, for they are <term>enveloped: "enclopes"</term> in immense wealth, and have acquired so much property...

<summary>Oresme contrasts the productive classes of society—who provide food, justice, and prayer—with money-changers and officials who grow rich solely by speculating on currency debasement. He argues that those who profit from these "alterations" are unnecessary to the public good and act against divine justice.</summary>
<keywords>social classes, natural riches, money-changers, billonneurs, speculation, commonwealth, economic justice, Nicole Oresme</keywords>

### *The twenty-second chapter: whether the community can make such <term>alterations: "mutacions"</term> in the money.*

<margin>For the ransom of the prince, or in case of misfortune.</margin>
Since the money belongs to the community, as was stated and demonstrated in the 6th Chapter <note>Oresme previously argued that while the Prince stamps the coin, the value belongs to those who trade with it.</note>, it seems that this community may ordain concerning it at its will, and thus change it and take profit from it at its pleasure, and treat it as its own property (1); and likewise, if due to war or for the ransom of its imprisoned prince <note>This is a direct reference to King John II of France, who was captured by the English at the Battle of Poitiers in 1356. His massive ransom necessitated creative—and often damaging—fiscal measures.</note>, or other cases of misfortune, this community had a necessary need for a large sum of <term>money: "pecune"</term>, it could then raise it through the alteration of the money. This would not be against nature, nor would it be usury, considering that the act would not be done by the prince alone, but by that community to whom the money belongs; by this, many of the reasons previously stated against the alter- <note>The word is cut off at the bottom of the page: "mu-" for "mutacions" (alterations).</note>

### *The twenty-third chapter. The argument for how the Prince may alter the Currency (1).*

<margin>Privilege to alter.</margin>
It is customary to say that, in cases of necessity, all things belong to the Prince; and so, by this reasoning, he may take and ordain as much and in such a way as he pleases and sees fit concerning the money of his kingdom, for an apparent necessity, or for the defense of the <term>commonwealth: "chose publicque"</term> <note>Literally "the public thing," similar to the Latin *res publica*.</note>, or to maintain his sovereignty and estate. The method of assembling and collecting <term>money: "pecune"</term> <note>From the Latin *pecunia*, referring to wealth or funds.</note> through the <term>alteration: "mutacion"</term> of the currency is very convenient and suitable, as can be proven by what was said in the previous chapter. And even assuming that the Prince could not normally alter the currency or take such profit from it by ordinary and common law; nevertheless, one might say that he could do so by another private right, such as a special privilege granted to him by the Pope, by the Emperor, or granted to him long ago by the community, and that he may possess and enjoy it by hereditary right because of his good merits. Furthermore, money, as stated above, belongs to the community, and the community can alter it, as stated in the previous chapter; therefore this commun-

<page-num>LXXI</page-num>

### *The twenty-fourth chapter. Responses to the preceding arguments and the main conclusion.*

Even though, in the solution to the first argument, many difficulties might perhaps arise, nevertheless, in briefly passing over what currently occurs to me: so that the <term>Prince: "Prince"</term> does not feign such <term>necessity: "necessité"</term> to exist where none does—as <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE) was the primary authority on political science in the Middle Ages; Oresme frequently cites his *Politics* to distinguish between a legitimate king and a tyrant.</note> tyrants do, according to Aristotle—it must be determined by the <term>community: "communauté"</term> or by the greater part of it, either expressly or tacitly, when, what kind, and how great a necessity appears to justify doing so. I say "expressly" because the community ought to assemble for this purpose, if it is possible and if the means exist.

<margin>When one can make an alteration and a loan.</margin>
I also say "tacitly," that is to say, if the necessity were so urgent that the people could not be called together in time, and if the necessity were so evident that it afterwards becomes notoriously apparent; in such a case, it is lawful for the Prince to receive some of the resources of his subjects, not through the <term>alterations: "mutacions"</term> <note>In this context, "alterations" refers to the practice of debasing the currency—reducing the weight or purity of coins while keeping their face value the same.</note> of the currency, but by way of a <term>loan: "prest"</term>, for which he must afterwards make full <term>restitution: "restitucion"</term> (1).

<margin>Restitution.</margin>
To the other argument where it was said that the Prince might have a privilege to change the currency: first, I

---

(1) <note>original: "Tunc non licet Principi aliquid recipere de facultatibus subditorum per mutationem monetæ, sed per modum mutui, de quo postea facienda est restitutio plenaria."</note> "In such a case it is not lawful for the Prince to take anything from the resources of his subjects through a change of the currency, but rather by way of a loan, for which full restitution must later be made."

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that any "necessity" used to justify seizing subjects' wealth must be verified by the community to prevent tyranny. He asserts that even in emergencies, the Prince should use loans requiring full restitution rather than debasing the currency.</summary>

<keywords>sovereignty, tyranny, consent of the governed, necessity, currency debasement, public debt, Aristotle, Nicole Oresme</keywords>

<margin>The Pope cannot grant this privilege.</margin>

Regarding the <term>community: "communaulté"</term>, it is also said in the twenty-second chapter that it cannot change the <term>currencies: "monnoies"</term>, except in certain cases; and if it committed this task to the <term>Prince: "Prince"</term> to carry out through a reasonable <term>alteration: "mutacion"</term> <note>Original: "mutacion." In medieval economics, this refers specifically to changing the weight, fineness, or value of coins.</note>, which it can do, as appears in this chapter and others already mentioned,

<margin>By privilege of the community.</margin>
the Prince would not do this thing as the principal actor, but as the executor of the <term>public ordinance: "ordonnance publique"</term>. <summary>Oresme argues that neither the Pope nor the Emperor can grant a Prince the right to debase currency because one cannot grant a "privilege" to do something inherently wrong. <meta>This page continues Oresme’s argument from page LXXII, where he posited that a community cannot fully alienate its rights to the Prince. <page-num>LXXIII</page-num>

[the community], which by nature is free and inclined toward liberty, would never knowingly submit itself to <term>servitude: "servitude"</term>, nor bow down to the yoke of <term>tyrannical power: "puissance tyrannique"</term>. And if the community, being deceived, overly terrified, or coerced, should grant this power to the Prince, such a <term>change: "mutacion"</term> <note>In this context, "mutacion" refers to a change in the legal or political state of the community's rights.</note>—having no regard for the resulting inconveniences—and given that 
<margin>The community can revoke its power.</margin>
it finds itself thus subjected, it may immediately revoke and recall this power. Furthermore, a thing which belongs to someone by <term>natural right: "droit naturel"</term> can never be justly transferred to another; just as <term>money: "monnoie"</term> belongs by right to the <term>community: "communaulté"</term>, as appears clearly enough from the chapters mentioned above. Just as the community cannot grant the Prince the power and authority to abuse the wives of his citizens at his will and whomever 
<margin>The community cannot grant the privilege of taking profit.</margin>
he pleases, similarly it cannot give him the privilege of making <term>currency: "monnoies"</term> at his will; a privilege of which he could only make ill use, and take such <term>profit: "gaing"</term> from its <term>alterations: "mutacions"</term> <note>Specifically the debasement or changing of the weight and fineness of coins to generate revenue for the crown.</note> as he pleased. And regarding what was mentioned above—that because the community might not agree on the alteration of currency, the right could descend to the power of the Prince—to this I say that this may well be done regarding a certain portion and for a certain time, but not to grant or give him the power to take so much profit through the undue alterations mentioned above.

To respond to the other argument taken from the fifth chapter, concerning how the Prince can have and take some...

<summary>Oresme argues that the community’s right to its currency is a natural right that cannot be fully surrendered to a Prince, comparing the abuse of coinage to a tyrant's abuse of his subjects' families. He asserts that any power the Prince has over money is as an executor for the public good, not for personal profit.</summary>

...profit from the <term>Coinage: "Monnoie"</term>, it is simply answered that this is like a small and limited allowance, which can never be increased by the aforementioned <term>alterations: "mutacions"</term> <note>Oresme uses "mutacions" to describe the practice of debasing the currency—changing its weight or metal purity to generate profit for the crown.</note>, but must remain stable, without any change. <page-num>LXXVI</page-num>

### *The twenty-fifth chapter. That a tyrannical Prince cannot long endure.*

In this chapter and in the final one following it, I intend to show and prove that demanding and raising <term>funds: "pecunes"</term> <note>Derived from the Latin *pecunia*, Oresme uses this term to refer to wealth or financial capital extracted from the populace.</note> through such <term>currency alterations: "mutations de monnoies"</term> is against the honor of the kingdom and to the detriment of all royal posterity. It is therefore necessary to know that between good 
<margin>
Tyrannical
behavior.
</margin>
governance of the kingdom and tyrannical rule, there is this difference: the tyrant loves and seeks his own profit more than he cares for or desires the common utility of his subjects, and he strives toward this end so that he may keep his people subjugated and in servitude. And the good king or prince, on the 
<margin>
The good
of public 
liberty.
</margin>
contrary, puts the public utility before his own private and personal interest, and above all things—after God and his soul—he loves the well-being and public liberty of his subjects. And this manner of rule is the true utility and nobility of the lordship; for a lordship is that much more noble and better when it looks toward and brings more profit to its subjects, as <note>Oresme is likely referencing Aristotle’s *Politics*, where the philosopher defines a king as one who rules for the common good and a tyrant as one who rules for himself.</note> **Aristotle** says; and furthermore, the king or prince is destined to endure longer, insofar as he lives with such intention and purpose. To this point, <note>Flavius Magnus Aurelius Cassiodorus (c. 485 – c. 585) was a Roman statesman and writer; Oresme frequently cites him as an authority on statecraft.</note> **Cassiodorus** says: "The discipline of governing is to love that which is expedient and necessary for the many (1);"

---

<note>(1) "The discipline of governing is to love what is profitable for many." <note>original: "Disciplina imperandi est amare quod multis expedit."</note></note>

<page-num>LXXVII</page-num>

<header>— LXXVII —</header>

<margin>
Dangers of the transfer of nations.
</margin>

...for whenever a kingdom turns into a tyrannical government, it cannot for long afterward be guarded or defended; for by such acts <note>Oresme is referring to the "currency alterations" mentioned on the previous page as acts of tyranny.</note>, it prepares itself in every way for its own decline, <term>transfer: "translation"</term> <note>Derived from the Latin *translatio imperii*, this refers to the historical and theological concept of the "transfer of rule" from one nation or lineage to another as a result of divine judgment or moral decay.</note>, or ruin. This is especially true in a temperate region, far from foreign rule, where there are men of noble character and free and frank customs who are not serfs; men who, by long custom, are neither hardened to nor accustomed to being governed by tyranny. For such people, servitude would be disadvantageous, involuntary, and oppressive, and consequently, violent—and therefore not lasting. As **Aristotle** says, things of a violent nature soon perish. For this reason **Tully** <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, often called "Tully" by medieval and early modern scholars.</note> says that no force or power of an empire is so great that, when held in fear or dread, it can be of long duration (1). And **Seneca**, in his tragedies, says: *No violent lordship is free from fear, but moderate rule lasts long* (2). Wherefore, after the princes were deposed, Our Lord rebuked and reproached them through the Prophet, saying that they commanded and ruled with severity, and for this reason their reign could not have long duration (3) <note>This is a reference to the Book of Ezekiel 34:4: "with force and with cruelty have ye ruled them."</note>. Further to this point, **Plutarch** said to the Emperor **Trajan** that the <term>republic: "chose publicque"</term> <note>Literally "the public thing" (*res publica*), referring to the state or the commonwealth.</note> is a body which, in the likeness of divine providence and by...

---

(1) <note>original: "Quod nulla vis imperii tanta est, quod premente metu possit esse diuturna."</note> "Because no power of empire is so great that it can be lasting while fear weighs upon it."
(2) <note>original: "Violenta nemo imperia continuat diu, Moderata durant."</note> "No one continues violent commands for long; moderate ones endure."
(3) <note>original: "Quod imperabant subditis cum austeritate et potentia."</note> "Because they ruled their subjects with austerity and power."

<meta>This page continues the argument from page LXXVII, concluding a sentence regarding the nature of the commonwealth as a living body and moving into a physiological analogy for economic inequality within a state. Nicole Oresme uses the "Body Politic" metaphor to argue that a ruler who hoards wealth at the expense of his subjects creates an unsustainable and "monstrous" imbalance.</meta>

<page-num>LXXVIII</page-num>

...its well-being is animated and brought about by sovereign equity and is governed by a certain moderation of reason (1); thus, therefore, the <term>commonwealth: "chose publicque"</term> <note>Oresme uses "chose publicque," a literal translation of the Latin *res publica*, to refer to the state or the collective interest of the people.</note> or kingdom is just like a human body, and so **Aristotle** maintains in the fifth book of the *Politics*; and just as the body is poorly disposed when excessive <term>humors: "humeurs"</term> <note>In medieval medicine, the body was thought to be governed by four "humors" (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile). Health required a balance; an excess of any one humor led to disease or swelling.</note> overabound in one true and vital member of it, to such an extent that the said member is often inflamed and too greatly enlarged by this, while the others remains dry and withered, for such a body cannot live for long, except by due and equal proportion. And thus similarly it can be said of the community or kingdom, when riches are attracted and accumulated beyond and above good <term>governance: "polyce"</term> <note>From the Greek *politeia*, referring to the organized system of government and social order.</note> on one side, and by him who lords and dominates there. And the comparison is easy enough to make, for when the prince—or anyone of whatever vocation or dignity he may be—wishes to draw to himself and in fact succeeds in gathering in great multitude, above his subjects or his peers and to their prejudice, many riches, he is like a monster in nature, just like a body whose head is so large that the rest of it is so weak that it cannot support it. Thus, just as such a man cannot help himself nor live for long, similarly the community or kingdom from which the prince draws to himself...

---

(1) <note>original: "Respublica est corpus quod divini numinis instar beneficio animatur, et summæ æquitatis agitur nutu, et regitur quodam moderamine rationis."</note> "The commonwealth is a body which, like the divine power, is animated by benefit, is moved by the will of supreme equity, and is governed by a certain moderation of reason." <note>This Latin quote is attributed to the *Policraticus* by John of Salisbury, a major influence on medieval political thought.</note>

<summary>Oresme employs the "Body Politic" metaphor to argue that a ruler who hoards excessive wealth at the expense of his subjects creates a "monstrous" and unstable state, much like a body with an overgrown, heavy head that the limbs cannot support.</summary>

<page-num>LXXIX</page-num>

...excessive riches, such as through the debasement of currency <note>original: "mutacions de monnoies"; Oresme was a famous critic of kings who changed the gold or silver content of coins to profit themselves at the expense of the people.</note>, salt taxes <term>gabelles: "gabelles"</term> <note>The gabelle was a much-hated tax on salt in medieval and early modern France.</note>, and other such exactions, it cannot long endure in prosperity, as it appears in the twentieth chapter; furthermore, just as in the mixing of voices too great an inequality neither pleases nor delights, and destroys and spoils all harmony <note>original: "consonance"</note>, but rather a proportionate and measured equality is required—for voices well-proportioned create joyful modulations or resonances or choirs; so therefore, universally, as for all the parts of the community, inequality of possessions and powers does not belong to nor harmonize within it; rather, too great a disparity dissipates and corrupts the harmony and sweetness of the <term>commonwealth: "chose publicque"</term>, as it appears from **Aristotle**, in the fifth book of the *Politics*.

For even stronger reason, then, that prince who is in the kingdom like the <term>tenor: "teneur"</term> <note>In medieval music, the "tenor" (from the Latin *tenere*, "to hold") was the fundamental voice that held the melody around which other voices harmonized.</note> and the principal voice (1) is so great that he exceeds all measure of greatness, and by this he is out of tune with the community through his avarice and accumulation of riches beyond measure, then the sweet melody of the royal <term>governance: "police"</term> <note>From the Greek *politeia*, meaning the organized system and health of the state.</note> will be troubled. For which reason, **Aristotle** says there is yet another difference between the king and the tyrant: for the tyrant wishes to be the most powerful by force over the entire community over which he presides; but the king and good prince is so <term>tempered: "attrempé"</term> <note>Meaning moderate or balanced in character and exercise of power.</note> that he is truly the most powerful of all his subjects, and yet he is in the midst of them, established as the least...

(1) <note>original Latin: "Qui est in regno ut tenor et vox principalis in cantu."</note> "Who is in the kingdom like the tenor and principal voice in a song."

<summary>This page uses a musical analogy to argue that economic inequality, specifically royal greed and currency manipulation, destroys the "harmony" of the state. Oresme distinguishes a king from a tyrant by the former's willingness to remain a balanced part of the community rather than a dominant force above it.</summary>

<page-num>LXXX</page-num>

...between that community of works and forces (1); and because royal power commonly and easily tends toward high and lofty things <note>Oresme is warning that power naturally seeks to expand itself upward toward absolute control.</note> (2), for this reason it is necessary to add and apply great caution and vigilant guard—indeed, the very highest and most principal prudence is required to preserve that power, so that it does not slip or fall into tyranny, as **Aristotle** says, and primarily because of the deceits and deceptions of adulators and flatterers who always incite princes to fall into tyranny, as **Aristotle** says. They are such as is said in the book of **Esther** <note>This likely refers to the additions to the Book of Esther (found in the Vulgate or Apocrypha), where King Ahasuerus describes how flatterers mislead even well-intentioned rulers.</note>, flatterers deceive the simple ears of princes; judging all others to be of their same [deceptive] natures, through their deceitful frauds and through their suggestions they worsen and ruin the states of princes and kings. But because avoiding and uprooting them is a difficult thing, that same **Aristotle** gives another rule by which the kingdom can be long preserved, which is that the prince does not expand (3) his <term>lordship: "seigneurie"</term> <note>Meaning his personal power and dominion over the lives and properties of his people.</note> too much over his subjects, that he does not commit <term>exactions: "exactions"</term> <note>Forced taxes or seizures of property without legal grounds.</note> or any seizures and detentions of them, outside the terms of justice, and that the law wills that he leaves them their <term>liberties: "franchises"</term> <note>In the Middle Ages, "franchises" were specific legal rights, exemptions, or privileges granted to cities, guilds, or individuals.</note>, if they have them; and if they do not...

---

(1) <note>original: "Regis vero temperantia est tali moderamine temperata, quod ipse est major atque potentior, quam aliquis ejus subditus, et tamen tota ipsa communitate inferior viribus et opibus, et sic in medio constitutus."</note> Indeed, the temperance of a king is tempered by such restraint that he himself is greater and more powerful than any one of his subjects, yet inferior in strength and wealth to the entire community as a whole, and thus established in the middle.

(2) <note>original: "Potestas regia communiter et leviter tendit in majus."</note> Royal power commonly and easily tends toward the greater.

(3) <note>original: "Non multum amplificet."</note> Let him not amplify [his power] much.

<summary>Oresme argues that a stable monarchy requires the king to remain "in the middle"—stronger than any individual but weaker than the whole community—while warning against the dangerous influence of flatterers who drive rulers toward tyranny.</summary>

->— LXXXI —<-
6

[...the subjects] have, that he grants them their due, and does not hinder them in it; likewise, that he does not use his <term>plenary power</term> <note>The concept of "plenitudo potestatis," or absolute, unrestricted authority.</note> but only that which is limited and regulated for him by true laws and good customs. Few things, says **Aristotle**, are to be left to the discretion of the judge or the Prince.

<margin>Example.</margin>
**Aristotle** also brings forward an example of **Theopompus**, king of **Lacedaemonia** <note>Also known as Sparta.</note>, who, when he had come into his <term>lordship</term> <note>His reign or dominion.</note>, abandoned and waived for his subjects several tributes and <term>exactions</term> <note>Forced taxes or demands for money.</note> that his predecessors had imposed upon them. Because of this, his wife wept bitterly and reproached him, saying it was a great shame and <term>pusillanimity</term> <note>A lack of courage or "smallness of soul."</note> for a son to hold the kingdom left to him by his father with less profit and revenue than he had received from his father. The good king, in just a few words, replied with a reason: "I make my kingdom perpetual" (1). O divine oracle! Oh, of what great weight was this word, and worthy of being painted in the halls of princes and kings in letters of fine gold! "I make my reign perpetual!" as if he meant to say: "I have increased my kingdom more through its duration in time than it was diminished by the moderation of my power."

Certainly, here is a greater word than that of **Solomon**; for if **Rehoboam** <note>The son of Solomon whose refusal to lighten the tax burden led to the rebellion of the ten tribes.</note>, of whom mention was made above, had received the kingdom from his father **Solomon** thus composed, and had maintained it in this manner, he would never have lost the tribes of **Israel** as he did; and he would never have been reproached with what is said—

---

(1) <note>original Latin: "Trado diuturnius."</note> "I hand it over to last longer."

->— LXXXIII —<-

### *The twenty-sixth and final chapter is that taking profit through the <term>alteration of currency</term> <note>original: "mutacion de monnoie"; refers to the practice of changing the weight or fineness of coins to generate revenue for the crown, often seen as a hidden tax.</note> is prejudicial to all royal <term>posterity</term> <note>Future generations or descendants.</note>.*

<margin>The transformation of the kingdom.</margin>
It is my intention to declare that the aforementioned alterations are against the King's honor and are prejudicial to the royal succession, as well as to his children, for which reason I put forward three points: the first is that this thing is most <term>shameful</term> <note>original: "vitupérable"; deserving of strong condemnation.</note> in a king and most prejudicial to his successors, through which the kingdom is disposed toward utter <term>perdition</term> <note>Ruin or destruction.</note> or risks being transferred to foreigners. And the king could not grieve enough nor weep, being so unfortunate and so miserable, if through his negligence or through his bad governance, such a thing should occur, by which he and all others would lose possession of the kingdom increased by the noble virtues of his predecessors, who for a long time had graciously protected it. Nor would it be a glorious thing for him, nor without the peril of his soul, if by the failing of his people, he suffered so many plagues, so many calamities and miseries of the sort that usually occur in the dismantling and transfer of kingdoms.

The second point: I presuppose that by <term>tyranny</term> <note>original: "tyrannisation"; the exercise of power without regard for justice or the common good.</note> one exposes the kingdom to ruin, just as it was declared in the preceding chapter. And because it is written in **Ecclesiasticus** <note>The Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 10:8: "A kingdom is translated from one people to another, because of injustices, and wrongs, and injuries, and divers deceits."</note>, that the kingdom is transferred...

<summary>This concluding chapter argues that debasing currency shames the king and endangers his dynasty’s future by inviting ruin or foreign conquest. It asserts that such economic injustice constitutes a form of tyranny that, according to scripture, causes a kingdom to be taken from one lineage and given to another.</summary>

<keywords>currency alteration, royal succession, tyranny, Ecclesiasticus, political ruin, kingly honor, governance, transfer of kingdoms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the instability of kingdoms that resort to tyranny. <page-num>LXXXIV</page-num>
<header>— LXXXIV —</header>

...and is transferred from nation to nation and from people to people, because of their injustices and injuries, insults and various frauds <note>original Latin: "Tyrannis autem injuriosa est et injusta"; Tyranny, however, is injurious and unjust.</note>. And furthermore, to move to more specific matters—and God forbid—that the free hearts of the French should ever be so corrupted that they would willingly be made slaves; therefore, a servitude imposed upon them could not long endure. For however great the power of tyrants may be, it is always a violation of the hearts of the free-born children of the subjects, and it is powerless against foreigners <note>original Latin: "Ideoque servitus eis imposita durare non potest. Quoniam, etsi magna sit tyrannorum potentia, est tamen liberis subditorum cordibus violenta, et adversus alienos invalida"; Therefore the servitude imposed upon them cannot last. Because, even if the power of tyrants be great, it is nonetheless a violation of the free hearts of the subjects, and invalid against strangers.</note>. Whoever, therefore, would try in any way to draw and induce the lords of France toward this tyrannical regime would certainly expose the kingdom to great disrepute and shame, and would prepare it for its end; for never did the most noble <term>lineage</term> <note>original: "sequelle"; the line of succession or descendants.</note> of the kings of France learn to tyrannize, nor is the <term>Gallic people</term> <note>original: "peuple gallican"; a term for the French, evoking their ancient roots and character.</note> accustomed to servile subjection; and for this reason, if the royal lineage of France departs from its original virtue, without any doubt, it will lose its kingdom and it will be transferred into another hand.

The third point I propose is this, which has already been sufficiently proven and often repeated: that taking or increasing one’s profit or <term>domain</term> <note>original: "demaine"; the lands and revenues held directly by the king.</note> through the <term>alterations of currency</term> <note>original: "mutations des monnoies"; the practice of changing the precious metal content or face value of coins to create a profit for the crown, a major theme of this treatise.</note> is an act full of tyranny and injustice, and

...along with this, which could not continue in the kingdom, nor in any kingdom whatsoever, if it were not in a country accustomed to being governed in <term>tyranny</term> <note>A form of government where the ruler exercises absolute power oppressively, often prioritizing personal gain over the common good.</note>. Great evils and inconveniences follow and arise because of these <term>mutations</term> <note>original: "mutacions des monnoies"; the practice of changing the weight or metal content of coins, commonly known as currency debasement.</note>, as has been said; but even more, it is certain that other evils must precede those that accompany them afterward. For such fraud, and, if I dare say, <term>larceny</term> <note>original: "larcin"; theft. The author uses this harsh legal term to argue that a king who arbitrarily changes the value of money is essentially stealing from his subjects.</note>, could not be advised by men who were not in their thoughts and intentions corrupted, and ready to advise all manner of frauds and tyrannical perversities wherever they see the prince bending and inclining—as we have seen enough for a short time now, for lack of a leader. 

I say then, by way of a summary, that the thing by which the kingdom is disposed toward <term>perdition</term> <note>original: "perdicion"; total ruin or destruction.</note> is ugly and prejudicial to the king and all his heirs and successors; and this thing is understood as governing his subjects tyrannically and by exacting and taking their substances through the mutations of currencies or otherwise. Likewise, such mutations and exactions are against the honor of all the <term>royal posterity</term> <note>The future generations and descendants of the royal bloodline.</note>, and very prejudicial, as has been sufficiently proven above.

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that currency debasement is a form of tyrannical theft that leads a kingdom to ruin. He asserts that such practices are advised only by corrupt officials who exploit weak leadership, ultimately dishonoring the king's descendants.</summary>

<keywords>tyranny, currency debasement, Nicole Oresme, corruption, royal honor, political ethics, larceny, perdition</keywords>

<page-num>LXXXVI</page-num>

## Conclusion of the Translator.

The matters set forth above are stated without <term>assertion</term> <note>original: "assertion"; in a medieval scholastic context, this refers to making a definitive, dogmatic claim. The author is presenting his findings for discussion rather than as absolute law.</note> or dogmatic affirmation, and are subject to the correction of wise and prudent men—and especially of you, my very dear and honored lord, who are well-versed and expert in most of these matters. For as **Aristotle** says, <term>civil affairs</term> <note>original: "besongnes civilles"; matters pertaining to the governance of the state and the conduct of citizens.</note> are more often than not doubtful and uncertain. Therefore, if anyone, for the love of seeking the truth, should wish to contradict these points or write against them, it shall be well; but if I have spoken wrongly, let him bear witness to the wrong with reason, so that he may not be seen as rashly and out of his own private will condemning for nothing that which cannot easily be assailed or contradicted.

->*Here ends the treatise on the alterations of currencies, produced by Master Nicholas Oresme, professor of Holy Scripture.*<-
<note>original: "Finis tractatus de mutationibus monetarum a magistro Nycholao Oresme, sacre pagine professore editus." Nicholas Oresme (c. 1320–1382) was one of the most famous thinkers of the 14th century; his title "professor of Holy Scripture" indicates he was a Doctor of Theology.</note>

<summary>In this concluding passage, the translator humbles himself before his patron and other scholars, acknowledging the inherent uncertainty of political science while defending his work against those who would criticize it without sound reason.</summary>

<meta>This page follows the conclusion of Oresme's treatise. Having finished the translation of the text itself, the translator now provides a bibliographical preface explaining the sources used for this edition, comparing various historical printings of the Latin original and providing biographical context for Nicholas Oresme.</meta>

We believe it useful to add to this publication of the translation of the *Treatise on Currencies*, made by the author, the original Latin text of the work by Nicole Oresme.

It was printed for the first time, as we have mentioned in our work, by Thomas Keet, in Paris, at the beginning of the sixteenth century. It was not possible for us to obtain this very rare volume, but we had at our disposal the copy of the *Small Works on Currencies* <note>original: "Opusc. de monetis"</note>, which contains the edition published in Lyon, in 1605, by Gothard Voegelin. This edition, although faulty and incomplete, is much superior to the text included in the *Sacred Library of the Holy Fathers* <note>original: "SACRA BIBLIOTHECA SANCTORUM PATRUM"</note> by *Margarinus de la Bigne* (Paris, 1589, vol. IX, p. 1291), and reproduced in the *Great Library of the Ancient Fathers* <note>original: "MAX. BIBLIOTH. VETERUM PATRUM"</note>, *first brought to light by Margarinus de la Bigne* <note>original: "primò quidem a Margarino de la Bigne in lucem edita"</note> (1697, Lyon, published by the Anissons). vol. XXVI, p. 226 (1). The order of the chapters in these latter editions reveals, better than Voegelin's edition, the main gap in the printed text; for after chapter XXII comes chapter XXVI immediately, instead of chapter XXIII, which concludes Voegelin's copy.

Mr. Roscher speaks of the reprint, even more faulty,
