In order to provide a more accurate reading, we have compared the volume by Voegelin (which we designate as *V*) with the best manuscripts from the Imperial Library (1).

These manuscripts, which served for the current edition, are three in number; we designate them as *A B C*.

**A.** Imperial Library, Latin collection from Saint-Germain, no. 1103. — 1st Folio 1, *Treatise on the Alteration of Currencies* <note>original: "Tractatus de mutatione monetarum"</note>; — 2nd folio 27, *On certain prefaces and their continuation and adaptation to any spiritual matters* <note>original: "De quibusdam exordiis et eorum continuacione et adaptacione ad quascumque materias spirituales"</note>; — 3rd folio 50 recto, *On certain legal maxims and rules of law which might sometimes be accepted as prefaces, or adapted in exemplary writing* <note>original: "De quibusdam brocardicis et regulis juris que interdum vel pro exordiis possent accipi, vel in exemplari dictamine adaptari"</note> <term>brocardic: a legal maxim or fundamental principle of law, often written in verse to aid memorization</term>; — 4th folio 66 recto, *Common sayings or proverbs matched with authors of the Bible or the Saints* <note>original: "Dicta sive proverbia vulgaria concordata auctoribus Biblie vel Sanctorum"</note>. This entire manuscript is written by the same hand; it is dated by a note placed after the Treatise by Nicole Oresme, which reads: *The titles end here, thanks be to God, in the year of our Lord 1397* <note>original: "Expliciunt tytuli, Deo gracias, anno Domini 1397"</note>.

**B.** Imperial Library, Carmelites of Maubert Square, no. 10, on paper, unpaginated, handwriting from around 1400, contains: 1st *Hugo of Balma's Treatise on the threefold life leading to wisdom* <note>original: "Hugonis de Balma Tractatus de triplici vita ad sapientiam"</note> (also attributed to J. Gerson); — 2nd *J. Gerson, Treatise on prayer* <note>original: "J. Gerson, Tractatus de oratione"</note>; — 3rd *Hugo of Saint Victor, Treatise on prayer* <note>original: "Hugonis de S. Victore Tractatus de oratione"</note>; *J. Gerson, Treatise in praise of writers of healthy doctrine* <note>original: "J. Gerson, Tractatus de laude scriptorum doctrine salubris"</note>; — 4th *Another Treatise beginning: Do not pay attention, my brothers* <note>original: "Alius Tractatus incipiens : Nolite attendere fratres mei"</note>; — 5th *Nic. Oresme, Treatise on currencies* <note>original: "Nic. Oresme, Tractatus de monetis"</note>; — 6th *J. Gerson, On contracts* <note>original: "J. Gerson, De contractibus"</note>; — 7th *The same author, On the passions* <note>original: "Ejusdem De passionibus"</note>; — 8th *An account of various sects* <note>original: "Narratio diversarum sectarum"</note>; — 9th *Albertano's Doctrine of speaking and keeping silent* <note>original: "Albertani Doctrina dicendi et tacendi"</note> (2).

**C.** Imperial Library, Latin collection 8733, A. A fifteenth-century manuscript, containing only the Treatise by Nicole Oresme. A miniature placed on the first folio of this volume, executed with great perfection, represents a minting workshop. Among the ornaments serving as its frame, one can distinguish the motto: *There is more in you* <note>original: "Plus est en vous"</note>, two bombards <term>bombard: an early type of large-caliber cannon used in the 14th and 15th centuries</term> and the fleur-de-lis shield; the motto and the bombards show that this volume was part of the library of the Lord of Gruthuyse; the royal arms were added later, when the books of this wealthy collector entered the King’s library under Louis XII.

# HERE BEGINS THE TREATISE ON THE ORIGIN, NATURE, LAW, AND ALTERATIONS OF MONEY, COMPOSED BY MASTER NICOLE ORESME, PROFESSOR OF SACRED THEOLOGY (1).

## Prologue.

To some it appears that any king or prince, by his own authority, may by right or (2) privilege freely change (3) the coins current in his kingdom, and dispose of them (4) at his pleasure, and take from this as much profit or gain (5) as he likes (6): to others, however, the opposite appears true. For this reason, I intend in the present treatise to write about this matter, <note>original: "lucrum seu emolumentum"; in this context, Oresme is discussing the "seigniorage" or the profit a ruler takes from minting or devaluing coins.</note>

(1) *This opening title is provided by manuscript B; manuscript C begins: "Treatise on the origin and nature, law and alterations of money." Manuscript C further contains this title written in the seventeenth century:*

->Law and Alterations<-
->of Money.<-

what (1) according to the philosophy (2) of Aristotle <note>Aristotle’s *Politics* and *Nicomachean Ethics* provided the medieval world with its primary definitions of money as a tool for exchange rather than a commodity for profit.</note> it seems to me should principally be said, beginning from the origin of money: asserting nothing rashly, but submitting all (3) to the correction of my superiors, who perhaps from these things I am about to say, will be able to be stirred to determine the truth upon this matter, so that, all doubt ceasing, everyone may together agree upon one opinion, and regarding this, find what may benefit princes and subjects, indeed the whole commonwealth, in the future.

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening argument of Chapter I. The previous page established that as human populations grew and lands were divided, a natural imbalance occurred where individuals possessed surpluses of some goods but lacked others. This page explains the transition from direct barter to the invention of "artificial wealth" or money.</meta>

-- XCIV --

little or nothing of that same thing; and regarding another thing, the opposite was true, just as perhaps someone abounded in sheep but lacked bread, and the farmer conversely <note>The farmer had bread but lacked sheep.</note>. Also, one region had an overabundance of one thing and a deficiency in another. Therefore, men began to trade without money, and one would give another a sheep for grain, and another gave his labor for bread or wool (1), and so it was with other things. This practice was still established in certain states for a long time afterward (2), as (3) Justin <note>Marcus Junianus Justinus, a 2nd-century Roman historian.</note> relates. But nevertheless, in this kind of exchange and transportation of goods, many difficulties arose. Men used their ingenuity to invent the use of money (4), which would be an instrument for exchanging natural riches with one another, through which human necessity is naturally assisted. For money itself is called **artificial wealth**: indeed, it has happened that someone with an abundance (5) of it died of hunger, as Aristotle gives the example of the greedy king who prayed that whatever he touched might turn to gold; the Gods granted this, and thus he perished from hunger, as the poets say <note>This refers to the myth of King Midas.</note>; since through money the necessities of life are not immediately assisted, but it is an instrument artificially devised for the easier (6) exchange of natural wealth. And without further proof, it can clearly be seen that coinage is very useful for a good civil community and advantageous for the uses of the State, or rather necessary: as Aristotle proves (7) in the fifth book of the *Ethics*. Although Ovid says of this:

<margin>Metamorphoses, I, 140.</margin>

> Wealth is dug out of the earth, the incitement to evils,
> And now harmful iron, and gold more harmful than iron,
> Had come forth, etc. <note>original Latin: "Effodiuntur opes irritamenta malorum, / Jamque nocens ferrum ferroque nocentius aurum / Prodierat, etc."</note>

For the perverse greed of wicked men causes this, not the money it-

<summary>Oresme explains that money was invented to solve the logistical difficulties of bartering goods and labor, distinguishing between "natural wealth" (food, clothing) and "artificial wealth" (money). He cites Aristotle and Ovid to warn that while money is a necessary tool for the State, it can lead to greed and ruin if its nature is misunderstood.</summary>

And in another place he says that (4) it is established that minters <term>monetarios: those who strike or create coins</term> were specifically created for the public use.*

### Chapter II.

->Of what material money should be made.<-

And since money is an instrument for exchanging natural wealth, as is clear from the preceding chapter, it was fitting that such an instrument be suitable (5). This is achieved if it is easily handled (6) or felt by the hands, and easily portable, and if for a small portion of it (7) one may obtain natural wealth in a greater quantity, along with other conditions that will be seen later. It was necessary, therefore, that the coin be made of a precious and rare material (8), such as gold. But there must be a sufficient abundance (9) of such material. For this reason, where gold is not enough, money is also made from silver; where these two metals are not enough or are not available, a mixture (10) must be made, or a simple coin of another pure metal; just as in ancient times it was made of bronze <term>aes: a term for copper or its alloys, like bronze or brass</term>, as Ovid tells in the first book of the *Fasti*, saying:

<margin>Fasti, I, 220.</margin>
> Once they gave bronze, but now a better omen is in gold,
> And the ancient coinage, conquered, gives way to the new.

The Lord also promised a similar change through Isaiah <margin>Isaiah 60:17</margin> <note>The biblical passage refers to God replacing bronze with gold and iron with silver.</note>.

It cites historical and biblical shifts from bronze to gold to justify the use of precious metals.</summary>

-- XCVI --

<margin>Cassiodorus, Book IV, chapter 34.</margin>
...the prophet (1) says: *Instead of bronze I will bring gold, and instead of iron I will bring silver.* <note>original: "Pro ære afferam aurum, et pro ferro afferam argentum." This is a quote from the Book of Isaiah 60:17.</note> These metals are indeed the most suitable for money. And, as Cassiodorus says, *Aeacus is said to have first discovered gold, and Indus, the King of Scythia, to have discovered silver (2) and to have handed them over for human use with the highest praise.* Therefore, it should not be permitted that so much of these metals be applied to other uses that the remainder is insufficient for coinage. Theodoric, the King of Italy <note>Theodoric the Great (r. 493–526 AD), ruler of the Ostrogothic Kingdom.</note>, rightly noticing this, ordered that the gold and silver which had been buried in the tombs of the dead according to the custom of the pagans be removed, and he had it brought to the use of the mint for the public benefit, saying:

> *It is a kind of crime to leave uselessly in the hidden places of the dead that which can sustain the lives of the living.*

Conversely, it is not helpful for a state that such material be too abundant; for this reason, bronze money fell out of use, as Ovid says. Perhaps it has also been (3) provided for the human race that gold and silver, which are most suitable for this purpose, are not easily found in great plenty, and that they cannot be easily (4) made through alchemy <term>alchimia: the medieval precursor to chemistry, which often sought to transmute base metals into gold</term>, as some attempt to do; nature herself, so to speak, rightly opposes them, as they strive in vain to exceed her works.

And thus, most conveniently, where silver is not abundant, there are three materials for coins: first gold, second silver, and third the mixed black money. But it must be observed (5) and noted as a general rule that a mixture <note>This refers to the practice of alloying or debasing metals.</note> should never be made, except only in the case of the less precious metal from which small money is usually made. For example, where there is currency made of gold and silver, a mixture should never be made in the gold currency, provided the gold is of such a nature that it can be minted without admixture. The reason is that every such mixture is inherently suspect, and the substance of the gold and its quantity cannot be easily recognized in a mixture. Therefore, no mixture should be made in coins except for the necessity already mentioned; and then it should be made where the suspicion is less or where the deception is smaller, and this is in the less precious metal (6). Furthermore, no such mixture should be made except solely for the common utility...

### *Chapter IV.*

### *On the form or figure of money.*

When men first began to trade or acquire wealth by means of money, there was not yet any impression or image upon it; rather, a portion of silver or bronze was given for drink or food, and this portion was measured by weight. And since it was tedious to resort so frequently to the scales <term>trutina: a balance or pair of scales used for weighing precious metals</term>, and money could not easily be matched to transactions (2) by weight alone; and since, moreover, in most cases the seller (3) could not know the substance of the metal or the manner of its mixture, it was therefore wisely (4) provided by the sages of that time that portions of money should be made of a certain material and a determined weight, and that (5) a figure should be imprinted upon them which, known to all (6), would signify the quality of the material of the coin <term>numisma: a piece of currency or coin, from which we get the word 'numismatics'</term> and the truth of its weight, so that, with suspicion removed, the value of the money could be known without labor. That such an impression was established as a messenger and a sign of the truth of the material and weight is shown to us by the ancient names of coins...

And for this reason, not every precious thing (6) is suitable to be made into a coin: for gems, lapis lazuli (7), pepper, and such things are not naturally suited for this, but especially gold and silver, as was touched upon above.

## Chapter VI.

### To whom the money itself belongs.

Although for the common utility (3) the prince has the duty to stamp the coinage <term>numisma: a piece of currency or stamped coin</term>, he is not, however, the lord or owner of the money circulating in his principality. For money is an equivalent instrument for exchanging natural riches <note>tangible goods such as food or materials, as opposed to artificial wealth like coins</note>, as is clear from the first chapter. Therefore, the money is the possession of those (4) to whom such riches belong. For if someone gives their bread or the labor of their own body for money, when they receive it (5), that money is theirs, just as the bread or the labor of the body was theirs, which was in their own free power—assuming that they are not a slave. For from the beginning, God did not give the freedom of ownership of things to princes alone (6), but to our first parents <note>Adam and Eve</note> and to all their posterity, as is stated in Genesis. Therefore, money does not belong to the prince alone. If someone, however, should wish...

<summary>Nicole Oresme argues that while the prince has the authority to mint and protect currency against counterfeiting, the money itself belongs to the people who earned it through labor or trade. He grounds this right to private property in the biblical book of Genesis, asserting that ownership was granted to all humanity, not just rulers.</summary>

Therefore, the money belongs to the community and to individual persons; and so says Aristotle in the seventh book of the *Politics*, and Cicero <note>referred to as Tullius</note> near the end of his *Old Rhetoric*.

### Chapter VII.

This is achieved by a method where the material to be minted—such as gold when it is handed over to be coined or sold for money—is exchanged for a lesser amount of currency than can be made (7) from it under a certain fixed price (8). And if such a portion or pension were excessive, this would result in the damage and prejudice of the whole community, as can be easily apparent to anyone.

## Chapter VIII.

### On the changes of money in general.

Before all else, it must be known that the prior laws, statutes, customs, or ordinances of any kind touching the community must never be changed without evident necessity. Indeed, according to <term>Aristotle</term> in the second book of the <term>Politics</term>, an old positive law is not to be abolished in favor of a better new one, unless there is a very notable difference in their quality; for such changes diminish the authority and reverence of the laws themselves, and much more so if they are made frequently. Now, however, it is the case that the circulation and price (2) of <term>currencies: "monetarum," the actual coins in circulation</term> in the kingdom ought to be like a certain law and a firm ordinance. The sign of this is that (3) pensions and certain annual incomes are fixed according to the price of money, namely to a certain number of <term>pounds: "librarum"</term> or <term>shillings: "solidorum"</term>. From this it is clear that a change of money ought never to be made, unless perhaps (4) a necessity should arise, or (5) an evident benefit for the whole community. Whence <term>Aristotle</term>, in the fifth book of the *Ethics*, speaking of coinage, says: *Nevertheless, it tends to remain more stable* <note>original: "Verumtamen... vult manere magis"</note>. A change of money, however (as I can determine in general), can be envisioned to occur in many ways: first, in the form or shape precisely; second, in (6) the proportion; third, in the price or name (7); fourth, in the quantity or weight; and fifth, in the substance of the material. For money can be changed by any (8) of these five ways individually or by several together. It is good, therefore, to clarify these ways by discussing them, and to inquire through reason if money can be justly changed by any of them, and when, and by whom, and how, and for what reason.

### Chapter X.

#### *Concerning the change in the ratio of currencies.*

Proportion is the comparison or relationship of one thing to another: just as in the matter at hand, there ought to be a fixed relationship in value and price between gold money and silver money. For since gold is by its nature more precious and rarer than silver, and more difficult to find or possess, gold itself of equal weight ought to be worth more in a certain proportion (1) <note>original Latin: "In certa proportione," a phrase omitted in manuscript version V.</note>; such as perhaps a ratio of twenty to one, so that one pound of gold would be worth twenty pounds of silver, one <term>mark: "marcha," a traditional unit of weight for precious metals, usually about 8 ounces</term> would be worth 20 marks, one ounce would be worth 20 ounces, and so on consistently. It is also possible for there to be another ratio, such as perhaps twenty-five to three, or any other. Nevertheless, this proportion should follow the natural relationship of gold to silver (2) <note>Version A reads: "of gold and silver."</note> in its preciousness, and according to this, such a proportion is to be established. It is not permitted to change this ratio arbitrarily, nor can it be justly (3) <note>Version V reads: "by right."</note> varied except for a real cause (4) <note>Versions A and C read: "rational cause."</note> and a variation on the part of the material itself, which nevertheless (5) <note>Version V reads: "because."</note> rarely happens. For instance, if significantly less gold were to be found (6) <note>Version A reads: "is found."</note> than before, then it would be necessary for it to be more expensive in comparison (7) <note>Version V reads: "in proportion."</note> to silver, and for its price and value to be changed. If little or nothing has changed in reality, then this could in no way be permitted to the prince. For if he were to change such a proportion at his whim, he could by this means draw to himself un- <note>The text breaks off here; the Latin "inde-" likely begins the word "indebite," meaning "undeservedly" or "unjustly."</note>

---

(1) In a certain proportion, omitted in V. — (2) A: of gold and silver. — (3) V: by right. — (4) A, C: rational. — (5) V: cause. — (6) A: is found. — (7) V: proportion.

<summary>This page defines the economic concept of the "proportion" or exchange ratio between gold and silver, arguing that it must be based on the natural scarcity of the metals rather than the arbitrary will of a ruler. Oresme asserts that a prince may only change this ratio if the physical availability of the metals significantly shifts.</summary>

...wrote injustices, <note>original: *scripserunt, etc.*</note> and so on. <note>The manuscript version V reads: "and they shall write injustices."</note> Furthermore, from the first and sixth chapters it is sufficiently clear that the money belongs to the community itself. Therefore, so that the prince cannot maliciously devise a false reason for changing the ratio of the coins as assigned in the present chapter, it belongs to the community alone to decide <note>Version V uses the word "determine."</note> if, when, how, and for how long such a ratio is to be changed; the prince must not usurp this power for himself in any way.

### Chapter XI.

### *On the change of the name of the money <note>Version V reads "of the monies."</note>.*

As was stated in Chapter III, some names or necessary characteristics of coins are derived from their author or the place where they are made, and these are of little or no importance to our current purpose. But other names are more essential and specific to the <term>coinage</term> <note>original: *numismati*</note>, such as the <term>denarius</term> <note>originally a Roman silver coin; in this period, often a penny</note>, the <term>solidus</term> <note>originally a gold coin, used here as a shilling</note>, the <term>libra</term> <note>a pound</note>, and similar terms. These denote the value or the weight, and they were established by the ancients with profound consideration and great mystery. Concerning this, Cassiodorus <note>A 6th-century Roman statesman and author whose works were standard in medieval education</note> says:

> "It should be noted," he says, "with what great reason the very coins were organized by the ancients. They decreed that six thousand denarii should make a <term>solidus</term>, so that the shaped roundness of the gleaming metal might fittingly encompass the age of the world—like a golden sun. They used the name of the number six <note>Version B and C read "denarius" instead of "the number six."</note> (which learned antiquity rightly defined as a 'perfect number') to signify the <term>ounce</term> <note>original: *uncia*</note>, which is the first step of measurement; this they calculated twelve times, in the likeness of the months of the year, to gather into the fullness of a pound from the cycle of the year. O, the inventions of the wise!"

<summary>This page argues that the right to change currency ratios belongs to the community rather than the prince and introduces the symbolic history of monetary names like the denarius and solidus.</summary>
<keywords>sovereignty of the community, monetary ratios, denarius, solidus, pound, Cassiodorus, history of money, perfect numbers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XI, which explores the origins and significance of the names of money, specifically addressing why changing these names is a form of deception.</meta>

<page-num>CVIII</page-num>

*“O foresight of our ancestors! It is an exquisite thing, which both distinguishes the necessities for human use and figuratively contains so many secrets of nature. Rightly, therefore, is it called a **libra** <note>original: *libra*; a pound, referring both to weight and a unit of currency</note>, which has been weighed with such great consideration of things.” Thus says he [Cassiodorus].*

If, however, we use these names and coins in another way for now, they must nonetheless never be changed without cause. Let there be, therefore, for the sake of example, three types of **coinage** <term>numisma</term> <note>a general term for a piece of currency or a coin</note>: let the first be worth one **denarius** <note>original: *denarium*; a penny</note>, the second one **solidus** <note>original: *solidum*; a shilling</note>, and the third one **libra** <note>a pound</note>. (1) If, then, (2) the name of one is changed and not the others, the proportion will immediately vary. Just as if someone called or made the first coin worth two denarii while the others remained unchanged, the proportion would be altered. This is not permitted to happen (as is clear from the preceding chapter) except perhaps very rarely, and I am not concerned with that for the moment. 

It is necessary, therefore, if the proportion is to remain unchanged and one coin changes its (3) name, that the others also be changed proportionally: for example, if the first is called “two denarii,” the second should be called “two solidi,” and the third “two pounds.” But if no other change occurred <note>i.e., if the physical weight and metal content of the coins remained the same</note>, it would be necessary to buy or price goods at a higher price proportionally. Yet such a change of names (4) would be done in vain and should not be done, because it would be scandalous and the naming would be false. For that would be called a **libra** [pound] which in truth was not a **libra**; which is unfitting, as has just been said. 

Nevertheless, no other disadvantage would follow where there were no **pensions** <note>original: *pensiones*; fixed annual payments or salaries</note> or any **revenues** <term>reditus</term> <note>income from land, rents, or investments</note> assigned to a specific sum of money. But where there are such things, it is immediately obvious that—along with the aforementioned disadvantages—such revenues would be proportionally reduced by such a change, or would increase irrationally and unjustly, and even to the detriment of many. For where pensions or

## Capitulum XII.

### *On the change of the weight of coins.*

If the weight of a **coin** <term>numisma</term> <note>original: *numismatis*; referring to the physical piece of currency</note> were changed, and along with this the value was varied proportionally, as well as the name and the appearance, this would be to create another kind of money; just as if one were to make two **half-pennies** <note>original: *obolos*</note> out of one **penny** <note>original: *denario*</note>, or something similar, without loss or gain. And this could sometimes be done lawfully due to some real transformation in the **mintable material** <note>original: *materia monetabili*; the raw bullion used for coining</note>, which can only happen very rarely, as was said regarding a certain other change in Chapter X. Now, however, I wish to speak of the precise change of the weight or quantity of money which is made while the name and value remain unchanged. And it seems to me that such a change is simply unlawful, especially for the prince, who in no way can do this except basely and unjustly. For first, because the image or **inscription** <note>original: *superscriptio*</note> on the coin is placed there by the prince to signify the certainty of the weight and the quality of the material, as was shown above in Chapter III. Therefore, if the truth of the weight did not correspond, it is immediately clear that it would be a most vile falsity and a fraudulent deception. For often measures

## Chapter XIII.

### Concerning the change of the material of coins.

Either the material of the coinage is simple or mixed (3), as was made clear in Chapter Three. If it is simple, it can be abandoned due to a shortage: for example, if no gold or very little can be found, it is necessary (4) for it to cease being minted; and if it were found anew in sufficient abundance, one should begin to make money from it, as has sometimes been done. On the other hand, some material ought to cease being minted because of excessive abundance. For because of this, **copper coinage** <term>ærea moneta</term> long ago went out of use, as was said in that same Chapter Three. But causes of this kind happen very rarely, and in no other case should a pure or simple material for coins be abandoned or newly adopted. If, however, there is a mixture in such material, it ought to be done only in a less precious metal that is itself capable of being minted (as was proved in the same Chapter Three) and in **black money** <term>nigra moneta</term> <note>coins with a high copper content mixed with silver; they appear darker as the copper oxidizes</note>, so that the pure may be distinguished from the mixture. This (5) mixture, moreover, ought to be according to a fixed proportion, such as ten parts of silver against one, or against three of another me—

In no other case, however, should such a mixture or the proportion of the mixture be changed. Most importantly, this can never be permitted to the **Prince** <note>The sovereign or ruler</note>, for the reasons given (2) in the preceding chapter, which apply directly to this (3) purpose, because the impression on the coin is a sign of the truth of the material and of such a mixture; therefore, to change this would be to falsify the currency. Furthermore (4), on certain coins is inscribed (5) the name of God (6), or of some saint, and the sign of the cross; which was devised and established in ancient times as a testimony to the truth of the coin’s material and weight. If, therefore, the Prince changes the material or weight under this inscription, he himself seems to silently commit a lie and perjury, and (7) to bear false witness, and even to become a transgressor of that legal precept which says: *You shall not take the name of your God* (8) <note>original: *Non assumes nomen Dei tui* — a reference to the Commandment against taking the Lord's name in vain (Exodus 20:7)</note>

Just as sometimes specimens of other measures (6) are kept by the community.

A change of money (7) is **composite** when several simple changes are combined into one; for example, if one were to simultaneously change the proportion of the money, the mixture of the material, and even the weight. And thus (8) multiple combinations of the simple changes (9) described above would sometimes occur. And since no simple change ought—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "composite changes" to currency from Chapter XIV. The previous page defined a composite change as one where multiple aspects of the coinage (such as weight and purity) are altered at once.</meta>

<page-num>CXIV</page-num>

...occur, except for the real and natural causes already mentioned <note>Referring to the scarcity of metal or significant shifts in market value discussed in Chapter XIII</note>, which happen very rarely, it should be known that even more rarely—indeed, perhaps never—has there occurred a true occasion for making a **composite change** of money. And if by chance it should happen, for even stronger reasons than with a simple change, such a composite change should never be made by the Prince, because of the dangers and inconveniences previously touched upon, but by the community itself. For if so many abuses follow from simple changes improperly made, as was said before (1); much greater and worse (2) abuses would follow from a composite change (3). For money (4) ought to be true and just in substance and weight, which was signaled to us in Holy Scripture, where it is said of **Abraham** that he bought (5) a field, for which he gave 400 **shekels** <term>sicli: an ancient biblical unit of weight and currency</term> of silver of approved public money <margin>Genesis 23:16</margin>. If, therefore, the money were good and not improperly changed, since it is durable for a long time, there would be no need to manufacture much of it, nor to have many minters at the community's expense. And in this there would be common utility, as was touched upon in Chapter VII. Universally, therefore, it must be concluded from the premises that no change of money, whether simple or composite, is to be made by the Prince's authority alone; and especially where he might wish to do this for the sake of the gain and profit to be taken from such a change.

->*That the profit which comes to the prince from the change of money is unjust.*<-

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter XIV regarding the illegitimacy of "composite changes" to currency. It begins Chapter XV, which specifically addresses why the profit a ruler gains from altering money is morally and legally unjust.</meta>

<page-num>CXV</page-num>

...the power which the **Prince** <term>princeps: the sovereign or ruler</term> wishes to assume for himself of changing the **coinage** <term>monetas: the official metallic currency</term> is the benefit or profit that he can obtain from it; for otherwise he would make so many and such great changes in vain. I wish, therefore (1), to show even more fully that such an acquisition (2) is **unjust**. For every change of money—except in the rarest (3) cases mentioned before—involves falsity and deception, and cannot belong to the Prince, as was proven earlier. Since, therefore, the Prince usurps this matter (4), which is unjust in itself, it is impossible that he should take profit from it justly. Furthermore, for as much profit as the Prince takes from it, it is necessary that the community itself suffer as much loss. But whatever the Prince does to the detriment of the community is an injustice and a **tyrannical act**, not a royal one, as **Aristotle** <note>Oresme frequently cites Aristotle's *Politics* to distinguish between a King (who rules for the common good) and a Tyrant (who rules for private gain).</note> says. And if he were to say, as tyrants are accustomed to lie, that he converts such profit to public utility, he should not be believed; for by the same reasoning he could take my coat from me and say that he needed it for the common good. Also, according to **the Apostle** <note>original: "apostolum"; refers to St. Paul in Romans 3:8, "Let us not do evil that good may come."</note>, evil things are not to be done so that good things may result. Therefore, nothing should be disgracefully extorted only to be later spent—as is falsely claimed—on worthwhile uses. Again, if the Prince by right can make one simple change of money and take some profit from it, by the same reasoning he can make a larger change and take a larger profit, and change it many times and have even more profit, and make a change or composite changes, and always increase the profit according to the methods touched upon before; and it is likely that he or his successors would proceed in this way, either by their own impulse or through advisors, since this would be permitted; because human nature is inclined and prone to increasing its own riches when it can do so easily, and thus at last...

---

(1) *So in manuscripts B and C; manuscript A says* Therefore I wish, *V. says* I wish therefore. — (2) *Manuscript A says* change. — (3) *Here a gap begins in manuscript V.* — (4) *Manuscript A omits "this matter."*

<summary>In Chapter XV, Oresme argues that any profit a ruler gains from debasing or changing currency is inherently unjust and tyrannical. He refutes the common excuse that such profits are used for the "public good," comparing it to a ruler stealing a citizen's clothes under the same pretext.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the end of Chapter XV, which discussed the Prince's power to manipulate currency. It concludes the warning that such power leads to absolute tyranny before beginning Chapter XVI, which uses Aristotelian philosophy to argue that profiting from the manipulation of money is a violation of the laws of nature.</meta>

<page-num>CXVI</page-num>

the **Prince** <term>princeps: the sovereign or ruler</term> could draw to himself almost all the money or wealth of his subjects and reduce them to slavery. This would be direct tyranny—indeed, true and perfect tyranny—as is clear from the philosophers and from the histories of the ancients.

### Chapter XVI.

->*That the profit gained from changing money is unnatural.*<-

Although all injustice is in some way (1) against nature, nevertheless, taking profit from the changing of **money** <term>monetæ: the official currency system</term> is an unnatural injustice in a very specific way. For it is natural (2) for certain kinds of natural wealth to multiply themselves, like grains of corn which, when sown, "the field returns with much interest," as **Ovid** <note>original: "fenore reddit ager"; a quote from the Roman poet Ovid, using the language of "interest" (fenus) to describe a heavy harvest.</note> says. But it is monstrous and against nature that an infertile thing should give birth, or that a thing sterile by its very nature should bear fruit or multiply from itself—which is exactly what money is. 

Therefore, since money itself brings (3) profit not by being spent in the trade of natural wealth (its own natural use), but by being changed into itself—as in changing one kind for another or trading one for another—such profit is vile and contrary to nature. For by this reasoning, **Aristotle** proves in the first book of the **Politics** <note>Oresme relies heavily on Aristotle’s *Politics*, specifically the sections regarding "Chrematistics" or the art of wealth acquisition.</note> that **usury** <term>usura: the practice of charging interest on a loan</term> is contrary to nature (4), because the natural use of money is to be an instrument for exchanging natural wealth, as has often been said. Whoever, therefore, uses it in another way misuses it against the natural purpose of money; for he makes, as Aristotle says, a **penny** <term>denarius: a common unit of currency; here used to mean money in general</term> give birth to a penny, which is against nature. Furthermore (5), in these changes where—

---

(1) *A* a certain c. — (2) *A* because it is natural. — (3) *A* has. — (4) *The words "For by this... because" are missing in manuscript A.* — (5) *C* Furthermore.

<summary>This page concludes Chapter XV by equating the seizure of subjects' wealth with tyranny and begins Chapter XVI, arguing that money is "sterile" by nature. Using Aristotle, the author claims that making money multiply through currency exchange is as unnatural as usury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter XVI, which posits that manipulating currency is a violation of natural law. Having previously cited Aristotle to argue that money is inherently sterile and should not "breed" profit, Oresme now moves to evidence from Roman statecraft and Holy Scripture to show that changing the definitions of weights and measures is a form of falsehood.</meta>

<page-num>CXVII</page-num>

profit is taken, it becomes necessary to call that a **penny** <term>denarius: a standard silver coin, though here used as a general term for a unit of currency</term> which in truth is not a penny, and that a **pound** <term>libra: both a unit of weight and a high-value denomination of money</term> which is not a pound, and so with the others as was said before. It is clear that this is nothing other than to disturb the order of nature and reason; hence **Cassiodorus** <note>Flavius Magnus Aurelius Cassiodorus (c. 485 – c. 585), a Roman statesman and author who wrote extensively on the importance of administrative integrity.</note> says: *“Give a **solidus** <term>solidus: originally a gold coin; Oresme uses it here as a symbol of a fixed unit</term> and take away from it if you are able; hand over a pound and diminish it if you can; it is certain that all these things are provided for by their names themselves—either you give the whole unit, or you do not pay the very things that are named. You cannot give the names of whole units while performing wicked diminutions.”* <note>original: "Da certe solidum et aufer inde si prævales... non potestis omnino nomina integritatum dare et scelestas imminutiones efficere."</note> Therefore, to violate such secrets of nature, and to wish to confound such certainties—does this not seem a cruel and foul mutilation of truth itself? It is established that weight and measure must be trustworthy, because all things are thrown into disorder if integrity is mixed with fraud. Again, in the **Book of Wisdom** <note>The Wisdom of Solomon 11:20. This was a standard medieval proof-text for the divine origin of mathematics and order.</note> it is said that God has disposed all things by measure, weight, and number; but in the alteration of money, profit is not taken unless fraud is committed in these most certain things, as I declared before. Therefore, he who seeks profit for himself from such alterations detracts from God and nature.

### Chapter XVII.

->*That to profit from the alteration of money is worse than usury.*<-

There are three ways, as it seems to me, in which someone can profit from money without putting it to its natural use: one is through the **art of exchange** <term>ars campsoria: the profession of the money-changer who profits from the difference in value between different currencies</term>, the keeping or merchandising of coins; another is **usury**; the third is the alteration of money. The first way is vile, the second is bad, and the thi...

<summary>Oresme argues that debasing currency is a "mutilation of truth" because it uses the names of fixed measures (like the pound) for diminished quantities, violating the divine order of "measure, weight, and number." <meta>This page continues the argument from page CXVII. Oresme is comparing different ways of making a profit from money, building on the Aristotelian idea that money is naturally sterile and should not "breed." He is now specifically contrasting the professional money-changer and the usurer with the sovereign who debases currency.</meta>

<page-num>CXVIII</page-num>

...is even worse. Aristotle made mention of the first two [methods of profit] and not of the third, because in his time such wickedness had not yet been invented. Moreover, Aristotle proves that the first method is base and blameworthy by the reasoning already touched upon in the preceding chapter; for this is, in a way, **making money give birth** <note>The concept that money should be a medium of exchange, not a source of growth like livestock or crops.</note>. He also calls the art of money-changing <term>campsoria: the professional business of exchanging one currency for another</term> by the name *abolostatica* <note>original: "abolostaticam"—a Greek-derived term referring to the weighing or exchanging of small coins</note>, which is commonly called *poitevinage* <term>pictavinagium: likely a reference to the small profits made on the coins of Poitou, or a general term for petty money-changing</term>. For this reason, Saint Matthew the Apostle, who had been a money-changer, did not return to his former work after the Lord's resurrection, as did Peter, who had been a fisherman. In assigning the cause for this, the **Blessed Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a central authority in medieval theology.</note> says that *“it is one thing to seek a living through fishing, and quite another to increase wealth through the profits of tax-collecting.”* He says, *“For there are many businesses which can be practiced either with difficulty or not at all without sin, etc.”* <note>original: "aliud est victum per piscationem quærere aliud thelonei lucris pecunias augere. Sunt enim, inquit, pleraque negotia quæ sine peccatis aut vix aut nullatenus exerceri possunt, etc."</note> For there are certain base arts <term>banausæ: from the Greek for mechanical or illiberal trades, often considered degrading to the soul in classical philosophy</term> which stain the body, such as cleaning sewers, and others stain the soul, as does this one [money-changing].

Concerning **usury**, it is certain that it is evil, detestable, and unjust, and these things are established by Holy Scripture; but it now remains to show that taking profit from the changing of currency is even worse than usury. A usurer hands over his money to one who receives it voluntarily, and who can afterwards help himself with it and thereby relieve his own necessity; and that which he gives to the other beyond the principal <term>sortem: the original capital or sum lent</term> is based on a voluntary contract between the parties. But the **prince** <note>The sovereign ruler.</note>, in an improper changing of the currency, takes the money of his subjects simply involuntarily, because he prohibits the circulation of the previous, better money—which anyone would rather have than the bad—then, without necessity and without any utility that could come from it to the subjects, he himself [takes it]...

<summary>Oresme argues that debasing currency is a greater sin than usury because it is an involuntary seizure of wealth by the state. He cites Aristotle and Pope Gregory to classify money-changing as a "base art" that corrupts the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page CXVIII. Oresme has just distinguished between the profit of money-changers (which is base) and the profit of usurers (which is wicked). He now turns his focus back to the prince who debases the currency, arguing that this practice is even more predatory than usury because it is forced upon the entire population without their consent.</meta>

<page-num>CXIX</page-num>

[the prince] will return to them debased money. Even if he makes it better than before, this is only done so that it may be debased in the future, and so that he may give the people less in equivalent value of the good money than he received of the other; and however it may be, he certainly retains a portion for himself. Therefore, in this way, because he receives a gain upon the money, contrary to and beyond its natural use <note>Following Aristotle’s *Politics*, Oresme argues that the "natural use" of money is as a medium of exchange, not as a commodity to be sold for profit.</note>, this acquisition is equal to usury. Indeed, it is **worse than usury**, because it is less voluntary—or rather, more against the will of the subjects—and it is done without the possibility of it benefiting them, and is entirely without necessity. And since the profit of the usurer does not exceed so much, nor is it so generally harmful to many as this [debasement] which is imposed against and upon the whole community, no less tyrannically than deceitfully, I doubt whether it should rather be called **violent plunder** <note>original: "violenta prædatio"</note> or a fraudulent act.

### *Chapter XVIII.*

->*That such changes of currency, in and of themselves, are not to be permitted.*<-

Sometimes, lest worse things happen and to avoid scandal, certain dishonest and evil things are permitted in the community, such as public brothels <note>A common medieval legal argument: the "lesser evil" principle, where small vices are tolerated by the state to prevent greater social breakdown.</note>. Sometimes also, for some necessity or opportunity, some base trade is permitted, such as the art of the **money-changer** <term>campsoris: one who exchanges one currency for another for a fee</term>, or even a wicked one, such as usury. But concerning such a change of money for the purpose of taking profit, there appears no worldly cause why such a great profit should or could be allowed. For through this, scandal is not avoided, but rather generated, as is sufficiently clear from the eighth cha-

<meta>catchword: cha-</meta>

<summary>Oresme concludes that currency debasement by a prince is a form of robbery worse than usury because it is involuntary and harms the entire community. He argues that unlike certain "tolerated evils" like brothels or money-changing, there is no moral or practical justification for a prince to profit from altering the coinage.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter XVIII on page CXIX. Having established that debasing the currency is a form of "violent plunder," Oresme now argues that such practices are historical innovations—deviations from the successful governance of antiquity—and uses the authority of the Romans and Ostrogoths to support his case.</meta>

<page-num>120</page-num>

<header>— 120 —</header>

<margin>Book I, letter 10.</margin>
...chapter, and many disadvantages follow from it, some of which have already been touched upon, and others will be seen later, nor is there any necessity or opportunity for doing this, nor can it be expedient for the public welfare. A manifest sign of this is that changes of this kind are newly invented, as was just mentioned in the preceding chapter. For it was never done in this way in cities or kingdoms formerly governed prosperously, nor have I ever found a history that made mention of this, with the exception that in a certain letter of Cassiodorus <note>Cassiodorus (c. 485 – c. 585 AD) was a Roman statesman and writer who served under King Theodoric the Great.</note> written in the name of King Theodoric of Italy, one small change made in the weight of the coin is very harshly rebuked and most effectively condemned; a change which an official <note>original: "efficacius"; variant "Arcarius" suggests a Treasurer or tax official</note> had made for the purpose of paying certain soldiers. Regarding this, the aforementioned king, writing to Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius, the famous philosopher and statesman who served as a high official under Theodoric.</note>, says among other things:

> *Therefore, let your wisdom, schooled in philosophical teachings, cast out wicked falsehood from the company of truth, lest it be tempting for anyone to subtract anything from that integrity.* <note>original: "Quapropter prudentia vestra lectionibus erudita dogmaticis scelestam falsitatem a consortio veritatis ejiciat, ne cui sit appetibile aliquid de illa integritate subducere."</note>

And after some other remarks, he says again:

> *Certainly, that which is given to those who labor ought not to be mutilated; rather, from the person from whom faithful service is demanded, a full compensation should be provided.* <note>original: "Mutilari certe non debet, quod laborantibus datur, sed a quo actus fidelis exigitur, compensatio minuta præstetur." Note: The variant "imminuta" suggests "undiminished" compensation.</note>

If indeed the Italians or Romans eventually made such changes, as appears from certain poor-quality old money that is sometimes found in the fields, this was perhaps one of the reasons why their noble dominion came to nothing. Thus, it is clear that these changes are so evil that by their nature they are not to be permitted in any way.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the negative consequences of currency debasement. Oresme transitions from the historical precedents in the previous chapter to the specific moral and practical "inconveniences" faced by a ruler who manipulates the coinage.</meta>

<page-num>CXXI</page-num>

### Chapter XIX.

#### *Concerning certain disadvantages affecting the prince, which follow from changes (1) in the currency.*

Many great disadvantages arise from such changing of the money. Some of these primarily affect the prince <note>In this context, "prince" refers to any sovereign ruler or head of state.</note>, others the whole community, and others specific parts of that community. Indeed, in the short time that has recently passed, many such things have been seen to occur in the kingdom of France; some have already been mentioned, yet it is useful to recount them here.

First of all, it is utterly detestable and shameful for a prince to commit fraud, to falsify the currency, to call "gold" that which is not gold, and a "pound" <note>original: "libra"; the pound was a standard unit of weight and account.</note> that which is not a pound, as was discussed in chapters 12 and 13.

Furthermore, it is the prince's duty to condemn counterfeiters. How, then, can he not blush with shame if that very thing is found in him which, if found in another, he would punish with a most shameful death (2)!

Again, as was said in Chapter VIII, it is a great scandal and base for a prince that the money of his kingdom never remains in the same state but varies from day to day; sometimes it is even worth more in one place than in another at the same time.

Also, during these times of change, it is very often unknown how much this or that coin <term>numisma</term> <note>A "numisma" is a piece of currency; Oresme argues that money should have a fixed value to serve as a reliable measure for all other goods.</note> is worth. One is forced (3) to trade, buy, or sell the money itself, or to argue over its price, which is contrary to its very nature. Thus, for a thing that ought to be the most certain of all, there is no certainty, but rather an uncertain and disordered confusion, to the disgrace of the ruler (4).

Also, it is absurd and entirely alien to royal nobility, to—

---

(1) *V* permutations, *B* change. — (2) *A C* to be punished. — (3) *V* this. — (4) *V* sovereignty.

<summary>Oresme argues that currency debasement is a form of royal fraud that shames the prince, as he punishes others for the very counterfeiting he commits. He highlights how instability in money value turns the currency itself into a commodity, causing economic confusion and disgracing the crown.</summary>

...to inhibit the circulation of the true and good money of the kingdom, and out of greed to command—or rather to force—subjects to use inferior money, as if he wished to say that good is evil, and vice

versa; yet regarding such people the Lord said through the prophet: *Woe to you who call good evil, and evil good.* Again, it is a disgrace for a prince to show irreverence toward his predecessors, for everyone (1) is bound by the Lord's commandment to honor their parents (2). A prince seems to detract (3) from the honor of his ancestors when he abolishes their good money and causes it, bearing their image, to be cut up; and in place of the gold money which they minted, he makes money out of bronze (4) <note>original: "æneam"; Oresme is describing the debasement of high-value gold coins into base metal equivalents.</note> in part. Furthermore, a king ought to greatly abhor tyrannical acts, such as these changes to the currency as has been frequently stated; for they are also prejudicial and dangerous for the entire royal line of succession, as will be shown more extensively in what follows.

<meta>This page continues Nicole Oresme’s economic argument against currency debasement, focusing on how these "changes" act as a hidden tax and drain the kingdom of its precious metals.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>CXXIII</page-num>

...which was previously touched upon in chapter XV, primarily because a prince could, through this <term>debasement</term> <note>original: "mutationes"; Oresme refers to the practice of changing the weight or silver/gold content of coins while keeping their face value the same.</note>, draw almost all the money of the community to himself and excessively impoverish his subjects. And just as certain chronic diseases are more dangerous than others because they are less noticeable, so such an extraction of wealth is practiced more dangerously the less it is perceived; for the burden of it is not felt as quickly by the people as it would be through another type of tax. And yet, almost no tax can be heavier, more general, or greater.

Furthermore, gold and silver are reduced in the kingdom because of such changes and debasements; because despite any guards or prohibitions, they are carried abroad where they are valued more highly. For men strive to carry their money to places where they believe it is worth more. From this, therefore, follows the reduction of the material for money within the kingdom. 

Also, those from outside the kingdom sometimes counterfeit and bring similar money into the kingdom, and thus they attract to themselves the profit that the king believes he is gaining. Still further, perhaps the very material of the money is partially consumed by melting and remelting it as often as usually happens where such changes are practiced. 

Thus, the mintable material is diminished in three ways on account of the aforementioned changes. Therefore, it seems they cannot last for a long time unless there is an abundance of mintable material in mines or from elsewhere; and thus eventually the prince would not have the means to make a sufficient supply of good money. 

Also, because of these changes, good merchandise or natural riches from foreign kingdoms cease to be brought to that land in which the money is so changed, since merchants from other parts prefer to go to those places where they receive money...

### Chapter XXI.

#### *Concerning other disadvantages that affect a portion of the community.*

Certain parts of the community are occupied in honorable or useful businesses for the whole <term>commonwealth</term> <note>original: "reipublicæ"</note>, such as in natural riches, to grow or handle them for the necessity of the community—such as churchmen, judges, soldiers, farmers, merchants, craftsmen, and the like. But another part increases its own wealth through base profit-seeking, such as money-changers, money-traders, or <term>speculators</term> <note>original: "billonatores"; those who traded in "billon" (debased metal) or took advantage of the differing values of coins to make a profit.</note>: this business is indeed shameful, as was said (5) in chapter XVIII. Therefore, these people—who are, as it were, superfluous to the commonwealth—along with certain others, such as those who receive and handle money, and their kind, take a large portion of the gain or profit resulting from the changes in currency, and they are enriched either by malice or by chance...

<meta>This page continues Nicole Oresme's argument in Chapter XXI. He is detailing how the debasement of currency unfairly enriches speculators and "money-changers" at the expense of the productive classes—the clergy, soldiers, and farmers—and how it creates a moral opening for counterfeiters.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>CXXV</page-num>

...then against God and justice, since these individuals are undeserving of such great wealth and unworthy of such great goods. Others, however, are impoverished by this, even though they are the best parts of that community; thus the prince harms many of his better subjects (1) through this, and (2) burdens them excessively. And yet, the total profit does not come to the prince himself, but a great part of it goes to those aforementioned people, whose business is base and mixed with fraud.

Again, when the prince does not make known to the people beforehand the time and manner of a future <term>change in currency</term> <note>original: "mutationis monetæ"; this refers to the sovereign's official decree to alter the weight or metal purity of coins.</note> which he intends to make, some individuals, through cunning or through friends, foresee this secretly. Then they buy merchandise with <term>weak money</term> <note>original: "moneta debili"; currency that is about to be debased or has lower intrinsic value.</note> and later sell it for <term>strong money</term> <note>original: "moneta forti"; currency of high precious metal content.</note>, and suddenly they become rich, gaining excessively and unduly against the legitimate course of natural trade. This seems to be, in a way, a type of <term>monopoly</term> <note>Oresme uses "monopolium" here to describe an unfair market advantage gained through insider information rather than just control of a commodity.</note>, to the prejudice and loss of all the rest of the community.

Moreover (3), through such changes, it is necessary that rents fixed by a sum of money be either unjustly diminished or at least unjustly increased, as was touched upon before in chapter XI concerning the change of the currency’s name. Likewise, the prince, through such diversifications and <term>sophistications</term> <note>original: "sophisticationes"; in this context, the word refers to the adulteration or "watering down" of the purity of the metal in the coins.</note> of currencies, gives evil men the opportunity to make counterfeit money—either because it is less against their conscience to falsify it since it appears to them that the prince has done the same, or because their forgery (4) is not so quickly detected. Thus, they can more easily perpetrate more evils while these conditions persist than if good money were always in circulation. Furthermore, while these conditions last (5), what innumerable perplexities, obscurities, errors, and inextricable difficulties occur in accounts, expenditures, and receipts (6)!

***

(1) *V* <note>Manuscript variant V</note> omits "his" [subjects]. — (2) *V* adds "thus." — (3) "Moreover," omitted in *V*. — (4) *V*: "because it is not so much against their conscience that the forgery..." — (5) *V*: "standing" <note>original: "stantibus"; suggesting a state of stagnation or persistence.</note>. — (6) *A* <note>Manuscript variant A</note>: "occur from compositions, from mixtures and receipts."

<summary>Oresme explains that secret currency debasement allows speculators to practice a form of "insider trading," enriching themselves while the general public and the state lose out. He also argues that state-led debasement encourages counterfeiting by providing a moral excuse for forgers and creating administrative chaos.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Nicole Oresme’s critique of currency debasement from Chapter XXI. Having previously argued that debasement enriches speculators at the expense of the productive classes, Oresme now concludes that list of grievances and transitions to Chapter XXII, where he explores whether the community—rather than the prince—has the right to alter the currency in times of extreme necessity.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>CXXVI</page-num>

From this situation, grounds for lawsuits and various legal disputes also arise: bad repayment of debts, frauds, disorders, numerous abuses, and many other inconveniences that I would not know how to explain—perhaps even greater and worse than those already listed. Nor is this a surprise, because, as <term>Aristotle</term> <note>original: "Aristoteles"; Oresme refers here to a common principle in Aristotelian logic: "Given one absurdity, many follow" (Physica I).</note> says, once one absurdity is admitted, many follow, and this is not (1) difficult to see.

### Chapter XXII.

#### *Whether the community can make such changes to the currency.*

Since money belongs to the community—as was shown in chapter six—it seems that the community itself can decide its fate as it wishes. Therefore, it may also vary the currency in any way it likes, take from it as much as it pleases, and treat it (2) as its own property; this is especially true if the community should need a large sum of money for a war, or for the <term>ransom of its prince</term> <note>original: "redemptione sui principis"; in medieval law, paying the ransom for a captured lord was one of the few "extraordinary" cases where a tax was considered universally legitimate.</note> from captivity, or some other such accidental case. For the community could then raise (3) that money through a <term>change in currency</term> <note>original: "mutationem monetæ"; here meaning an official adjustment of the value or composition of coins to generate a profit for the public treasury.</note>, and this would not (4) be against nature or like usury, since it would not be the prince doing it, but (5) the community itself, to whom the money belongs. By this means, many of the arguments previously made against currency changes would cease to apply and would have no place here. It seems not only that the community can do this, but even that it should (6) do so when a <term>tax</term> <note>original: "collecta"; a general term for a public collection or tax.</note> is necessary (7), because in such a currency change, almost all the good qualities required in a <term>tallage</term> <note>original: "tallia"; a form of arbitrary tax levied by a lord or government upon their subjects.</note> or tax seem to be combined; for—

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XXII of Nicole Oresme’s "De Moneta." In the previous section, Oresme began exploring whether a community—rather than a prince acting alone—has the right to alter its currency to raise funds for emergencies like war or a royal ransom. Here, he lists the perceived advantages of using currency debasement as a form of taxation before offering his own nuanced critique of when such a measure is actually appropriate.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>CXXVII</page-num>

[It] brings in much profit <note>original: "lucrum"</note> in a short time; it is very easy to collect and distribute or assign without employing many people, without fraud by the collectors, and with small expenses. Furthermore, no [tax] can be imagined that is more equal or proportional, because generally, he who is able to do more, pays more, and it is—relative to its amount—less perceivable or felt, and therefore more tolerable without the danger of rebellion or the grumbling of the people. For it is most universal, so that neither cleric nor noble can exempt themselves from it by privilege or other means—as many wish to do with other <term>taxes</term> <note>original: "collectis"; refers to general levies or assessments on the population.</note>. From such exemptions arise envy, dissensions, lawsuits, scandals, and many other inconveniences that do not come from such a <term>currency alteration</term> <note>original: "mutatione monetæ"</note>; therefore, in the aforementioned case, it can and should be done by the <term>community</term> <note>original: "communitatem"</note> itself.

Regarding this matter, however, saving a better judgment, it seems to me for the present that it can be said: either that sum of money which the community needs must be transferred or spent (2) in remote parts and among peoples with whom there is no trade; and it is also so great that the <term>monetary material</term> <note>original: "materia monetabilis"; the actual physical gold or silver used for minting coins.</note> will, for this reason, be notably less in this community for a long time. And in this case, a tax can (3) be made through a change in the currency, either in its material or in its mixture. For if it were (4) done otherwise, such a change would later have to be made for the reason assigned and according to the method set out in <term>Chapter XII</term>.

But if the aforementioned sum is not so large, or if it is spent in another way—however it may be—so that the monetary material is not notably less in the community for a long time because of it, I say that, besides the inconveniences begun in the present chapter, there would still follow more, greater, and worse consequences than those explained above from such a change of currency than from another tax; and most especially there would follow the danger...

<summary>Oresme explores the argument that currency debasement is a "fair" tax because it affects everyone equally, but he argues it should only be used if a massive amount of bullion is leaving the country permanently.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from Chapter XXII to Chapter XXIII of Nicole Oresme’s "De Moneta." In the previous section, Oresme examined whether a community could justify altering its currency for the public good. Here, he concludes that even if a community does so, the measure must be temporary. He then moves into Chapter XXIII, where he explores the opposing arguments—specifically the traditional views that grant the prince the power to debase currency during times of necessity or through special legal privileges.</meta>

<page-num>CXXVIII</page-num>

...lest finally the prince might wish to claim this power for himself, and then all the disadvantages previously mentioned would return. Nor does the first reason stand in the way—the one which stated that the money belongs to the <term>community</term> <note>original: "communitatis"; the body of citizens or the state.</note>—because neither the community nor any individual can justly abuse their own property or use it unlawfully, as the community would be doing if it altered the currency in such a way. And if by chance the community itself should make such an alteration in any manner, then the currency must, as soon as possible afterward, be restored to its proper and permanent state, and the taking of profit from this currency must cease.

### *Chapter XXIII.*

#### *In which it is argued that the prince may alter the currency.*

It is often said that in a case of necessity, all things belong to the prince. He therefore may take from the coinage of his kingdom as much and in whatever manner seems expedient to him for an imminent or pressing necessity, or for the defense of the <term>commonwealth</term> <note>original: "reipublicæ"; literally "the public thing," referring to the state or the public interest.</note> or the sovereignty of his kingdom. Indeed, the method of collecting money through <term>currency alteration</term> <note>original: "mutationem monetæ"</note> is very convenient and suitable, as might be proven by those points discussed in the preceding chapter. Furthermore, even assuming that the prince cannot <insert>(1)</insert> <note>A variant reading in manuscript B suggests "might not be able to."</note> alter the currency in such a way and take so much <term>profit</term> <note>original: "emolumentum"</note> from it by ordinary or common law, it might nevertheless be said that he can do this by another private right. For instance, by a special privilege from the Pope, or the Church, or the Roman Emperor, or even one granted to him long ago by the community as a hereditary right on account of his great merits. Likewise, the money belongs to the community itself, as is clear from Chapter VI, and the community can alter it as was stated in the preceding chapter; therefore, the community itself can, or could, by its authority...

<summary>Oresme concludes that any communal currency debasement must be temporary and non-profit-seeking before presenting traditional arguments that justify a prince's right to alter money during emergencies or through special legal privileges.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter XXIII of Nicole Oresme’s "De Moneta." On the previous page, the text began exploring arguments in favor of the prince’s right to alter currency, particularly in times of necessity. This page continues that line of reasoning, presenting the legal and social justifications used by those who believe the prince should have total control over the mint and its profits to maintain the dignity of the crown.</meta>

<page-num>CXXIX</page-num>
<sig>9</sig>

...granting the prince the power to change the currency and stripping itself of the right to regulate and alter the money, giving a portion of the profit from the money to the prince to be taken by him however he wished. Likewise, if by common law it belongs to the <term>community</term> <note>original: "communitatem"; the body of citizens or the state as a collective.</note> to regulate the currency, as has been said often, and yet the community—because of the discord of the multitude—could not agree on a single method, could it not yield in this matter? Could it not agree that from then on and for the future the management of the currency should rest entirely upon the prince's will? Certainly; and it follows that by reason of this agreement, he himself should take a profit in the <term>alteration</term> <note>original: "mutatione"; refers here to debasement or changing the weight/alloy of coins.</note> or regulation of the money. 

Likewise, in chapter seven it was said that a certain <term>fee</term> <note>original: "pensio"; a fixed payment or tax for the service of minting.</note> ought to be set for the making of money, and that from and above that fee the prince can or should have something. Therefore, by the same reasoning, he can have or take more and more upon this, and consequently as much as he would through the alteration of the currency; therefore, he can raise that profit in the same way through such alterations. 

Likewise, it is necessary for the prince to have certain and large revenues from the community from which he can maintain a noble and honorable status, as befits princely <term>magnificence</term> <note>original: "magnificentiam"; in medieval philosophy, this was the virtue of greatness in action and lifestyle appropriate for a ruler.</note> or royal majesty. It is also necessary that these revenues come from the prince's own domain or from the proper right of the royal crown. It is possible, therefore, that a single large part of these revenues was long ago assigned to the business of the coinage, such that it would be permitted for the prince to receive profit by changing the currency. It is also possible that if this were taken away, the remaining revenues would never be sufficient for the state and status belonging to the prince. To wish, therefore, to take from him (1) the power of altering the currency—this is to attempt something against the honor of the kingdom; it is to <term>disinherit</term> <note>original: "exhæreditare"</note> the prince, indeed to impoverish him and deprive him of his state of magnificence. This would be not only unjust, but also a cause of shame for the whole community, for it is not fitting for a community to have a prince unless he is flourishing in an excellent state.

(1) <note>original French: "Ici se termine la lacune de V."</note> *Here ends the gap in manuscript V.*

<meta>This page transitions from the presentation of arguments for the prince’s power to change the currency (Chapter XXIII) to Chapter XXIV, where Oresme provides his rebuttal. He argues that fiscal necessity must be legitimate and community-verified, rather than a tool for tyranny.</meta>

<page-num>CXXX</page-num>

## Chapter XXIV.

### *Response to the aforementioned points and the principal conclusion.*

Although many difficulties might perhaps arise in the solution to the first argument, nevertheless, passing over them briefly for now, it occurs to me that lest a prince should feign such a necessity when there is none—as <term>tyrants</term> <note>Oresme frequently cites Aristotle’s *Politics* to distinguish between a legitimate king, who rules for the common good, and a tyrant, who rules for his own profit.</note> do, according to Aristotle—it must be determined by the <term>community</term> <note>original: "communitatem"; the body of citizens or the state as a collective.</note>, or by its more powerful part, either expressly or tacitly, when, what kind, and how great a necessity is imminent. 

By "expressly," I mean that the community ought to be assembled for this purpose, if the opportunity <note>original: "facultas"; one manuscript variant reads "difficultas" (difficulty).</note> is available; but "tacitly," if the necessity is so urgent that the people cannot be summoned, and yet so evident that it afterwards becomes notoriously clear. For then it is permissible <note>Some manuscript variants read "it is not permissible," though the context of a loan suggests Oresme allows for emergency measures.</note> for the prince to take something from the resources of his subjects—not through the <term>alteration of the currency</term> <note>original: "mutationem monetæ"; the practice of debasing the weight or alloy of coins to create a profit for the treasury.</note>, but by way of a loan, for which full <term>restitution</term> <note>original: "restitutio plenaria"; Oresme insists that emergency funds are a debt the crown owes to the people, not a gift.</note> must be made later. 

Regarding the other point, where it is said that the prince can have a <term>privilege</term> <note>A legal right or exemption granted by a higher authority, such as the Pope or Emperor.</note> to change the currency: first, I do not involve myself in the power of the <term>Pope</term>, but I believe he has never granted this, nor would he; since in doing so he would be giving license for wrongdoing, which no one would deserve to receive by acting well. Concerning the <term>Roman Emperor</term>, I say that he could never give any prince a privilege to do that which is not permitted to himself, such as this alteration of the currency, as is clear from what has been said before. Concerning the community, it was also stated in Chapter XXII that it cannot change the currency except in a certain case; and even then, if it should entrust this task to the prince with a reasonable limitation—which can [be understood] from that same chapter and...

---

<note>(1) "Or" — (2) "Difficulty" — (3) "Then it is not permissible," and omitting "not" before "through." These notes refer to variations in the original Latin manuscripts used for this edition.</note>

<summary>Oresme concludes that a prince cannot unilaterally declare a "necessity" to debase currency; such needs must be verified by the community, and any funds taken should be treated as a loan to be repaid. He further argues that neither the Pope nor the Emperor has the authority to grant a prince the right to alter money, as no one can grant a privilege to perform an act that is inherently unjust.</summary>
<keywords>Necessity, Tyranny, Consent, Restitution, Papal Power, Imperial Authority, Debasement, Community Rights</keywords>
