<meta>This page continues Nicolas Oresme’s rebuttal of the idea that a prince has absolute ownership over a nation’s currency. Oresme is arguing that even if a community "grants" this right to a prince, such a grant is often invalid if made under duress or if it violates natural law.</meta>

<page-num>CXXXI</page-num>

to appear to others, the prince would no longer do this as the principal author, but as the <term>executor</term> <note>original: "executor"; one who carries out a legal or public decree rather than creating it for personal benefit.</note> of a public ordinance. 

Regarding the other point, when it is said that the **community** to whom the money belongs can strip itself of its right and hand it all over to the prince, and thus the entire right of coinage would devolve to the prince; first, it seems to me that a well-advised community would never do this; nor is it even permitted to change the money in any way or make bad use of its property, as was said in Chapter XXII. Furthermore, a community of citizens, which is naturally free, would never knowingly reduce itself to **slavery**, or subject itself to the yoke of **tyrannical power**. Therefore, if the community, being deceived or terrified by threats or coerced, should grant the prince such <term>alterations</term> <note>original: "mutationes"; refers to the debasement or changing of the currency's value to generate revenue.</note>, not noticing the inconveniences that follow and that it would become servilely subject because of this, it can revoke this grant immediately or in any way possible. 

Furthermore, a thing which belongs to someone as if by **natural right** cannot be justly transferred to another. If, then, the money belongs to the free community itself—as is clear enough from Chapters I and VI—just as the community cannot grant the prince authority to abuse the wives of any citizens he wishes, so it cannot give him such a privilege over the coinage which he could only use for evil by exacting such a profit from its alteration; as is clear from many preceding chapters. 

Through this it is also clear what was further added about a community not in agreement regarding the regulation of money, which can yield, in this regard, to the prince's judgment. I say that it can do so regarding certain matters and for a time, but not by granting him the power to take such great profit from the aforementioned improper alterations. Regarding the other argument taken from Chapter VII, concerning the fact that the prince can...

<meta>This page continues Nicolas Oresme’s treatise, specifically addressing the counter-arguments used to justify a prince profiting from the debasement of currency. Oresme is refuting the idea that such profit is a legitimate right of the crown or a necessary source of revenue for the state.</meta>

<page-num>CXXXII</page-num>

to have a <term>profit</term> <note>original: "emolumentum"; referring here to the financial gain the prince might extract from the coinage.</note> on the money, the answer is easily given: that this is like a certain small and limited <term>pension</term> <note>original: "pensio"; a fixed payment or allowance.</note>, which cannot be increased at will through the aforementioned <term>alterations</term> <note>original: "mutationes"; the practice of changing the weight or silver content of coins.</note>, but remains fixed without any change whatsoever. Regarding the other point, it is granted that the prince may have revenues, and he ought to maintain a magnificent and most honorable status; but these revenues can and should be assigned elsewhere and collected in a different manner than through such improper changes, from which so many evils and so many inconveniences arise, as was shown previously (1). Even assuming that some part of these revenues is derived from the money, it nevertheless ought (2) to be of a certain and determined quantity—for example, two <term>shillings</term> <note>original: "solidi"; a common unit of currency.</note> for every <term>mark</term> <note>original: "marcham"; a traditional unit of weight used for precious metals.</note> that is minted, or something similar. In that case, the revenue would be collected without any change or any unreasonable and enormous increase of profit that can result from the detestable alterations so often mentioned.

Regarding these matters, it must be universally (3) concluded: that the prince cannot make such changes or take profit in such a way, neither by common or ordinary law, nor by privilege, gift, concession, pact, or any other authority or any other means whatsoever; nor can it be part of his <term>domain</term> <note>original: "dominium"; sovereign ownership or right.</note>, nor pertain to him in any manner. Furthermore, to deny him this (4) is not to disinherit him or to act against (5) the royal majesty, as mendacious flatterers, sophists, and **traitors to the commonwealth** falsely claim. Again, since the prince is duty-bound not to do this, he does not deserve to receive any pension or lordship (6) in exchange for abstaining from such an abusive exaction; for this seems to be nothing other than a ransom price for redemption from **slavery**, which no king or good prince ought to demand from his subjects. Likewise, assuming and not granting that

<meta>This page continues Nicolas Oresme’s "Treatise on the Origin, Nature, Law, and Alterations of Money." In the preceding section, Oresme argued that the prince’s right to collect a fee for minting must be fixed and limited, rather than an excuse for arbitrary profit. He now transitions into a moral and political argument, distinguishing between a rightful king and a tyrant based on their treatment of the public economy.</meta>

<page-num>CXXXIII</page-num>

even if he [the prince] held the privilege of taking a small fee above the cost of the coinage for the sake of making it good and for maintaining it in its proper state, he should still forfeit such a privilege in the event that he abused it so much that he changed and falsified the money for his own profit, increasing his gain no less greedily than shamefully (1).

## Chapter XXV.

### *That a Tyrant cannot last long.*

In these two chapters, I intend to prove that demanding money through such <term>alterations of the coinage</term> <note>original: "mutationes monetæ"; the practice of debasing the metal content or changing the face value of coins to enrich the crown.</note> is against the honor of the kingdom and to the detriment of the king's entire posterity. It must be understood, therefore, that there is this difference between a royal and a tyrannical government: that a tyranny loves and seeks its own private good more than the common benefit of its subjects, and strives for this purpose—to keep the people servilely subjugated to it. A king, on the contrary, prefers the public utility to his private utility, and above all things, after God and his own soul, he loves the good and the public <term>liberty</term> <note>original: "libertatem"; here Oresme links economic stability directly to the political freedom of the citizenry.</note> of his subjects.

And this is the true utility and nobility of a princedom; its rule is nobler and better to the extent that it is over free people or better men, as **Aristotle** says <note>Referring to Aristotle's *Politics*, where he argues that the best government is one that rules over free citizens rather than subjects in a state of servitude.</note>, and it is the more (2) lasting the longer the king's intention perseveres in such a purpose. As **Cassiodorus** <note>A Roman statesman and writer (c. 485 – c. 585 AD) whose works were widely used in the Middle Ages to teach the virtues of governance.</note> says: *the discipline of ruling is to love what is advantageous for the many* <note>original: "disciplina imperandi est amare quod multis expedit"</note>. For as often as a kingdom turns (3) toward tyranny, it is not preserved for a long time thereafter; because through this it leads to its <term>diminution</term> <note>original: "diminutionem"</note> (4), its transfer to other hands, or its total destruction (5).

---

(1) *Variant V:* acting. — (2) *Variant A:* and by so much... by how much; *Variant V:* and from it. — (3) *Variants C and V:* is turned. — (4) *Variant A:* division. — (5) *Variant V:* in every way.

<summary>Nicolas Oresme defines the distinction between a king and a tyrant, arguing that a ruler who debases currency for private gain acts as a tyrant and risks the collapse of his dynasty. He asserts that a legitimate monarchy is characterized by its dedication to the public good and the economic liberty of its subjects.</summary>

<keywords>tyranny vs. monarchy, public utility, debasement of currency, economic liberty, Aristotle, Cassiodorus, royal succession, political ethics</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicolas Oresme’s argument from Chapter XXV regarding the nature of kingship versus tyranny. On the previous page, Oresme began to distinguish a true king—who loves the liberty of his subjects—from a tyrant, who seeks to keep his people in servitude for his own profit. He continues here by citing classical authorities to prove that such oppressive rule is inherently unstable.</meta>

<page-num>CXXXIV</page-num>

[is advanced] (1) <note>original: "properatur"; Oresme is concluding a thought that such rule is bettered or advanced when it concerns free men. Manuscript variations suggest "is ruled" or "is prepared".</note>, especially in a temperate region far from any slavish barbarity, where men are free by their social interaction, their customs, and their very nature. These are not slaves, nor are they men whose hearts have been hardened by the habit of living under a tyranny. For such people, servitude would be harmful and against their will, and tyrannical oppression would be an act of pure violence; therefore, it cannot long endure, for as **Aristotle** says, things governed by violence are very quickly brought to ruin. For this reason, **Cicero** <note>original: "Tullius"; Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator and statesman.</note> says that no imperial power is so great that it can remain lasting when it is maintained by the pressure of fear. And **Seneca**, in his tragedies, says:

<margin>
Troades, lines 259–60.
</margin>

->No one maintains a violent empire for long,<-
->but moderate ones endure.<-

This is why the Lord, through the prophet, rebuked those princes who had failed in their duty, saying that they ruled their subjects with harshness and force. Furthermore, this point is explained in another way: for **Plutarch** says to the Emperor **Trajan** that the <term>commonwealth</term> <note>original: "respublica"; the public state or "body politic."</note> is like a certain body (2), which is brought to life by the favor of divine power (3); it is moved by the will of supreme justice and governed by the guidance of reason.

A commonwealth or a kingdom is, therefore, like a human body, as **Aristotle** maintains in the fifth book of his *Politics*. Just as a body is in a poor state when the <term>humors</term> <note>original: "humores"; in medieval medicine, the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile) which had to be balanced for health.</note> flow excessively into one of its limbs, so that the limb (4) often becomes swollen (5) and overgrown while the others wither and waste away (6), the proper balance is lost and such a body cannot live for long. In the same way, a community or a kingdom suffers when wealth is drawn into one of its parts beyond measure. For a community or—

---

(1) *Manuscript A* reads "is ruled," *Manuscripts B and C* read "is prepared." — (2) *Manuscript V* reads "in a certain way." — (3) *Manuscript V* reads "is as if animated by the benefit of divine power." — (4) *Manuscript V* omits "and that limb." — (5) *Manuscripts C and V* read "inflamed." — (6) *Manuscript A* reads "and thinned out."

<summary>Oresme uses classical philosophy and medical analogies to argue that a stable kingdom requires a balance of wealth, comparing a tyrant’s excessive taxation to a disease where one limb grows bloated at the expense of the rest of the body.</summary>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicolas Oresme’s argument from Chapter XXV of "On the Alteration of Money" (De Moneta). Building on the previous page's discussion of Cicero and Seneca, Oresme now uses the metaphor of the human body and musical harmony to illustrate the dangers of a ruler who amasses excessive wealth at the expense of his subjects.</meta>

<page-num>CXXXV</page-num>

A kingdom whose rulers <note>original: "principantes"</note>, in comparison to their subjects, grow enormously in wealth, power, and status, is like a monster—like a man whose head is so large and so thick that it cannot be supported by the rest of his weak body. Just as such a man cannot help himself, nor live for long, so too a kingdom cannot endure if its prince draws to himself excessive wealth, as happens through the **alterations of the currency** <note>original: "mutationes monetae"; the practice of debasing coins to increase the crown's profit, which Oresme argues is a form of hidden taxation.</note>, as was shown in chapter XX. Furthermore, just as in a blending of voices, extreme or improper equality <note>original: "aequalitas"; some manuscript variants suggest "inequality," but the context of musical harmony suggests Oresme is arguing for a balanced, proportional arrangement.</note> does not please or delight, for it destroys and defiles the whole harmony; rather, it requires a proportional and measured inequality, by which the sweet melodies of a joyful choir stand out. So also, generally speaking, among all parts of a community, an absolute equality of possessions or power is neither fitting nor harmonious. Yet, an excessive disparity also scatters and corrupts the harmony of the <term>commonwealth</term> <note>original: "reipublicae"; the state or public interest.</note>, as is clear from **Aristotle** in Book V of the *Politics*.

Most especially, if the prince himself—who is in the kingdom like the **tenor** <term>tenor</term> <note>In medieval music, the "tenor" was the foundational voice that "held" the melody (from the Latin "tenere"). Oresme uses this to show the prince should support the state, not overwhelm it.</note> and the principal voice in a song—exceeds in magnitude and is out of tune with the rest of the community, the melody of the royal government will then be disturbed. Because of this, according to **Aristotle**, there is yet another difference between a king and a tyrant. For a tyrant wishes to be more powerful than the entire community over which he presides by force; but the moderation of a king is tempered by such a measure that he is indeed greater and more powerful than any one of his subjects, yet he is nonetheless inferior in strength and wealth to the community as a whole, and is thus positioned in the middle.

But because royal power commonly and easily tends toward...

<summary>Nicolas Oresme uses metaphors of a monstrously large head on a weak body and discordant musical harmony to argue that a king who amasses excessive wealth—specifically through currency debasement—destroys the stability of the state. He distinguishes a king from a tyrant by noting that a true king remains subordinate to the collective power of his community.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter XXV and moves into Chapter XXVI of "On the Alteration of Money." Oresme is explaining that a ruler's power must be balanced to ensure the longevity of the state. He draws on the classical authority of Aristotle and the biblical examples of Solomon and Rehoboam to warn against the corrupting influence of flatterers and the dangers of absolute power.</meta>

<page-num>CXXXVI</page-num>

<header>— CXXXVI —</header>

is greater, therefore the greatest caution must be applied and a vigilant watch kept; indeed, the highest and most fundamental prudence is required to preserve it, lest it slide into **tyranny**. This is especially necessary because of the deceits of flatterers, who <note>original: "adulatorum fallacias"</note> (1) have always pushed princes toward tyranny, as **Aristotle** says. For these men—as we read in the **Book of Esther**—deceive the simple ears of princes (who, by their own nature, assume others are like themselves) with clever fraud, and through their suggestions, the noble pursuits of kings are corrupted.

But since it is difficult to avoid or root them out, Aristotle himself provides another rule by which a kingdom can be preserved for a long time. It is this: that the prince should not greatly increase his dominion over his subjects; he should not impose exactions or seizures; he should leave or grant them their liberties and not hinder them. Nor should he use the <term>fullness of power</term> <note>original: "plenitudine potestatis"; a legal concept implying absolute, unrestrained authority. Oresme argues here for constitutional limits.</note>, but rather a power limited or regulated by laws (2) and customs. For, as Aristotle says, few things should be left to the personal whim of a judge or a prince.

Aristotle brings forward the example of **Theopompus**, King of the Spartans <note>original: "Lacedæmoniorum rege"</note>, who had remitted many powers and taxes to the people (3) that had been imposed by his predecessors. When his wife wept and reproached him, saying that *it was shameful to hand over to his sons a kingdom of less profit* (4) *than he had received from his father*, he replied: ***I hand it over more enduringly.*** <note>original: "trado diuturnius"</note>

O divine oracle! O word of such weight, which should be painted in golden letters in royal palaces! "I hand it over," he said, "more enduringly," as if he were saying: *I have increased the kingdom more by its duration in time than it has been diminished by the moderation of my power.* Behold, here is one even greater than **Solomon**. For if **Rehoboam** <note>original: "Roboam"; the son of Solomon whose harshness led to the rebellion of the ten tribes of Israel.</note>, whom I mentioned above, had received and held the kingdom from his father Solomon arranged in this way, he would never have lost ten of the twelve tribes of Israel (5), nor would he have been reproached with the words: *You have profaned your seed to bring [wrath upon your children]...* <note>original: "Prophanasti semen tuum inducere"; a quote from the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 47:20, referring to the consequences of a ruler's folly.</note>

<summary>Oresme argues that a stable kingdom requires a prince to limit his own power and resist the influence of flatterers. He uses the Spartan King Theopompus as a model of how moderating authority actually makes a reign last longer.</summary>

### Capitulum XXVI.

->*That taking profit from the alteration of money is prejudicial to the whole royal power.*<-

I propose to demonstrate that the aforementioned alterations <note>meaning the debasement or changing of the weight and value of coins</note> are against the honor of the king and are harmful to the royal lineage. For this, I set forth three premises:

**First**, that it is a blameworthy thing in a king, and harmful to his successors, if by his actions the kingdom is set on the path to ruin, or disposed to be transferred to foreigners; nor could a king grieve (1) or weep enough, were he so unhappy and so (2) miserable as to do anything through his own negligence or through his bad government by which he or his heirs would lose a kingdom increased by so many virtues and gloriously preserved for so long. Furthermore, his glorious soul would be in danger if, through his own failing, the people suffered so many and such great plagues and calamities as usually happen in the destruction or the transfer of kingdoms.

**Second**, I suppose that a kingdom is exposed to ruin through <term>tyrannization</term> <note>original: "tyrannisationem"; the act of ruling as a tyrant rather than a lawful monarch.</note>, as was declared in the preceding chapter. And since, as it is written in the Book of **Ecclesiasticus**, *A kingdom is transferred from one people to another because of injustices and outrages, and various deceits*, and tyranny is indeed injurious and unjust.

In addition to this—to descend to specific details—far be it that the free hearts of the **French** (3) <note>original: "Francigenarum"; Oresme is appealing to the national identity and perceived inherent love of liberty among the French people.</note> should have degenerated to such an extent...

---

(1) <note>Manuscript variant: V reads "to say" instead of "to grieve."</note> — (2) <note>Manuscript variant: V omits "so... so."</note> — (3) <note>Manuscript variant: V reads "should degenerate."</note>

<summary>Oresme argues that currency debasement is a form of tyranny that threatens the stability of the throne and risks the transfer of the kingdom to foreign powers. He emphasizes the king's moral responsibility to maintain the state for his successors and the spiritual danger of causing his subjects to suffer.</summary>
<keywords>tyranny, currency debasement, French monarchy, Ecclesiasticus, political stability, royal succession, justice</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter XXVI. Having established that a king who ruins his kingdom or turns it into a tyranny risks losing his throne, Oresme now applies these principles specifically to the French monarchy. He argues that because the French people are naturally free, they will not tolerate the "tyranny" of currency debasement, which he views as a form of theft that undermines the crown's legitimacy.</meta>

<page-num>CXXXVIII</page-num>

...that they should become slaves voluntarily; therefore, a servitude imposed upon them cannot last. For even if the power of **tyrants** is great, it is nonetheless a violent act against the free hearts of their subjects, and it is weak against external enemies. Therefore, whoever would draw the lords of France toward such a tyrannical rule in any way would expose the kingdom to great danger and prepare it for its eventual end. For the noble offspring of the kings of France has not learned to rule as tyrants, nor has the French people become accustomed to being servilely subjected. Thus, if the royal offspring degenerates from its ancient virtue, it will without a doubt lose the kingdom.

— **Third**, I suppose, as has already been proven and often repeated, that to take or increase profit through the **alteration of money** <note>original: "mutatione monetæ"; refers to the medieval practice of debasing currency by reducing its precious metal content while maintaining its face value to profit the crown</note> is a deceitful, tyrannical, and unjust act. Indeed, it cannot be continued within a kingdom unless that kingdom has already, in many other respects, turned into a tyranny. From this, not only do disadvantages follow, but certain other evils must precede it, and others must accompany it; for this practice cannot be advised by men who are not corrupt in their intentions and ready to suggest every tyrannical fraud and wickedness, wherever they might see a prince inclined toward this or able to be swayed.

I say therefore, in summary, that a thing by which a kingdom is disposed toward ruin is base and prejudicial to the king and (1) <note>Manuscript variant A omits "and"</note> to his heirs (2) <note>Manuscript variant V adds "prejudicial"</note>—and this was the first premise; and that this involves the kingdom being drawn or converted into a tyranny—and this was the second (3) <note>Manuscript variant V uses "stated" instead of "second"</note>; and that one is driven toward this through the alteration of money, as was stated in the third premise. Therefore, the **exaction** <note>original: "exactio"; a forced payment or tax</note> which occurs through such alterations is against the honor of the king, and is prejudicial to the entire royal posterity (4) <note>Manuscript variant A reads "power" [potestati] instead of "posterity" [posteritati]</note>, which was the point to be proven.

<summary>Oresme concludes his argument by asserting that the French people are inherently free and that currency debasement is a tyrannical act that endangers the future of the French monarchy.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to Nicole Oresme’s treatise. <page-num>CXXXIX</page-num>

These things, therefore, as I stated before, are offered without dogmatic assertion and are subject to the correction of the wise. For, according to Aristotle <note>Oresme refers here to a principle found in Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*, suggesting that political and social matters do not allow for the same mathematical certainty as the physical sciences, requiring instead practical wisdom and deliberation.</note>, civil and political matters are for the most part doubtful and uncertain. 

If anyone, therefore, out of a love for discovering the truth, wishes to contradict these statements or write against them, he does well; and if I have spoken wrongly, let him bear witness to the error, but let him do so with reason, lest he seem to condemn on a whim or a prejudice that which he cannot effectively disprove.

->*Here ends the treatise on the alteration of money.*<- <note>original: "Explicit tractatus de mutatione monetarum." The word "Explicit" is a traditional medieval formula used to mark the end of a manuscript.</note>

# NICOLAUS COPERNICUS

<page-num>CXXXIX</page-num>

These things, therefore, as I stated before, are offered without dogmatic assertion and are subject to the correction of the wise. For, according to Aristotle <note>Oresme refers here to a principle found in Aristotle’s *Nicomachean Ethics*, suggesting that political and social matters do not allow for the same mathematical certainty as the physical sciences, requiring instead practical wisdom and deliberation.</note>, civil and political matters are for the most part doubtful and uncertain. 

If anyone, therefore, out of a love for discovering the truth, wishes to contradict these statements or write against them, he does well; and if I have spoken wrongly, let him bear witness to the error, but let him do so with reason, lest he seem to condemn on a whim or a prejudice that which he cannot effectively disprove.

<meta>This page serves as a transition from the medieval economic theories of Nicole Oresme to the Renaissance insights of Nicolaus Copernicus. It introduces Copernicus's 1526 treatise on currency, explaining its historical recovery and publication in the 19th century.</meta>

# PREFACE.

---

The monetary doctrines for which Nicole Oresme proved to be a skilled interpreter met an illustrious defender at the beginning of the sixteenth century. In 1526, at the invitation of King Sigismund I of Poland and Chancellor Szydlowiecki, the great Copernicus outlined the true principles of the subject in his *System of Money* (*On the Minting of Coinage* <note>original: "Monetæ cudendæ ratio"</note>). This short treatise was first published in the *Warsaw Memorial* (*Pamietnik Warszawski*), in the August 1816 issue, by the learned history professor Félix Bentkowski, who included a Polish translation alongside the original Latin text. It was reproduced in the most recent edition of Copernicus's works (1).

This publication was based on an authentic copy of the original manuscript, which is preserved in the archives of Königsberg. It was discovered there around 1815 by Severin Vater, a professor of theology at the University of Königsberg and a corresponding member of the former *Society of the Friends of Letters* of Warsaw, who was honorably known for his research on Polish language and antiquities. At the request of another distinguished scholar, Samuel Linde, rector of the Warsaw Lyceum, Vater

(1) Warsaw, 1854, a magnificent folio volume of 641 pages: *Nicolaus Copernicus of Toruń, Six Books on the Revolutions of the Celestial Spheres, with the First Account by G. Joachim Rheticus, along with several minor writings of Copernicus now collected for the first time, and his life* <note>original title: "Nicolai Copernici Turoniensis, de revolutionibus orbium cœlestium libri sex, accedit G. Joachimi Rhetici narratio prima, cum Copernici nonnullis scriptis minoribus nunc primum collectis, ejusque vita."</note>. Warsaw, in the year 1854. — The Latin text is accompanied by a Polish translation by Mr. Jean Baranowski. *On the Minting of Coinage* occupies pages 565-574.

<summary>This preface introduces Nicolaus Copernicus’s 1526 treatise on money, detailing its 19th-century rediscovery in the Königsberg archives and its subsequent publication by Polish scholars.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Copernicus, Monetæ cudendæ ratio, monetary theory, Sigismund I, economic history, Félix Bentkowski, Königsberg archives, numismatics</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the rediscovery of Nicolaus Copernicus’s monetary treatise, detailing the authentication of the manuscript by the Königsberg archivist and the historical circumstances that led Copernicus to involve himself in Prussian and Polish financial affairs.</meta>

had an authentic copy of this work made and deposited in the library of the latter institution, accompanied by the following attestation: “I testify, having been requested to do so, that this preceding copy agrees word-for-word with its true original existing in the most sacred archives of the Kingdom of Prussia, with the seal of the Archive affixed. Königsberg <note>original Latin: "Regiomonti"</note>, February 1, 1816. Carl Faber, Internal Archivist of His Royal Majesty.”

The curator of the Königsberg archives, Faber, provided a description of the manuscript, composed in these terms: “Copernicus’s opinion on the creation of a new currency in Prussia. — From the year 1526. — This autograph production by Copernicus is, as proven by the numerous corrections in the author's hand, the very original of the first draft. This document is bound in a volume containing various other dissertations on Prussian money up to the year 1528. It was among the many manuscripts seized by the Swedes during the war and sent to Stockholm. The Prussian government obtained its restitution in 1801; it is now deposited in the Königsberg archives, 1st section, number IX.”

The author of the *Life of Copernicus* (1), Mr. Julien Bartoszewicz, rightly insists on the importance of the *Treatise on Money*, a product of this famous scholar's genius. This work indeed commands attention as a historical document of great significance. In it, Copernicus reveals the vivid sense of patriotism that led him to place his attachment to royal power and his love for the Polish homeland above provincial interests.

The Grand Master of the Teutonic Order, Albert of Brandenburg, had seized the properties of the Chapter of Warmia <note>a semi-autonomous ecclesiastical state in Prussia</note>, of which Nicolaus Copernicus was a member as a canon of Frauenburg <note>now Frombork, Poland</note>. Copernicus was sent in 1521 and 1522 to the assembly of the Prussian lands, held at Graudentz <note>now Grudziądz, Poland</note>, to plead the case before the King of Poland against the usurpation that had been committed. Aside from this specific interest, a grave general interest had been

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Nicolaus Copernicus’s involvement in the economic crisis of Prussia. The previous page ended discussing how the Grand Master of the Teutonic Order, Albert of Brandenburg, had seized Church properties; this page details how his financial abuses and the debasement of currency by major cities were countered by Copernicus and King Sigismund I.</meta>

thwarted by the abusive practices of the *Grand Masters* <note>The heads of the Teutonic Order, a Germanic medieval military order that ruled parts of Prussia.</note>. Since the Peace of Thorn (1466), which had forced them to recognize the suzerainty of the King of Poland for East Prussia—established as a fiefdom—while West Prussia had returned to Poland, these leaders had never ceased to debase the currency, devaluing it through fraudulent alloys. The cities of Thorn <note>Toruń</note>, Elbing <note>Elbląg</note>, and Danzig <note>Gdańsk</note>, swept up by this bad example and relying on their privileges, also struck their own coins and degraded the *standard* <note>The 'titre' or standard refers to the percentage of pure precious metal contained in the coin.</note>, vying with one another in this race to the bottom.

King Sigismund wished to remedy this disorder; he set about bringing the Prussian currency and the royal currency of the rest of Poland into a single unity. Copernicus energetically supported this project: he knew that bad money had driven out the good; the coins of higher quality had either left the country or had been thrown into the melters' crucibles <note>This economic principle, that "bad money drives out good," is often called Gresham's Law, though Copernicus described it clearly here before Gresham.</note>.

Under Albert of Brandenburg, fine silver made up only one-twelfth of the composition of coins that were originally manufactured at a standard of nine-twelfths!

Copernicus labored in vain to have a sound currency of proper weight and standard restored: the cities turned even their own poverty and the scarcity of precious metal into a weapon to maintain what they claimed as a right—the ability to issue debased legal tender.

The opinion of Copernicus—so remarkable for its exact knowledge of the subject, the clarity of its presentation, and the fairness of the proposals inspired by an enlightened love for the public good—did nothing but excite murmurs against him from the representatives of Danzig, Thorn, and Elbing, who swayed the nobility to vote against it.

The question of Prussian currency was not resolved until 1526 by Sigismund I, who, persevering in the idea of unity, had Copernicus complete the first draft of the remarkable treatise reproduced in this volume. A convention in 1528, mentioned by Lengnich <note>Gottfried Lengnich (1689–1768), a noted historian of Danzig and West Prussia.</note> in his great work

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the economic reforms in Prussia, specifically how historical accounts and Nicolaus Copernicus’s own writings document the transition from the 1522 Graudentz assembly to the formal coinage treatise of 1526 under King Sigismund I.</meta>

on the history of the *Prussian Lands*, ended this dispute (1).

Copernicus’s work on currency was only known through a partial reproduction made in an old German dialect—scarcely intelligible today—by C. Schütz in his *History of Prussian Affairs or True and Proper Description of the Lands of Prussia, etc.* <note>original: "Historia rerum Prussicarum oder wahrhafte und eigentliche Beschreibung der Lande Preussen, etc."</note>, by *N. C. Schützen* (*Leipzig, 1599, folio edition, p. 480*). The author claims to provide Copernicus's account word-for-word; he is undoubtedly referring to the opinion shared at the assembly of Graudentz <note>Modern-day Grudziądz in Poland.</note> in 1522—an opinion that Copernicus expanded and completed in 1526 at the invitation of the King of Poland. This is also the view seemingly accepted by *David Braun* in his work on the currencies of Poland and Prussia (2).

Felix Bentkowski points to a specific passage regarding the Prince of Prussia as decisive proof that the date of Copernicus’s writing was later than the 1522 assembly at Graudentz. Indeed, it is well known that Albert <note>Albert of Brandenburg-Ansbach, the last Grand Master of the Teutonic Knights.</note> did not take this title, granted by Sigismund I, until 1525. It was at this time that the Grand Master Albert made peace with the King, paying him homage for the Prussian possessions which, upon the advice of Martin Luther, were converted into a secular duchy (1525).

Sigismund I had recognized the value of the arguments presented by Copernicus in 1522. He invited him to draft a more extensive treatise; such was the origin of *On the Minting of Money* <note>original Latin: "Monetæ cudendæ ratio"</note>, which served as the foundation for the decisions made by the King in 1526.

This work, intended to spark economic and political reform, appeared to us to be of sufficient importance both because of its—

<meta>This page concludes the editorial introduction regarding the publication of Nicolaus Copernicus’s economic writings. own merit and by the author’s name, so that we might join it to the treatise by Nicole Oresme <note>A 14th-century French scholar and bishop who wrote early influential works on economics.</note>, uniting the two most interesting historical documents relating to *illustrious monetary matters* <note>original Latin: "præclara res numeraria"</note>. We have placed a French translation alongside Copernicus's Latin text.

It remains for us to say a few words regarding the *informal talk* which precedes the *System of Money*.

Invited to participate in the *lectures of the Salle Barthélemy* <note>A popular public lecture hall in 19th-century Paris used for "popular education" for the masses.</note>, we attempted, before an audience of three thousand people, composed in part of ladies, to translate the truths of science into the form most accessible to everyone. The question of *money* is perhaps the one about which the most errors and prejudices circulate. It seemed useful to us to address it on this occasion, all the more so because we were thus led to speak of one of the glories of Poland, Copernicus, and because we could make known a remarkable production whose existence was scarcely suspected.

We hesitated for some time to reproduce here a *chat* <note>original: "causerie"</note> whose style, more literary than scientific, risked being out of place with the gravity of this volume. We only decided to do so upon the advice of distinguished men, who approved of this attempt to popularize some sound ideas regarding one of the most arduous problems of political economy.

Once our resolution was made, we did not wish to change anything of what we had said, and we reproduce this *informal talk* exactly as it was captured by stenography <note>A method of shorthand used to record live speeches.</note>, with all the hazards of improvised speech.

->L. W.<- <note>Louis Wolowski (1810–1876), the editor of this volume and a prominent economist.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the editorial introduction to the text of the public lecture itself. It introduces the speaker's rationale for discussing Nicolaus Copernicus not as an astronomer, but as an economist, and begins to set a humble, popularizing tone for the audience at the Salle Barthélemy.</meta>

### INFORMAL TALK

#### ON THE TREATISE

# ON MONEY

## BY NICOLAUS COPERNICUS

---

The subject of our talk this evening has perhaps caused some surprise; it is a subject rather dry by nature: money. I must explain the reasons that determined this choice. First, there is a personal motive; second, the interest attached to the remarkable work by one of the most glorious representatives of ancient Poland in the field of science, Copernicus; and finally, the importance of the subject itself.

Although it may not be very proper, allow me to begin with myself.

I chose this subject because, more than anyone, I fear yielding, within these walls, to the passion of speech; I fear going too far, and I wanted to place an ingot <note>A block of precious metal, like gold or silver, used here as a metaphor for a heavy weight to keep his emotions in check.</note> beside my heart to restrain its movements.

My second motive is Copernicus, that great figure who stands out from the past to carry—along with his name—the glory of the country that gave him life to the entire universe, whose mysteries he penetrated.

In speaking to you of Copernicus, I do not intend to follow him into the heavens; my task will be more modest: I shall remain on earth. I shall not have the temerity <note>Excessive confidence or audacity.</note> to speak to you about

<meta>This page continues the speaker's introduction to his lecture. Having established that he will not be discussing Copernicus's astronomical achievements, he now pivots to Copernicus's less famous role as an economic theorist, comparing him to the French thinker Nicole Oresme and setting him ahead of later Italian and English economists.</meta>

his great work on the movement of celestial bodies, that admirable discovery of the true system of the world. My learned colleague, Mr. Bertrand <note>Joseph Bertrand (1822–1900), a mathematician and historian of science at the Académie des Sciences.</note>, of the Academy of Sciences, is currently preparing a most remarkable work that should exhaustively cover all that concerns Copernicus in this great regard. I only intend to approach the manifestation of this powerful genius from one small side.

Copernicus is the author of a *Treatise on Money* <note>original: "Traité de la monnaie"</note>, very little known, but which deserves to be. I recently claimed for my second homeland, France, the honor of having preceded other nations in the sound assessment of the grave and important problems related to currency. I tried to show how Nicole Oresme <note>A 14th-century polymath and Bishop of Lisieux who wrote one of the earliest specialized treatises on money.</note>, Bishop of Lisieux and advisor to King Charles V, called the Wise, had, as early as the end of the fourteenth century, laid down with marvelous precision the true principles concerning money. Allow me to associate with this glory the country where I was born—that noble land watered today with blood and tears <note>A poignant reference to Poland, which at the time of this lecture in the 1860s was suffering under foreign partitions and the aftermath of the failed January Uprising against the Russian Empire.</note>—for Copernicus produced a work no less remarkable on this great question.

Today, although many strange ideas still circulate in this regard, the problem of money is generally known and familiarly approached. It was otherwise in times past. Error dominated this point as it did many others. The Italians, who until now had been regarded as the precursors of truth in this branch of political economy <term>political economy: the original name for the study of economics, specifically how production and trade relate to law and government</term>, only treated this matter in depth at the end of the sixteenth century. The first publication devoted to this question in Italy is by Scaruffi <note>Gaspare Scaruffi (1519–1584), an Italian economist who authored 'Altinonfo' in 1582 regarding a universal monetary system.</note>; it dates from the last years of the sixteenth century (1582). England, that country which has risen so high in the field of science—

Now, as Oresme dates from the end of the fourteenth century, the priority belongs incontestably to France.

But it must be said, in those difficult times, ideas did not travel quickly. Communication routes did not exist, those routes thanks to which the entire world today tends more and more to become like a single family, endowed with the common heritage of the treasures of the intellect. Railways were undreamed of, and paved roads barely existed. The movement of ideas was concentrated within a narrow radius; the finest productions risked being forgotten, unless they appealed to religious sentiment, to popular passion, or lifted souls with the accents of poetry. The ideas of Nicole Oresme expired, so to speak, with him; Copernicus, when he took up the same problem, possessed a true merit of invention and originality; he worked from his own resources, and not from the resources of others.

Do I need to remind you who Copernicus was? If we know the true system of the world today, it is to him that we owe it; a writer in whom the most varied knowledge was combined with the finest genius and that admirable common sense—the essential characteristic of the French spirit—Voltaire, said:

> “The beam of light that illuminates the world today set out from the small town of Thorn.” <note>Thorn is the German name for Toruń, the city in modern Poland where Copernicus was born.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nicolaus Copernicus, following the previous page's introduction of him as a man of genius. The text begins mid-word, completing the adjective "Polish" (polonaise) in reference to his nationality or the spirit of his people.</meta>

...ish nation. It was there that he wrote that admirable book on the revolutions of the celestial spheres <note>original: "révolutions célestes"; a reference to Copernicus's seminal work, *De revolutionibus orbium coelestium*, published in 1543.</note>, that book which, by destroying ancient errors, ceased to have the earth regarded as the center of the world and man as the sole purpose of creation. Under Copernicus’s compass, the earth no longer appears as anything but a planet which, carried by a double movement, describes its annual orbit around the sun, while rolling itself upon its axis in the space of twenty-four hours. This system singularly confuses common idioms. "The sun rises," we say every day, whereas it is the sun that remains motionless and the earth that turns!

Such an extraordinary idea was bound to meet many skeptics; in the early days, Copernicus and his discovery were ridiculed. Actors portrayed on stage the singular effects that the earth's rotation would have produced, according to them, if this rotation had been anything other than a whim. But these jests did not prevent the truth from finally shining in everyone's eyes and drawing universal admiration for the magnificent discovery of the system of the world.

Without wishing to dwell on this point, I must recall that those who shared the spoils of ancient Poland <note>A reference to the Partitions of Poland (1772–1795), where the territory of the Polish-Lithuanian Commonwealth was divided among Prussia, Russia, and Austria.</note> also wanted to seize its glory by disputing its claim to Copernicus.

Attempts have been made to turn him into a German. To do justice to such a claim, it is enough to recall that Humboldt <note>Alexander von Humboldt (1769–1859), the famous Prussian polymath and geographer.</note> and Arago <note>François Arago (1786–1853), a French physicist and astronomer.</note> loudly condemned it; it is enough to say that the whole of Poland, by subscribing to the monument erected in Warsaw to consecrate the memory of Copernicus, protested against this new usurpation of which they wanted to make her a victim!

> Whose simplicity exceeds its grandeur.

Copernicus shattered forever the complicated structure of Ptolemy’s <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD) was an astronomer whose model placed the Earth at the center of the universe, surrounded by "crystal spheres."</note> crystal spheres; he revealed the simple and admirable harmony that connects the bodies rolling through space, just as a similar harmony actually connects the interests of society, however diverse they may appear!

The clarity of vision and soundness of judgment that led to the great discovery of the world’s system also guided Copernicus when he approached the delicate, complicated, and difficult subject of *currency* <note>original: "la monnaie." Copernicus wrote an influential treatise on economics titled *Monetae cudendae ratio* (1526), where he formulated the principle that "bad money drives out good."</note>.

People have always complained about this *cursed money*, of which one never possesses enough to satisfy one's desires. <meta>This page continues the discussion of Nicolaus Copernicus, transitioning from the historical resistance to his astronomical discoveries to his insights into political economy, specifically the nature and function of money.</meta>

errors and prejudices. No one stood to gain or lose by the Earth revolving around the sun, or remaining motionless, and yet scientific evidence was long resisted. It is different with money viewed as an element of wealth; people have been only too inclined to seek it in the crucibles of new alchemists <note>The author compares modern economic theorists or speculators to "alchemists," who historically sought to create gold from lead, suggesting that people often look for magical ways to create wealth where none exists.</note> and to confuse it with wealth itself. In reality, it is the *medium* <note>original Latin: "medium," meaning a middle ground or a means through which something is achieved.</note> of circulation—a *medium* that performs wonders far more serious than those of certain other *mediums* who have also come from America <note>This is a play on words; the author is likely mocking the mid-19th century craze for Spiritualist "mediums" and seances that originated in the United States and spread to Europe.</note>.

Things change their form, their place, or their hands. They change form through human industry; they change place when objects that abound in certain locations are carried to others; they change hands when exchange provides those who desire something with the object they lack, in return for giving up what they possess or the services they can provide. Money intervenes here in all its power.

There is one point upon which I must insist, for it has been little addressed until now, and it allows for the clearest possible accounting *of* the importance and utility of that great instrument of human labor: money. In reality, it constitutes a powerful and productive machine for the decomposition and recomposition of labor; it appears as the most energetic lever of human association. It is often accused and wrongly condemned because the services that it alone can provide are not sufficiently understood.

It is thanks to money that every labor (whether provided in the form of salaried service or in the form of association) is immediately valued and encounters instan-

...taneously its reward. It is thanks to money that we put time into operation—that fabric from which life is made—and that we collect and accumulate the product of hours and minutes, so as to make the fate of all less miserable. Money plays the most important, the most active role in the relations between peoples. We shall return to this point shortly.

In former times, money was held in great honor. Our own century is presented as an age of money! It seems that it is being slandered; on this point, as on many others, it is better than one might suppose. If we look back toward antiquity, we see a true idolatry of precious metal in every form; later, just as in ancient times every means had been exhausted to attract as much of this wealth as possible into the interior of each country, every legal artifice was exhausted to achieve the same result. In one of his tragedies, Crébillon <note>Prosper Jolyot de Crébillon (1674–1762), a French playwright known for his dark tragedies.</note> has Rhadamiste <note>The protagonist of the play *Rhadamiste et Zénobie* (1711), who is a prince of Armenia.</note> say, in order to show how certain lands are condemned to a severe and harsh fate:

> Nature, like a stepmother in these dreadful climates,
> Produces, instead of gold, only iron and soldiers.
<note>original: "La nature marâtre, en ces affreux climats, / Ne produit, au lieu d’or, que du fer, des soldats."</note>

We believe that iron and soldiers can be a very good thing, especially in these current times <note>A likely reference to the political upheavals or wars of the mid-19th century when this was written.</note>. But gold appeared to every eye as the highest goal toward which human desires could reach.

In antiquity, gold was divinized. One of the brilliant poets of Greece—the one who would be called the Molière of antiquity, if anyone could be compared to Molière—Aristophanes, presents in one of his sharp comedies the blind and inert Plutus, the god of wealth <note>original: "Plutus," the Greek god of riches. In Aristophanes' play, he is depicted as blind so that he distributes wealth randomly.</note>, courted by all the deities of Olympus. Jupiter, the king

The ancients also glimpsed the weak side presented by this element so envied by all. You know the old fable of King Midas—that king whose ears were revealed by certain indiscreet reeds <note>According to myth, Midas was given donkey ears by Apollo. His barber whispered the secret into a hole in the ground; reeds grew there and whispered the secret whenever the wind blew.</note>. Midas, who wanted nothing but to amass treasures, was condemned to see everything transform into gold under his hands, and he died of hunger. The allegory unveils the error of confusing <term>wealth</term> with the possession of precious metals; these are the vehicle of wealth, and not wealth itself.

A comparison comes to my mind, and I cannot resist the desire to share it with you. In my youth (unfortunately a long time ago), I read with greed and repeated with enthusiasm the verses of a great poet, one of the purest glories of Poland: Adam Mickiewicz <note>Adam Mickiewicz (1798–1855) is the national poet of Poland, a leading figure in Polish Romanticism.</note>, that powerful genius on whose brow shines the double halo of glory and misfortune. In his poem *The Forefathers' Eve* <note>original: *Les Aïeux* (Polish: *Dziady*)</note>, Mickiewicz depicts a situation quite different from that of King Midas, yet arriving at a similar lesson. It concerns a young man who has loved and is betrayed; his fiancée abandons him to marry a rich man. In his grief, he invokes a punishment similar to the one the gods had inflicted upon Midas.

I tried (I was eighteen then) to translate Mickiewicz, for I committed many verses in my youth; and I am doubly guilty, for I committed them in both Polish and French. I still remember this passage, and I am going to recite it to you while asking for some indulgence, for I have not—

<meta>This page continues the speaker’s exploration of the historical obsession with gold. The previous page concluded with a poem by Adam Mickiewicz linking the myth of Midas to modern greed and the ancient quest of the Argonauts. Here, the speaker transitions from mythology and poetry into the economic history and the "superstitions" of the Middle Ages and the Age of Discovery.</meta>

In the Middle Ages, there were many new Jasons <note>In Greek mythology, Jason led the Argonauts to capture the Golden Fleece; the speaker uses him as a symbol for those seeking fortune through adventure.</note> who rushed in pursuit of gold, that marvelous element in which all human happiness was thought to be concentrated. It is a singular thing—and this is not the only circumstance where one is led to make this observation—that the very mistakes of the human spirit sometimes serve the march and progress of civilization and humanity. This great error, this confusion made between the metal and wealth itself, tore men away from their routine immobility and from the soil upon which they seemed to have taken root; it pushed them toward distant discoveries and mingled them with one another. When gold was not found in the depths of a jealous soil, they went to seek it elsewhere. It was gold they were pursuing, those bold navigators who landed in various parts of the world, those intrepid adventurers who contributed so much to the discovery of a new hemisphere! <note>A reference to the Americas and the "Age of Discovery" driven by the search for *El Dorado* and mineral wealth.</note>

Another economic error arose later, which always had the same goal: to attract as much gold as possible. I am speaking of the <term>mercantile system</term> <note>Mercantilism: an economic theory dominant from the 16th to 18th centuries that held that a nation's wealth was measured by its supply of gold and silver.</note> upon which were grafted the prohibitive system and the ultra-protective system from which, thank God, we are rid of today.

Gold inspired a true superstition; the moment of reaction had to arrive. It had been exalted too much, and eventually, it was lowered much too far.

A letter from Christopher Columbus (I could not choose a more eminent example), addressed to Ferdinand and Isabella after his fourth voyage, bears proof of the respect and adoration of which gold was the object.

> "Gold is an excellent thing," he writes. "With gold, one forms treasures; with gold, one does everything one desires in this world; one even makes souls reach paradise." <note>original: "L’or est une chose excellente... avec de l’or on fait tout ce qu’on désire en ce monde, on fait même arriver les âmes en paradis." This quote is from Columbus's 1503 letter from Jamaica to the Spanish monarchs.</note>

Today, around this magnificent pedestal on which—

He concludes with a quote from Christopher Columbus illustrating the near-religious reverence once held for the metal.</summary>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the historical "errors" of the Middle Ages—such as the obsession with finding gold through alchemy or exploration—to a more nuanced economic view. The speaker moves from discussing the physical pursuit of gold to its philosophical and functional role in society, citing the historian Michelet to defend gold's utility against those who see it only as a "chimera" or a "tyrant."</meta>

...which you see the god of gold raised up, there are many who repeat the famous refrain from *Robert the Devil* <note>A popular 1831 opera by Giacomo Meyerbeer; the specific aria "L’or est une chimère" (Gold is a chimera) highlights the deceptive nature of wealth.</note>: *Gold is a chimera*; for many somewhat adventurous minds, gold, for a certain time, seemed to be nothing but a costly deception.

Christopher Columbus and Robert are both wrong. One must neither deify gold, nor think to deny its useful and fertile influence. Mr. Michelet <note>Jules Michelet (1798–1874), a celebrated French historian known for his vivid, poetic prose.</note>, the eminent historian who knows how to combine a picturesque imagination with keen insight, has rightly pointed it out:

> "Let us be careful not to speak ill of gold. Compared to feudal property, to land, gold is a superior form of wealth. A small thing, mobile, exchangeable, divisible, easy to handle, easy to hide, it is wealth already refined, I was going to say spiritualized... This docile metal serves every transaction; it follows, easy and fluid, all circulation (1)."

Were it not for the brilliance of the expression, and if one looked only at the accuracy of the judgment, one would believe that an economist had written these lines. The virtues of gold are, in fact, admirably summarized and defined in the short passage I have just read to you.

To those who speak of the tyranny of gold, of a usurped royalty, or of the uselessness of this gear in the social machine, I will oppose something to which I attach great value and which seems to me to outweigh even the most brilliant theories: the unanimous consent of the human race. Molière <note>The 17th-century French playwright; a famous anecdote claims he would read his plays to his servant to see if she understood the humor, trusting her "common sense."</note>, when he wanted to be sure of the correctness of an idea, consulted his servant. I believe that many statesmen would do well to consult this servant of Molière, which is universal common sense.

<meta>This page continues the speaker's defense of gold as a legitimate economic tool rather than a mere "chimera." Having just quoted the historian Michelet on gold's "spiritualized" and fluid nature, the author now addresses the historical fallacy that the value of money is derived solely from the decree of a ruler rather than the intrinsic value of the metal itself.</meta>

No, gold cannot be disdained. It is not a hollow symbol of wealth, imposed by authority and accepted through blind trust—a symbol whose value would reside only in the stamp decorating it, which would lend itself with singular ease to all forms of tampering, and which would thus encourage the exploits of counterfeiters, whether of high or low standing.

There was a time when counterfeiters did not hide in caves to practice their trade. They governed the State and carried out their business with a perfectly clear conscience. Was the sovereign's will not sufficient to bestow value upon the metal discs put into circulation to represent all other values and all other products? Was assigning a certain price to a specific coin not enough to give that coin the value indicated by its face value (1)? <note>The author uses the term "titre," which refers to the legal value or denomination assigned to a coin, often in contrast to its actual precious metal content.</note> However, regardless of their authority or the violence accompanying its exercise, they always failed in these undertakings, and they could never understand the reasons for these failures.

At times, the Church sought to punish such practices; popes issued bulls of excommunication against the forgers of currency; in this way, they responded to the cries of suffering from the people; the great quarrel of Philip <note>A reference to King Philip IV of France, known as "the Fair" (r. 1285–1314), who famously debased the French currency to fund his wars, earning him the nickname "the Counterfeiter King."</note>

(1) In the sixteenth century, the great legal scholar Dumoulin <note>Charles Dumoulin (1500–1566), a renowned French jurist who wrote extensively on contracts and usury.</note> had pointed out this dangerous error; faithful to the same line of thought, d’Aguesseau <note>Henri François d'Aguesseau (1668–1751), Chancellor of France and a respected legal mind.</note> wrote these words, full of wisdom and marked by a rare accuracy, during the era subject to the risky deceptions of Law <note>John Law (1671–1729), the Scottish economist whose "Mississippi System" created a massive speculative bubble and subsequent economic crash in France in 1720.</note>: “Gold and silver possess a natural value which they in no way receive from the prince. In vain would the prince wish to force this principle and do violence to nature itself: authority does not have the power to fully subjugate reason and enslave common sense.” (*Considerations on Currency.*)

<meta>This page continues the speaker's historical analysis of currency debasement, moving from the specific case of Philip IV of France to a broader European context. the Fair <note>Philip IV of France, "le Bel," whose name began on the previous page</note> with the Holy See is linked in part to the debasement of the currency. Dante—about whom one of my distinguished colleagues (1) spoke to you so brilliantly—casts counterfeiters into the depths of Hell, and among those damned souls appears Philip the Fair (2).

The example of this king was not an isolated case. The clear conscience felt by most sovereigns, arising from the false idea they held regarding money, made such practices nearly uniform throughout all of Europe—except in one country that almost always avoided these abuses, and that country is the very one that people today would like to wipe off the map of the world! <note>The speaker refers to the Partitions of Poland, which had been divided among Russia, Prussia, and Austria during the late 18th and 19th centuries.</note>

Poland has, more than any other state, faithfully maintained a <term>honest currency</term> <note>The original "monnaie droite" refers to currency that maintains its legal purity and weight without being "clipped" or debased with cheaper metals.</note> of standard and weight. It has also been spared another misfortune (to use a very mild term): that of paper money, the ultimate expression of currency debasement. Indeed, as one reduces the amount of fine gold and silver in each coin, and as one increases the proportion of the <term>alloy</term> <note>A mix of precious and base metals; increasing the alloy content makes the coin less valuable.</note>, the intrinsic value of each piece declines; if this amount drops to a truly "homeopathic" proportion, it is essentially paper money that is being manufactured.

A memory from my school days will help me express my thoughts exactly. Who has not tasted *abundance*? This hardly fortifying beverage consists of a great deal of water mixed with a very little wine. It is the perfect image of debased currency—that artificial *abundance* one claims to produce by means of altering the money. Like the drink from school, this false *abundance* provides very little benefit.

<meta>This page transitions from the speaker’s anecdotal remarks about currency debasement (concluding the thought from the previous page regarding "homeopathic" proportions of precious metals) to a focused examination of Nicolaus Copernicus's economic theories. I have allowed myself to drift into this casual conversation, and I risk leaving the main subject that has brought us together too far to the side. I must introduce you to Copernicus’s <term>treatise on money</term> <note>Copernicus wrote *Monetae Cudendae Ratio* (On the Minting of Money) in 1526 at the request of King Sigismund I of Poland.</note>. This remarkable and generally ignored work is something I must address.

Copernicus understood well the importance of the purity and fixity of currency; he denied princes the power to establish its value at will. Here is the preamble to his treatise:

> “However innumerable the <term>scourges</term> <note>The original French uses *fléaux*, referring to devastating calamities.</note> that usually lead to the decline of kingdoms, principalities, and republics, the following four are, in my view, the most formidable: discord, mortality <note>Refers to plague or high death rates.</note>, the sterility of the earth <note>Refers to famine or crop failure.</note>, and the deterioration of currency. As for the first three, the evidence is such that no one is ignorant of them. But for the fourth, which concerns money, except for a few men of great sense, few people concern themselves with it. Why? Because it does not happen all at once, but little by little, through a sort of latent action, that it *ruins the State*.”

These words are admirable in their clarity and truth. — Copernicus then moves on to a sketch of the nature of money.

> “Gold and silver, marked with a stamp, constitute the currency intended to determine the price of things that are bought and sold, according to the laws established by the State or by the prince. Money is therefore a kind of common measure for the estimation of values; this measure must be fixed and conform to the established rule. Otherwise, there would necessarily be disorder in the State. Buy—

<meta>This page continues the presentation of Nicolaus Copernicus's economic treatise, specifically transitioning from his warning about the "scourge" of currency debasement to his definition of what money is and why it was invented. It concludes the quote from the previous page's preamble.</meta>

> ...buyers and sellers would be deceived at every turn, as if the ell <note>An *aune* (ell) was a traditional unit of length used primarily for measuring fabric.</note>, the bushel <note>A *boisseau* (bushel) was a traditional unit of dry volume used for grain.</note>, or the weight did not maintain a certain fixed quantity. Now, this measure resides, in my view, in the valuation <note>Copernicus uses the term *estimation* to describe the face value assigned to money.</note> of the currency. Although this valuation is based on the quality of the material, it must nevertheless be distinguished from the *value* itself. Indeed, money can be valued at more than the material from which it is made, and the reverse <note>original: *vice versâ*</note>. »

As Copernicus explains, metallic currency—acting as both *sign and pledge*—contains within itself the law of exchange equilibrium and becomes the regulator of circulation. It constitutes the fixed point of comparison to which everything relates, and it thus serves as a common scale for all goods, whose exchange it facilitates.

> « The establishment of money, says Copernicus, is born of necessity. Although exchanges could have been conducted by simply weighing gold and silver—since these metals are regarded everywhere, by the unanimous consent of men, as things of value—nevertheless, because there would be numerous inconveniences in always being obliged to carry weights, and as not everyone is able to recognize the purity of gold and silver at a glance, it was agreed that the authorities should mark the currency with a stamp. This stamp is intended to reveal how much gold and silver each piece contains, and to serve as a guarantee of public trust. »

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Copernicus's economic principles. The previous page ended mid-word ("guarantee"), likely referring to the state's guarantee of a coin's value. This way of valuing metals is used in a country that is hardly distinguished by a rapid march along the path of progress. In China, they weigh the money, which is given an emblematic shape, that of little shoes, <note>The author is referring to *sycee* (from the Cantonese *sai-si*), which were silver bullion ingots used in China. Their boat-like shape often resembled a small shoe.</note> as if to make it understood that, thanks to money, the world moves. A singular way of moving, to be constantly bumping against the need to submit each piece to an individual expert evaluation! The stamp affixed by the authority, by making known the quantity of precious material and by determining the price of the denominations, serves significantly to facilitate exchange and the promptness of transactions. To this end, the authority plays a considerable role in all the arrangements of money; it must fulfill it with scrupulous accuracy, and see to it that each coin is the exact representation of the value it states, of the fineness <note>original: *titre*</note> it proclaims. The authority must not act like those Roman emperors who constantly degraded the composition of the coins. Money rings with a sound that is more or less pure depending on the nature of its composition; this sound, being more or less altered, could serve to mark the various periods of decadence.

The debasement of money and the grave consequences it entails were perfectly understood and condemned by the upright mind and elevated genius of Copernicus. He also understood the utility of monetary unity. The age-old wish of France was to achieve what we possess today, which everyone enjoys without noticing—just as one breathes the surrounding air without realizing the service it provides—which was to have one law, one weight, one <note>The text ends here, but this is a reference to the famous Revolutionary slogan: *une loi, un poids, une mesure* (one law, one weight, one measure), which advocated for the standardization of systems across the nation.</note>

Monetary unity was ranked among the conditions that most directly affect the organization of the State; in reality, any blow struck against either the unity or the integrity of the currency causes a real injury to the social body; it has incalculable consequences. One of the most distinguished and honorable men to have directed France's finances, Mollien, <note>François-Nicolas Mollien (1758–1850) was a French financier who served as Napoleon's Treasury Minister. Why do we attach such great importance to it? Thanks to money, and to the direct, instantaneous estimation it allows regarding the share each person takes in communal production, this production moves forward and develops rapidly; exchanges multiply, and society becomes less and less wretched. Thus Mirabeau (not the great orator, but Mirabeau the father) <note>Victor de Riqueti, Marquis de Mirabeau (1715–1789), was a prominent French economist and father of the famous Revolutionary figure.</note> once wrote that the three greatest inventions of the human mind were *the alphabet, money, and the economic table* <term>tableau économique: a visual model of the economy designed by François Quesnay to show how wealth circulates through society</term>. The Phoenicians invented the alphabet, which allows for the communication of thoughts, and money—that "alphabet of exchange"—which allows for the communication of products between men.

If time had permitted, and if I did not fear to tire you, I would have spoken to you of the essential conditions of money: the necessity that it have an intrinsic value <term>intrinsic value: the inherent worth of the material, such as gold or silver, as opposed to value assigned by law</term> and a high value in a small form; that it be easily portable; that it could pass from hand to hand; and that it remain durable and unalterable (1).

The condition of durability, by preventing this portion of wealth from evaporating, brings about a very important economic effect. The mass of precious metals is preserved and increases constantly; thus, the ratio existing between annual production and the total quantity already acquired decreases, giving the value greater stability; this explains in part how the predic- <note>The text breaks off mid-word, likely intended to be "prédictions" (predictions).</note>

measure in proportion to their being of more general use, of a more similar quality, and easier to divide into parts of equal value. All are more or less suited to be a universal guarantee of exchange in proportion to their being less susceptible to loss and alteration in their quantity and quality.

**§ XLII. *Reciprocally, all money is essentially a commodity.***

One can only take as a common measure of values that which has a value, that which is received in trade in exchange for other values; and there is no universally representative guarantee of a value other than another equal value. A money of pure convention <term>money of pure convention: also known as "fiat" money; currency that has value only because of government decree rather than inherent material worth</term> is therefore an impossible thing.

**§ XLIV. *Metals, and especially gold and silver, are better suited for this than any other substance, and why?***

We have now arrived at the introduction of precious metals into commerce. All metals, as they were discovered, were admitted into exchanges because of their real utility: their brilliance made them sought after as ornaments; their ductility and solidity made them suitable for making vessels more durable and lighter than those of clay. But these substances could not enter into commerce without almost immediately becoming the universal money. A piece of any metal whatsoever has exactly the same qualities as another piece of the same metal, provided it is equally pure. Now, the ease with which one can separate, by various chemical operations, a metal from other metals with which it might be alloyed, means that they can always be reduced to the degree of purity or, as it is expressed, to the *standard* <term>standard: translated from the French 'titre', referring to the proportion of pure precious metal in an alloy</term> that one desires; then the value of the metal can only differ by its weight. By expressing the value of each commodity by the weight of the metal given in

exchange, we will therefore have the clearest, most convenient, and most precise expression of all values, and from then on it is impossible that in practice it would not be preferred over any other. Metals are no less suitable for becoming the universal guarantee of all the values they can measure; as they are capable of all imaginable divisions, there is no object in commerce whose value, large or small, cannot be exactly paid by a certain quantity of metal. To this advantage of lending itself to all sorts of divisions, they add that of being unalterable, and those that are rare, like silver and gold, have a very great value within a very inconsiderable weight and volume.

These two metals are therefore, of all commodities, the easiest to verify for their quality, to divide for their quantity, to preserve eternally without alteration, and to transport to all places at the lowest cost. Any man who has a surplus commodity, and who does not at the moment need another commodity for use, will therefore hasten to exchange it for silver <note>The French word "argent" means both "silver" and "money."</note>, with which he is more certain than with anything else of procuring the commodity he wants at the moment of need.

### § XLV. *Gold and silver are constituted, by the nature of things, as money and universal money, independently of any convention or law.*

There then are gold and silver constituted as money and universal money, and this without any arbitrary convention of men, without the intervention of any law, but by the nature of things. They are not at all, as many people have imagined, *signs of value*; they have a value themselves. <note>This is a key point in classical economics: the author argues that money has "intrinsic value" because it is a physical commodity, rather than just a "sign" or "token" representing value.</note> If they are capable of being the measure and the guarantee of other values, this property is common to them with all other objects that have a value in commerce.

Money thus acquires one of the essential characteristics for it to fulfill the role of an intermediary in circulation: its value is the least changeable of all those belonging to the things of this world, especially for transactions completed at relatively close time intervals. Within this limit, the value of money becomes almost invariable.

...[unal]terable and easier to transport than other commodities, it is more convenient to employ them to measure and represent values.

### § XLVI. *Other metals are only employed for these purposes subsidiarily.*

All metals would be capable of being used as money. But those that are very common have too little value within too large a volume to be used in the current exchanges of commerce. Copper, silver, and gold are the only ones in habitual use.

And even then—with the exception of a few peoples to whom neither mines nor commerce had yet been able to provide a sufficient quantity of gold and silver—copper has never served except in exchanges of the smallest values.

### § L. *The use of money has greatly facilitated the separation of various labors among the different members of society.*

The more money <note>original: "l'argent"; in this context, the author refers both to silver specifically and to the general concept of money.</note> took the place of everything, the more each person could—by devoting themselves solely to the type of cultivation or industry they had chosen—relieve themselves of all care to provide for their other needs. They could think only of procuring as much money as possible through the sale of their fruits or their labor, being certain that, with this money, they would have everything else. It is in this way that the use of money has prodigiously hastened the progress of society.
