<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical requirements of money, specifically focusing on the divisibility and homogeneity of precious metals compared to other assets like diamonds or livestock. It follows the previous page's conclusion that money must be able to be divided and reunited without losing value.</meta>

-> - 32 - <-

at will. A broken diamond, even if it were the Koh-i-Noor <note>The Koh-i-Noor is one of the largest cut diamonds in the world, part of the British Crown Jewels; the author uses it here to illustrate that once a gemstone is cut or broken, it cannot be made whole again without losing its primary value.</note> itself, would lose value significantly, and one could no longer restore it to its original form. Metal is fusible; it divides and recombines easily, allowing it to follow all the variations of circulation.

Precious metal is always and everywhere homogeneous. Whether gold comes from California, Australia, or the Altai <note>A mountain range in Central Asia, then a major source of Russian gold.</note>; whether silver arrives from Mexico, Peru, or Saxony, they present an identity that is found in no other thing. Nowhere else would one find this essential quality of identity, homogeneity, and divisibility.

In primitive times, livestock were used as an instrument of exchange. Even today, this is practiced in America <note>The author likely refers to the frontier regions of the Americas or South American pampas.</note>, and can be suitable, in a pinch, for transactions of a certain value. But it is difficult, for small purchases, to cut oxen into steaks and chops, and even more difficult to put them back together and transport them from one place to another. *Living money* also entails other inconveniences and troubles; it can neither be preserved without expense, nor stored without loss (1).

Another quality of precious metals is being fa— <note>The text likely continues as "facile à transporter" (easy to transport).</note>

This mass, combined with that of a roughly equal value of silver, increases by barely *one-fortieth per year*, while the uses for *precious metals* multiply with the progress of civilization. Without mentioning the luxury of gildings that stream from every side, nor the gold powder that fashion attempts to scatter upon ladies' hair (as it is quite ugly, we hope it will not be used much), let one consider the quantity of jewelry, precious and sweet memories, from the *wedding ring* <note>original: "alliance"</note>, which sanctifies the union of spouses, to the cross, a revered sign of salvation with which the peasant woman loves to adorn herself; let us account for so many other objects of taste and whim, the use of which is made widespread by the increasing prosperity of populations. > [stora]ge, so that, in order to keep the animal portion of my proceeds <note>original: "recette" — the box office takings or receipts.</note> alive, I must sacrifice all the vegetables to it."

It is difficult to better highlight, through the law of opposites, the advantages of *metallic money*, preserved without loss, used without embarrassment, transported without effort, accumulated without waste, and which maintains, without new effort, the power with which it is endowed—always consistent with itself. There are no two head of cattle, two sacks of wheat, two acres <note>original: "arpents" — an old French unit of land area, roughly equivalent to an acre.</note> of land that are identically the same; money alone does not vary in its nature.

One can therefore understand that we are not on the verge of seeing the markets close for the production of gold and silver.

That is not all: as the world progresses, social relations are modified in a way that requires more currency for the settlement of increasingly varied accounts. More and more, labor—instead of being paid only by a portion of the products necessary for existence—is paid in money: the <term>monetary economy: an economic system where goods and services are exchanged for money rather than through barter</term> is everywhere replacing the <term>natural economy: a system based on subsistence and the direct exchange of goods or labor</term>, which was summarized by the granting of a small plot of land where forced labor <note>original: "corvée" — unpaid labor that a peasant owed to a lord as a form of "rent."</note> represented the rent. Even in Russia, since the Emancipation Act <note>The 1861 reform by Tsar Alexander II that abolished serfdom, freeing millions of peasants.</note>, wheat no longer costs only the "blows of a stick distributed to the peasants" to produce, as Sismondi <note>Jean Charles Léonard de Sismondi (1773–1842), a Swiss historian and economist known for his critiques of industrial capitalism.</note> expressed it. Gold has yet to ruin anyone; it possesses a singular virtue for populating deserts, making great cities rise from beneath the earth as if by the stroke of a wand, and summoning light and life into vast solitudes. What a sudden transformation!

<meta>This page continues the author's discussion of the economic growth spurred by precious metals, moving from specific examples like Australia to the broader mechanics of global trade and the relationship between physical money and credit.</meta>

global trade now encompasses about *fifty billion in value*. — This result has been achieved thanks, in part, to gold and silver, which stimulate production by facilitating the mutuality of services; they constantly raise the need for exchange to a higher power by extending the division of labor, which is correlative to the easy distribution of money—that precious vehicle of relations, that common language of values.

Communications between men begin with the path, crudely cleared through the forest; then come the small roads, the highways, the paved roads, the canals, and the railways. Something analogous occurs for the agents of circulation <note>In this context, "circulation" refers to the movement of money and goods through an economy.</note>: it begins with crude barter; it gains life through the choice of a common measure, however imperfect it may be; it takes a broader flight the moment metallic money finds use; and it rises to the widest proportions through the introduction of the great family of bills of exchange, promissory notes, fiduciary signs <note>“Signes fiduciaires” refers to paper currency or financial instruments that rely on public trust (fides) rather than having intrinsic value like gold.</note>, bank notes, checks, and by means of the ingenious processes of current accounts and <term>virements: a system of transferring credits between bank accounts without using physical cash</term>.

One should not imagine that fiduciary signs make metallic money useless or superfluous. They are merely its reflection, just as the moon shines with brilliance borrowed from the sun; what would become of it if the sun were to be extinguished? The solid ground of gold and silver remains the true basis of human transactions; it borrows useful auxiliaries from credit, but it alone serves as the true support for the edifice of production and the circulation of wealth; the rest is valuable only by consequence. Man can facilitate the movements of the metallic mechanism; he can perfect its springs; he cannot suppress it.

<summary>The author argues that while modern credit instruments like checks and notes expand trade, they are entirely dependent on the "sun" of metallic gold and silver, which remains the essential foundation of the global economy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that while credit and paper instruments are useful, they cannot exist without the physical foundation of gold and silver. The previous page ended by describing gold and silver as the "solid ground" of human transactions.</meta>

...without compromising everything. Those who have claimed to banish precious metals by substituting them with fictitious signs—paper money—have sometimes misled minds through a fatal illusion: like the enchanters of the Middle Ages, they showed golden fruits to fascinated gazes; but the charm was not long in breaking, and nothing remained but oak leaves! <note>The author uses a folklore motif where "fairy gold" or magic money reveals its true, worthless nature—often dead leaves—once the spell fades.</note>

There is a very simple proof that reveals the futility of dreams of paper money; it is the very designation given to these scraps of paper called upon, so it is claimed, to drive gold and silver out of human commerce. What do they claim to be, in fact? *Francs, florins, dollars, rubles, thalers, pounds sterling*, that is to say, the equivalent of specific quantities of gold and silver. They wish to deny the precious metals, and yet they affirm them; the names they borrow protest against the goal they seem to pursue. If one were permitted to use such an image, these notes bear witness to their faith in gold even while blaspheming against what they would like to portray as a usurped royalty. <note>The author argues that paper money is hypocritical because it defines its value using the very metals it seeks to replace.</note>

There is nothing "ideal" here, but rather something real and substantial in the services rendered by precious metals. The various qualities that distinguish them and which we have tried to review—their unalterable durability, the ease of their preservation and transport, their flexible nature which allows them to be divided and reunited while adapting to every variation in price, their character of purity and identity, their homogeneous quality—everything, even their very rarity, calls them to serve as intermediary instruments for exchange by allowing them to measure value.

Such is the office of money.

*To measure is to compare one magnitude with another magnitude of the same kind, taken as a unit. Moreover...*

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument regarding the intrinsic nature of money. Having previously argued that paper money is merely a "sign" that points back to precious metals, the author now distinguishes between mere measuring tools (like a ruler) and money, which must possess the same value it measures.</meta>

just as we would not know how to use an object devoid of weight or dimension to determine extent or weight, and as one could not calculate space by means of the kilogram, likewise one can only measure value by a value.

But it is important not to confuse the instrument of measurement with the measured object itself. The thermometer indicates heat, but it is not heat; the scale simply marks weight, but it is not the equivalent of that weight. A meter <note>A unit of length.</note> or a hectoliter <note>A metric unit of volume equal to one hundred liters.</note> are measures of length and capacity, without being confused with the objects they determine, and without replacing them.

It is otherwise for *value*, which is intended to determine other *values*; it is at once the measure and the *equivalent*, the sign and the pledge. It is one thing to possess gold and silver, which measure value, and another to obtain a meter or a hectoliter, which measure length and capacity. For having failed to make this vital distinction—for not having understood that only the equivalent of value allows it to be graduated <note>In this context, "graduated" refers to the ability to scale or divide value into precise, measurable increments.</note>—lofty minds have committed the strangest errors regarding money.

*Value does not exist by itself; it expresses a relationship*; to be estimated, it requires a standard <term>standard (étalon): a recognized requirement or fixed quantity used as a basis for comparison</term> that is at the same time an equivalent. One does not surrender the scale, the meter, or the thermometer when calculating weight, length, or heat; on the contrary, one transfers money—or the sign, which is only worth as much as it represents money and makes it demandable at will.

It is not enough for an object to be *valuable*; in order for it to serve as an instrument of circulation, it must also be easily transmissible and adapt to any quantity of value. These qualities are found to the highest degree in

<summary>The author argues that money is unique among measuring tools because it must possess intrinsic value (an "equivalent") to function, unlike a ruler or thermometer which are separate from what they measure. He asserts that value is a relationship that requires a physical standard—gold or silver—to be effectively traded and divided.</summary>

<keywords>intrinsic value, measurement vs equivalent, monetary standard, instruments of circulation, gold and silver, economic theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 40. Having established that money must be a physical "equivalent" rather than just a measuring tool like a ruler, the author now explains why gold and silver specifically are the only materials suited for this role in human society.</meta>

gold and silver; they are added to the other specificities that distinguish them and which naturally call upon them to fulfill the high function of linking human interests, making them contribute to a common result, and aiding the development of sociability <note>In 19th-century political economy, "sociability" refers to the capacity of humans to form stable, interconnected societies through trade and mutual dependence.</note>, by stimulating both the **division of labor** <term>division of labor (division du travail): the specialized assignment of different parts of a manufacturing process or task to different people in order to improve efficiency</term> and the association of efforts, by means of the instantaneous remuneration of services rendered.

One can review all the objects of creation; no other presents the qualities or offers the advantages of gold and silver as a measure of value. Popular common sense has not been mistaken in this; it rightly disdains perilous fictions <note>The author likely refers here to paper money or speculative financial instruments that lack "intrinsic" value.</note> and clings to the faithful guarantees of reality. This is how commerce between men is maintained and grows.

Doubtless, when comparing epochs far distant from one another, one can observe notable variations in the price of the metal, and consequently in the measure accepted for comparing the value of things. Precisely because money possesses a physical body and is not something ideal, it shares the fate of everything material; it cannot represent something invariable or perfect. What can be said is that it constitutes the least inaccurate way of estimating *value*, and that the defects it presents regarding stability almost entirely disappear when it comes to shorter-term transactions, which constitute the nearly total mass of human transactions. For these spans of time, which are counted in years, the successive influx of newly mined metal—constituting only a minimal fraction of the mass already acquired—exerts only an imperceptible effect on the price, especially in our day. At the time of the discovery of America <note>The "Discovery of America" (1492) led to a massive influx of gold and silver into Europe from the New World, causing a historical period of high inflation known as the Price Revolution.</note>, Europe possessed barely a fortieth of the gold and

<summary>The author argues that gold and silver are uniquely qualified to serve as money because they facilitate the division of labor and provide a stable, physical guarantee of value that "popular common sense" trusts over paper fictions. He acknowledges that while the value of precious metals can fluctuate over centuries (such as after the discovery of the Americas), they remain the most stable and "least inaccurate" measure for the vast majority of human trade.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s discussion on the stability of precious metals as a standard for value. Having previously argued that gold and silver are the most reliable measures, he now addresses the historical dangers of currency manipulation and introduces the economic theories of Nicolaus Copernicus.</meta>

the money it holds today; the occurrence of a few hundred million was enough then to cause a disturbance in prices that billions could not provoke now.

The debasement of currency <note>The author uses "altération," referring to the historical practice of reducing the amount of precious metal in a coin while keeping its face value the same.</note> can alone gravely influence the balance of contracts and agreements. When this *legal fraud* <note>The author uses this provocative term to describe state-sanctioned currency devaluation, which he views as a breach of trust between the government and the people.</note> occurs, good faith is lost, everything is left to chance and insidious maneuvers; the honest man and the laborer are the first victims of the blow thus dealt to the sincerity of relations.

Copernicus <note>While famous as an astronomer, Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543) was also a respected mathematician and economist who wrote "On the Minting of Coin," a treatise on currency debasement.</note> has vigorously demonstrated the dismal results of such abuses. After researching the causes of the depreciation into which Prussian money had fallen, he continues in these terms:

> "However, those whom this concerns look coldly upon this immense ruin of Prussia (1), and their indolence allows this homeland—so sweet to all—to decay and be entirely ruined; this homeland which, after piety towards God, imposes on them the most sacred duties, and for which they should sacrifice even their lives.
> "While the Prussian currency, and consequently the homeland, are afflicted by such vices, only the goldsmiths and those who understand precious metals profit from our misfortunes. They sort out the old coins, which they melt down to sell the silver, always receiving more silver from the inexperienced common people for the same amount of currency. When the old *sous* <term>sous: an old French and European coin name, used here to refer to the Prussian schillings or similar currency units</term> have almost entirely disappeared, they choose the best among..."

(1) One must not forget that Prussia was then a province of Poland. <note>In the early 16th century, Royal Prussia was an autonomous territory under the protection of the Polish King.</note>

<meta>This page continues the translated passage from Nicolaus Copernicus’s treatise on currency. The previous page ended with a description of how goldsmiths profit from melting down undervalued coins, while the general public suffers from a degraded money supply.</meta>

the rest, leaving only the mass of the worst coins. From this comes that incessant complaint heard on all sides, that gold and silver, grain and household provisions, wages, the work of craftsmen, and everything that men usually use, increases in price. But our negligence prevents us from seeing that the high cost of all things stems from the debasement <note>The original term "avilissement" refers to the lowering of the intrinsic value or purity of the coinage.</note> of the currency. Indeed, their price increases and decreases in proportion to the money."

I must also recall another most remarkable passage: it proves the full depth of Copernicus's views and the insight of his mind, which allowed him to grasp one of the fundamental concepts of the science of wealth <note>This "science of wealth" refers to what we now call Political Economy or Economics, which was still in its infancy during Copernicus's time.</note>:

> "We see," he says, "countries that have good money flourish, while those that have only bad money fall into decay and wither away... Weak money feeds laziness far more than it relieves poverty..."

Let us conclude these citations by indicating the importance Copernicus attached to the great question of *monetary unity*. On this subject, he expresses himself as follows:

> "If we wish at last to remedy the misfortunes of Prussia by reforming the currency, we must first prevent the confusion that can result from the diversity of mints <note>Copernicus refers to "ateliers monétaires," the various workshops authorized to strike coins. At the time, decentralized production led to inconsistent quality and weight across the region.</note>. It destroys, in fact, the equality of value, and it is more difficult to keep several mints within the line of duty than just one.

> "We should therefore designate two places in all, one on the lands subject to His Royal Majesty (1), the other on the lands..."

---

(1) The King of Poland.

which are under the power of the prince <note>original: "au pouvoir du prince"</note> (1). In the first mint <note>The author uses "atelier" (workshop), referring to the facilities where coins were struck.</note>, a currency would be struck which, on one side, would bear the royal insignia, and on the other, those of the land of Prussia. In the second, the currency would bear the royal insignia on one side, and the image of the prince on the other, *so that both currencies might be under the control of the royal power, and that they may have legal tender and be accepted throughout the whole kingdom* (of Poland) by virtue of a decree from His Majesty; this would be of no small importance for the reconciliation of minds and for mutual transactions.

(2) A singular comparison could be made here. > "Money does not receive its value from public authority: the stamp marks its weight and its fineness, but it does not give it value. It is the material itself that constitutes its value."

—finds, he says, a decisive proof in the Polish currency, which thus preserves its value <note>original: "son prix"</note> across the vast expanse of the kingdom."

I need not insist further: as you can see, the same thought that inspired Copernicus when he assigned the Earth and the Sun their true places in the vast spaces of the universe also guided him when he determined the role that money is called to play amidst the complexity of social interests, which it must serve.

The great modern idea is expressed in these terms: *labor creates wealth*. Now, money is the most powerful instrument of labor and of the coordination of efforts. Thus, the Copernicus of the laws of production, Adam Smith <note>Adam Smith (1723–1790) was a Scottish economist often called the "Father of Capitalism"; the author compares his foundational work in economics to Copernicus's foundational work in astronomy.</note>, after making human labor—the fruit of liberty—the point around which social wealth gravitates, was not mistaken about the true role of money.

The genius of Copernicus, after unveiling the celestial harmonies, allowed him to glimpse one of the essential aspects of social harmonies; these have since shone with a pure radiance, thanks to the powerful minds of Adam Smith and Frédéric Bastiat <note>Frédéric Bastiat (1801–1850) was a French classical liberal economist famous for his "Economic Harmonies," which argued that the interests of different members of society are naturally compatible.</note>. Man, the goal of production, is also its primary source; he pours his intelligence and his soul into the external objects he appropriates for his use. The world of the spirit fertilizes, disciplines, and governs the world of matter. To accomplish this great work, a common language of interests is needed, just as a common language of ideas is. Gold and silver, by leading us from the known to the unknown, allow us to measure *value* and instantly appreciate services rendered; they provide the most precious bond that brings men together through the attraction of mutual advantage, and they multiply products by facilitating

the satisfaction of needs; they thus render distinguished services to human association.

Money regularized exchanges; its effective assistance allowed circulation to develop with such marvelous ease that production took off rapidly, bringing comfort and prosperity everywhere. The higher role of civilization consists in part in the organization—both solid and flexible—of the means to carry out exchanges, which provide the momentum for labor <note>The author uses the term "travail" to encompass both the physical act of working and the broader economic concept of labor.</note>. As civilization grows, the relationships between people expand and diversify; money, by providing the facility to specify the value of products at any moment, accelerates their multiplication. It enables man to utilize every hour, every minute, by ensuring a legitimate and immediate reward for the service rendered.

Gold and silver, therefore, constitute an instrument that costs much less than it yields—a solid instrument for the circulation of products and a powerful lever for their creation. By prompting the division of labor <note>The "division of labor" is the economic principle where tasks are specialized to increase efficiency, a concept popularized by Adam Smith.</note> and the pooling of efforts, metal becomes the most active agent for applying the three principles upon which, thank God! the world rests today: labor, liberty, and justice!

# ON THE METHOD OF MINTING MONEY <note>original: "Monete Cudende Ratio"</note>

->BY<-

## NICHOLAS [COPERNICUS].

---

Although there are countless scourges <note>original: "pestes"; the author uses this strong term to describe social and economic disasters as if they were biological plagues.</note> through which kingdoms, principalities, and republics <term>republics: from the Latin "respublica," referring generally to the state or the "commonwealth" rather than a specific form of government</term> are accustomed to decline, there are, in my judgment, four that are most powerful: civil strife, death, the barrenness of the soil, and the debasement of the currency. The first three are so obvious that no one is unaware of them; but the fourth, which concerns money, is considered by only a few and only the most insightful, because it does not overthrow the state by a single blow all at once, but gradually, through a certain hidden process.

Now, money is stamped gold or silver, used to count the prices of things to be bought and sold according to the decree of whatever state or ruler. Money is, therefore, a sort of common measure for valuations. However, something that is meant to be a measure must always maintain a firm and fixed standard. Otherwise, the order of the state must necessarily be thrown into confusion, and both buyers and sellers will be defrauded in many ways, just as if the yardstick, the bushel, <note>a "modius" or bushel was a standard unit of volume for grain</note> or the weight did not maintain a fixed quantity. This, therefore

# TREATISE ON MONEY

->BY<-

## NICHOLAS COPERNICUS (1). <note>Copernicus (1473–1543), while famous for his work in astronomy, was also a diplomat and economist. He wrote this treatise at the request of the Polish King to address the monetary chaos in the region of Prussia.</note>

---

However innumerable the scourges may be that usually bring about the decline of kingdoms, principalities, and republics <term>republics: from the Latin "res publica," referring here to the state or the common good of the people</term>, the following four are, in my view, the most formidable: discord, mortality, the barrenness of the earth, and the deterioration of the currency. As for the first three, their evidence is such that no one is ignorant of them. But for the fourth, which concerns money, except for a few men of very great insight, few people concern themselves with it. Why? Because it does not ruin the State all at once, but little by little, through a sort of latent <note>meaning "hidden" or "under the surface"</note> action.

Gold or silver marked with a stamp constitutes the money used to determine the price of things that are bought and sold, according to the laws established by the State or the prince. Money is therefore, in a way, a common measure for estimating values; but this measure must always be fixed and conform to the established rule. Otherwise, there would necessarily be disorder in the State: buyers and sellers would be deceived at every turn, as if the ell <term>ell: an old measurement for fabric, typically the length of a man's arm</term>, the bushel <term>bushel: a standard unit of volume for measuring grain</term>, or the weight did not maintain a certain fixed quantity. Now, this measure resides,

<summary>Nicholas Copernicus begins his treatise by ranking the debasement of currency alongside war and plague as a primary cause of national decline. He defines money as a crucial, fixed measure of value that must remain stable to ensure fair trade and social order.</summary>
<keywords>Nicholas Copernicus, monetary debasement, economic stability, measure of value, statecraft, inflation, weights and measures</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicholas Copernicus's treatise on the nature of money. Following his introduction of currency as a "measure" of value, he now moves into the practical reasons for its existence—the convenience of coins over raw bullion—and explains the technical reasons for mixing precious metals with copper.</meta>

I consider the estimation <term>estimation: the face value or nominal value assigned to a coin by the state</term> of money itself to be its measure; and although this is based on the quality of its material, its value must nonetheless be distinguished from its estimation. For money can be valued at more than the material it consists of, and vice versa. <note>original: "potest enim pluris estimari moneta quam ejus qua constat materia et e converso." Copernicus is highlighting the difference between "face value" (what the law says a coin is worth) and "intrinsic value" (what the metal inside is worth).</note>

The reason for the establishment of money is a necessary one. Although the exchange of goods could have been carried out by the weight of gold and silver alone—since, by the common consent of men, gold and silver are held in value everywhere—it was nonetheless inconvenient to always carry weights around. Furthermore, the purity of gold and silver is not immediately recognizable to everyone. Therefore, it was decreed that money should be marked with a public seal <term>seal: the official stamp of the mint or the sovereign</term>. This signifies that a just quantity of gold or silver is contained within, and invites trust in the governing authority.

It is also customary to mix copper with money, especially silver, for two reasons (as I see it). First, so that the coins are less exposed to the schemes of those who would scrape them or melt them down, which would happen if they consisted of pure silver. Second, so that when a mass of silver is broken into tiny parts and small denominations <term>scrupulos: literally "scruples," referring here to very small fractions or tiny coins</term>, the coins might retain a convenient physical size through the addition of the copper. A third reason can be added: so that the coins do not wear out too quickly through constant use, but might last longer through the support of the copper.

A just and fair valuation of money is when it contains slightly less gold or silver than what can be bought for it—namely, only as much less as is required to cover the expenses of min- <note>The original Latin "mo-" is a fragment, likely intended to be "monetandi," meaning the costs of minting or coining the metal.</note>

<summary>Copernicus defines the difference between a coin's face value and its metal content while explaining why societies moved from weighing raw bullion to using stamped coins. He also provides a technical defense for using copper alloys to prevent the melting of coins and to ensure they are durable and easy to handle.</summary>
<keywords>monetary theory, face value, intrinsic value, copper alloy, minting, public trust, silver, gold</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "estimation" (face value) versus the intrinsic value of the metal. ...in my opinion, regarding the estimation <term>estimation: the face value or nominal value assigned to a coin by the state</term> of money. Although this estimation is based on the quality of the material, it must nevertheless be distinguished from the value itself. Indeed, money can be valued at more than the material of which it is made, and vice versa. <note>Copernicus is highlighting the difference between "face value" (what the law says a coin is worth) and "intrinsic value" (what the metal inside is worth). This distinction is central to his theories on inflation.</note>

The establishment of money was born of necessity. Although exchanges could have been conducted by simply weighing gold and silver—since these metals, by the unanimous consent of men, are everywhere considered precious things—there would nonetheless be many inconveniences in always being obliged to carry weights. Furthermore, as not everyone is capable of recognizing the purity of gold and silver at a glance, it was agreed everywhere to have money marked by authority with an imprint <term>imprint: the official stamp or "face" of the coin struck by the mint</term> intended to express how much gold and silver each piece contains and to serve as a guarantee of public trust.

It is customary to mix copper into money, especially silver coins. I see two main reasons for this: first, so that they are less susceptible to being withdrawn or melted down, which would happen if they were made of pure silver. Second, so that when a silver piece is divided into smaller parts and even very tiny coins, it maintains a convenient size thanks to the alloy—that is, the copper mixed in. <note>Without copper, a small-denomination silver coin would be so tiny it would be easily lost; the copper adds "bulk" without adding precious metal value.</note> To these two reasons, a third can be added: as money is worn down by constant circulation, it is reinforced by a copper alloy, which makes it last longer.

Money is valued at its true rate when it contains slightly less gold or silver than the quantity of these metals it is intended to purchase, precisely as much

<meta>This page follows the introduction to the "estimation" of money, specifically why copper is added to silver to prevent coins from being melted down and to keep them at a manageable size for daily use. ...should be deducted from the mints. For the official mark <term>mark: the stamped imprint or seal of the issuing authority</term> ought to add some dignity <note>original: "dignitatem" — here meaning value, prestige, or official status</note> to the material itself.

This status loses its value for the most part because of an excessive quantity; for instance, if so great a supply of silver has been converted into coinage that people desire silver bullion more than the coins themselves. In this way, the dignity of the money perishes, because it is no longer possible to buy as much silver with the coins as the money itself contains. One then perceives a greater profit in melting down the silver by destroying the money. The remedy for this is to strike no more money until the currency has equalized itself and is once again rendered more valuable than silver bullion.

Value is also corrupted in many ways: either due to a defect of the material alone—namely, when more copper than is proper is mixed with the silver while keeping the weight of the coin the same—or due to a deficiency in weight, even if the mixture of copper and silver is correct. Or, what is worst of all, through both defects at the same time. Value also fails on its own and is worn down by long use, for which reason alone the coinage ought to be restored and renewed. A sign of this is if the silver found in the money is significantly less in quantity than what was paid for it, which is rightly understood as a deficiency in the coinage.

Having set forth these general premises concerning money, let us turn specifically to the Prussian <note>Prussia here refers to Royal Prussia, a province of the Kingdom of Poland where Copernicus lived and served.</note> currency, showing first how it has reached such worthlessness <note>original: "levitatem" — literally "lightness," referring to the debasement or loss of intrinsic value.</note>.

It circulates under the names of **marks**, **scots**, and so on; and there are under these same names also weig... <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "pon-" likely for "pondera" (weights).</note>

13

that must be deducted to pay the costs of minting <term>minting: the process of manufacturing coins from raw metal</term>. The official stamp of guarantee adds some value to the material itself. Currency loses its value especially when it has been over-multiplied—when, for example, such a large quantity of silver has been transformed into coinage that people begin to seek out silver bullion <term>bullion: silver or gold in the form of bars or ingots rather than coins</term> more than the currency itself. Money loses all its dignity <note>original: "dignité" — in this context, it refers to the legal and symbolic value granted by the state</note> when it can no longer buy as much silver as it contains and when there is a profit to be made by melting it down. The only remedy then is to strike no more money until it has regained its balance and has reclaimed a value higher than that of silver.

The value of money depreciates for various reasons: either by the alteration of the fineness <term>fineness: the proportion of pure precious metal in the coin's alloy</term>, when the same weight contains a copper alloy that exceeds the intended measure; or because the weight is lacking, even though the alloy was introduced at the proper degree; or, what is worst of all, when both defects occur at the same time. The value of money is also lost on its own as a result of long service, which wears down the metal and diminishes its quantity; this reason alone is enough to justify putting new money into circulation. One recognizes this necessity by an infallible sign: when the silver contained in the coins weighs notably less than the silver intended to be acquired with them. It is understood that a deterioration of the currency results from this.

Having provided these general principles regarding money, let us move to the specific study of Prussian money and show how it has become so debased.

It circulates under the names of marks, *scots* <note>original: "scotes" — a unit of currency and weight used in Northern Europe, also known as skoter</note> (2), etc. The same denominations also designate weights; the mark...

...weights. For a **mark** of weight is half a **pound**. But a **mark** of account <note>original: "marcha numeri" — refers to a specific number of coins rather than a weight of metal</note> consists of 60 **shillings** (*solidi*): all of which are commonly known. But lest the ambiguity between number and weight cause confusion, whenever "mark" is mentioned hereafter, let it be understood as the number of coins; by the name of "pound," understand the weight of two marks, and for "half-pound" (*selibra*), take the weight of one mark.

We find, therefore, in ancient records and literary monuments that under the administration of **Conrad von Jungingen** <note>Grand Master of the Teutonic Knights (1393–1407). His reign is often viewed as the peak of the Order's economic power.</note>, that is, shortly before the **Battle of Tannenberg** <note>Also known as the Battle of Grunwald (1410), a catastrophic defeat for the Teutonic Order that triggered their economic decline.</note>, a half-pound—that is, a mark of weight—of pure silver was purchased for two Prussian marks and 8 **scots** <term>scot: a unit of currency and weight used in Prussia; 24 scots made up one Prussian mark</term>. This was at the time when three parts of pure silver were mixed with a fourth part of copper, and from a half-pound of this mass, they produced 112 shillings. When a third part was added to this—which is 37 shillings and a third of a shilling—it makes a total sum of 148 shillings and 2 pence, weighing two-thirds of a pound (*bessem*), which is 32 scots of silver; this amount undoubtedly contained three parts of pure silver (equivalent to a half-pound). But it has already been stated that the price was 140 shillings per half-pound. The remainder, which was short by 9 and one-third shillings, was covered by the **valuation** <term>valuation: the legal value assigned to a coin by the state, which includes the cost of minting and the state's profit</term> of the money. Thus, its legal valuation was appropriately aligned with its intrinsic metal value.

Coins of this type belong to **Henry**, **Ulrich**, and **Conrad** <note>Refers to the Grand Masters Heinrich von Plauen, Ulrich von Jungingen, and Conrad von Jungingen</note>, which are still occasionally found in treasuries today. Then, after the defeat of **Prussia** and the aforementioned war, the ruin of the state began to increase more and more every day...

But, so that the same name given to the currency and the weight does not become a cause of confusion, wherever, in the following sections, we speak of a ***mark***, it must be understood as the currency; when we say ***the pound***, it will refer to the weight of 2 marks, and the half-pound will mean the mark by weight.

We find in ancient deliberations and in written documents that under the administration of **Conrad von Jungingen** <note>Grand Master of the Teutonic Knights from 1393 to 1407.</note>, shortly before the **Battle of Tannenberg** (the year 1410), the half-pound—that is, the mark of pure silver—was worth 2 Prussian marks and 8 **scots** <term>scot: a unit of currency and weight used in Prussia; 24 scots made up one Prussian mark</term>; to three parts of pure silver was then added a quarter part of copper, and from a half-pound of this alloy, 112 **shillings** <note>original: "sous" — referring to the *solidus* coin</note> were minted. By adding a third to this—that is, 37 1/3 shillings—one obtains a total of 149 1/3 shillings (weighing 2/3 of a pound, which is 32 scots of silver) which evidently contain 3/4 pure silver, or the equivalent of a half-pound of fine metal. We have already said that the half-pound of pure silver was worth 140 shillings. The 9 1/3 shillings of excess correspond to the face value added by the minting process. In this manner, the nominal price was maintained in a proper relationship with the intrinsic value.

Such were the coins from the time of the (Grand Masters) **Heinrich**, **Ulrich**, and **Conrad**; they are still encountered from time to time in hoards. Later, after the defeat suffered by Prussia and the war of which we have spoken, the decline of the State, in respect to its currency, became more apparent day by day. <meta>This page continues the technical history of the Prussian currency's decline. page 56

...to appear in the coinage. For the **shillings** <note>original: "solidi"</note> of **Henry** <note>Heinrich von Plauen, Grand Master from 1410 to 1413.</note>, though they appeared similar to those mentioned before, are found to contain no more than three-fifths silver. This error grew until the ratio was reversed: they began to mix three parts copper with only one part silver, so that it could no longer be called silver money, but more accurately copper money. Nevertheless, it still retained the weight of 112 shillings to the half-pound.

Since it is hardly suitable to introduce new, high-quality money while the older, debased money remains in circulation, how much greater was the error here? By introducing a cheaper new currency while the older, better one remained, they not only corrupted the old coins but, so to speak, drove them out of use entirely. <note>This is an early observation of what we now call "Gresham’s Law"—the economic principle that "bad money drives out good."</note> 

When they wished to remedy this error during the administration of **Paul von Rusdorf** <note>original: "Michaelis Rusdorff" — likely referring to the Grand Master who reigned 1422–1441</note> and restore the coinage to its former, better state, they minted new shillings which we now call **gros** <note>original: "grossos"</note>. However, because it seemed that the older, cheaper coins could not be abolished without a financial loss, they remained in use alongside the new ones—a significant mistake.

The two old shillings were exchanged for one new one, and it happened then that a "double mark" was imposed upon the common people: namely, a mark of the new shillings and a mark of the old. The former was called the "new" or "good" mark, while the latter was the "old" or "light" mark, though both consisted of sixty shillings. The **halfpennies** <note>original: "oboli," referring to the pfennig or small denarius</note> remained in their usual use. Consequently, only six were exchanged for an old shilling, but twelve were required for a new one. From this, one can easily infer that from the beginning, a shilling consisted of twelve halfpennies. For just as we commonly use the word ***mandel*** <term>mandel: a traditional German counting unit representing fifteen items</term> to refer to the number fifteen, so in most German lands the word ***schilling*** persists as a name for the number twelve. This persevered...

page 57

...contain no more than three-fifths silver. This debasement <note>original: "faiblage" — the reduction of precious metal content in coins.</note> increased until they arrived, by a reverse process, at mixing three parts copper with only one-quarter silver; from then on, it would have been more accurately described if one had spoken of copper money, rather than silver money. The weight of 112 **shillings** <note>original: "sous"</note> still corresponded, however, to the half-pound.

While it is by no means suitable to introduce a new and good currency when the old is bad and continues to circulate, a much more serious error is committed by introducing, alongside an old currency, a new and even weaker one; the latter does not merely depreciate the old, but, so to speak, drives it out by force. <note>This describes the economic principle that "bad money drives out good."</note> 

Under the administration of **Michael Rusdorff** <note>Referring to Paul von Rusdorf, Grand Master of the Teutonic Order from 1422 to 1441.</note> (1439), they wished to remedy this evil and return the currency to its former state of purity. They struck new shillings, those which we today call ***gros*** <note>original: "grossos" — a thicker, high-value silver coin.</note>. But because they did not believe they could—due to the financial loss that would have resulted—withdraw the old coins, which were not worth as much, they committed a greater fault by allowing them to remain in circulation alongside the new ones. Two old shillings were exchanged for one new one, and a "double **mark**" <note>original: "marc"</note> existed on the market: namely, the mark of the new shillings and the mark of the old. 

The "new mark" of the former (the "good") and the "old mark" of the latter (the "weak") were both divided into 60 shillings. As for the **obols** <note>A small denomination coin, typically the smallest unit of account.</note>, they kept their usual value, so that for one old shilling only 6 were given, whereas 12 were required for a new one. Originally, the shilling consisted of 12 obols; this is easy to understand, for just as we commonly use the word ***mandel*** <note>A traditional Germanic unit of quantity representing the number 15.</note> for the number 15, likewise in many Germanic provinces the...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the debasement of Prussian currency, shifting from the general history of the metal content to the specific transition of "new shillings" into "scots" and "groats." It details how the decentralization of minting rights to cities further degraded the silver purity of the coinage.</meta>

page 58

The name of these **new shillings** <note>original: "novorum solidorum"</note> has endured until our own time; later, I will explain how they eventually came to be called **groats** <note>original: "grossi" — a thicker, high-value silver coin</note>.

Thus, eight **marks** <note>original: "marche"</note> of these new shillings—at sixty coins per mark—contained one **pound** <note>original: "libram"</note> of pure silver. This is quite evident from their composition. They consist of half **copper** <note>original: "eris"</note> and half silver, and eight marks of them (at sixty coins each) weigh nearly two pounds. The old coins, however, were equal to the new ones in weight, as previously mentioned, but worth only half as much in value. Since they contained only one-quarter silver, sixteen marks were produced from a single pound of pure silver, resulting in a total weight four times greater than the silver content. Later, as the state of the country changed and the cities were granted the power to mint their own money, they began to exercise this new privilege; the supply of money increased in quantity, but certainly not in quality <note>original: "bonitate" — literally "goodness," referring to the purity or intrinsic value of the metal</note>. They began mixing four parts copper with a fifth part of silver in the old shillings, until eventually twenty marks were exchanged for one pound of silver. 

In this way, because those "new shillings" were now worth more than double the value of the recent ones, they were reclassified as **scots** <note>original: "scoti" — a Prussian unit of weight and currency value equal to 1/24th of a mark</note>. Consequently, twenty-four of them were counted toward a "light mark." This meant that a fifth of the currency’s intrinsic value within the mark was lost. Later, after these "new shillings"—now called scots—began to disappear because they were being accepted as currency even in the **March** <note>likely referring to the Margraviate of Brandenburg</note>, it was decided to revalue them at the rate of a groat. This set their value at three shillings—a massive error and one entirely unworthy of the council of the leading men. It was as if **Prussia** could not exist without them, even though they were worth no more than fifteen **pennies** <note>original: "denariis"</note> of the currency then in circulation, the sheer volume of which was already weighing down the economy.

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the debasement of Prussian currency. It explains the transition from "new shillings" to "scots" and eventually "groats," detailing how the silver content per pound decreased as cities gained minting privileges and the money supply was inflated.</meta>

The word *shilling* <note>original: "schilling"</note> refers to the number 12. This name for the "new shillings" was preserved until our own day.

I will explain later how they were transformed into *groats* <note>original: "gros" — a "thick" silver coin of higher value than a standard penny</note>.

Eight **marks** of the new shillings (at sixty shillings per mark) contained one **pound** of pure silver, as is easy to calculate. Indeed, they were composed of half **copper** and half silver. These eight marks (at the rate of sixty shillings per mark) weigh nearly two pounds. As for the *old* shillings, although they represent the same weight, they are worth half as much. Since they contained only one-quarter silver, it took 16 marks to equal a pound of fine silver, which weighed four times as much as the silver content itself. 

Following the changes that occurred in the country—when the cities acquired the right to mint coins (3) and exercised this new privilege—the currency increased in quantity but not in value. They began to mix only one-fifth part silver with four parts copper in the old shillings, so that a pound of silver was represented by 20 marks. The new shillings were thus worth more than double the old ones; they were therefore turned into **scots** <note>original: "scotes" — a unit of currency used in the Baltic region and Prussia</note>, of which 24 were counted to one "weak" mark: the currency lost one-fifth of its intrinsic value per mark. 

But as the new shillings, having become *scots*, increasingly disappeared—because they were accepted throughout the entire extent of the **March** <note>original: "Marche" — referring here to the March of Brandenburg or the border territories of the Teutonic Order</note>—they were assigned the value of **groats**, that is to say, three shillings. This was done even though they had no greater value than fifteen **pence** <note>original: "deniers" — the smallest common silver coin, from the Latin "denarius"</note> of the current currency, the excessive quantity of which was depressing the price. This decision was reached through a most serious error, entirely unworthy of such an assembly of the most notable citizens, as if the

...by its own estimation. Therefore, the **groats** <note>original: "grossi" — a "thick" silver coin of higher value</note> differed from the **shillings** <note>original: "solidi"</note>, being worth a fifth or sixth part less than the established rate. Through this deceptive and unfair valuation, they diminished the dignity <note>In this context, "dignity" refers to the intrinsic value or purity of the coin</note> of the shillings. Perhaps it was necessary for the injury to be avenged in this way—the same injury that the shillings had previously inflicted on the groats when they forced them to become **scots** <note>original: "scotos" — a unit of currency used in Baltic trade</note>. 

But woe to you, **Prussia**, who—alas!—pay the penalty for a poorly managed **republic** <note>original: "reipublice" — the author refers to the Prussian state as a commonwealth or public entity</note> with your own destruction. Thus, while both the estimation and the value of the money were vanishing everywhere, the minting of coins did not cease entirely. Since funds were insufficient to make the later coinage equivalent to the former, a coin worse than the one before was always introduced, which suppressed and extinguished the quality of the preceding one. This continued until the estimation of the shillings proportionally matched the value of the groats, and 24 "weak" **marks** <note>original: "marche leves" — "weak" marks refer to those with lower silver content compared to "heavy" or "strong" marks</note> were exchanged for one **pound** of **silver**.

At the very least, some small remnant of the currency's dignity should have remained, since no thought was given to its restoration. But the habit that grew over time—or rather, the license to adulterate, plunder, and corrupt the **coinage**—could not stop, nor does it stop to this day. For it is shameful and painful to say what kind of money was produced afterward and what its current state is. Indeed, it has collapsed into such worthlessness today that 30 marks hardly contain a single pound of silver. 

What remains if help does not come, except that Prussia—emptied of gold and silver—will henceforth have a purely **copper** currency? Consequently, the importation of foreign goods and all **commerce** <note>original: "negociationes"</note> will soon perish. For what foreign merchant would...

There was thus a difference of a fifth or a sixth less than the established value between the **groats** <note>original: "gros"</note> and the **shillings** <note>original: "sous"</note>, and through this false and unjust valuation, the groats depreciated the shillings. The **shillings** thus atoned for the wrong they had originally done to the **groats** by forcing them to be changed into **scots** <note>original: "scotes" — a unit of currency used in Baltic trade</note>.

Woe to you, land of Prussia, who—alas!—pay for the faults of a bad government with your ruin! Although the face value <note>original: "valeur d’estime" — the nominal value assigned by the state</note> and the real value of the money vanished simultaneously in this way, they continued to manufacture more coins. But as the costs of minting were not covered, the money grew steadily worse, successively degrading the existing currency, so that the value of the **shillings** and that of the **groats** eventually leveled out proportionally, and one finally ended up paying for a pound of pure silver at the price of 24 **weak marks** <note>A "weak mark" refers to a unit of currency with a reduced silver content compared to the "heavy" standard</note>.

Such were bound to be the results of the deterioration of the currency, whose fineness <note>original: "titre" — the proportion of pure precious metal in the alloy</note> no one thought to restore. The inveterate habit of melting down and falsifying the money in every way has not yet ceased in our day. What will become of this money, and what it has already become, one is ashamed and pained to say: it is so debased today that 30 marks barely contain a pound of silver. What will happen if no remedy is applied? Prussia, stripped of gold and silver, will have nothing left but a copper currency, which will halt foreign imports and ruin all trade. Indeed, what foreign merchant would want to exchange goods for copper money? And who among us will be able to, in other

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Copernicus's analysis of the debasement of Prussian currency. Who will want to exchange their goods for copper money <note>original: "moneta cuprea"</note>? Finally, who among our people will be able to purchase foreign goods in distant lands with this same money? Yet those whom it concerns look down with contempt upon this massive disaster for the Prussian state <note>original: "reipublice prussiane"</note>; through their listless negligence, they allow their most beloved homeland—to which, after their devotion to God, they owe not only their greatest duty but even their very lives—to slide miserably and perish more and more every day.

Since Prussian currency, and the whole country along with it, suffers from such great defects, only the <term>goldsmiths<note>original: "aurifices" — in this period, goldsmiths often acted as unofficial money-changers and assayers because they possessed the tools to test metal purity.</note></term> and those who are experts in the quality of metal benefit from its distress. For they collect the old coins from the mixed money, from which they sell the melted-down silver <note>original: "argentum"</note>, always receiving more silver from the unskilled common people in exchange for the mixed currency. But once those old **shillings** <note>original: "solidi"</note> have completely disappeared, they choose the next best ones, leaving behind a heap of even worse coins. 

From this comes that common and perpetual complaint: that gold <note>original: "aurum"</note>, silver, grain prices <note>original: "annonam"</note>, household wages, the labor of craftsmen, and whatever is standard for human use, have exceeded their normal price. Yet, in our dazed state, we do not consider that the high cost of all things arises from the worthlessness of the currency. For prices rise and fall according to the condition of the money—especially gold and silver, which we value not in brass or copper, but in gold and silver. For we say that gold and silver are like the foundation of the currency, upon which its valuation <note>original: "estimatio"</note> rests.

But perhaps someone will argue that a meager currency is suited for use...

While the Prussian currency, and consequently the homeland, are afflicted by such vices, only the <term>goldsmiths<note>original: "orfévres" — In the 16th century, goldsmiths often acted as unofficial money-changers and assayers because they possessed the tools to test metal purity.</note></term> and those who are knowledgeable in precious metals profit from our misfortunes. From this arises that constant complaint heard on all sides: that gold and silver, grain, domestic provisions, and the labor of craftsmen—everything people normally use—is increasing in price. Our negligence prevents us from seeing that the high cost of all things stems from the <term>debasement of the coinage<note>original: "l’avilissement du numéraire" — The process of reducing the amount of precious metal in a coin while maintaining its face value, leading to inflation.</note></term>. In effect, their price increases and decreases in proportion to the money, especially that of precious metals, which we value not in brass or copper, but in gold and silver; for gold and silver constitute the foundation of money and determine its value.

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Copernicus’s economic treatise. Following the discussion on how debased currency causes inflation and benefits only money-changers, Copernicus now addresses the common arguments used to defend poor-quality coinage. He refutes the idea that "cheap" money helps the poor or the agricultural class, arguing instead that a stable, high-value currency is the only foundation for a flourishing state.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>

[Some argue that debased money] is more convenient for human use: namely, that it assists the poverty of the people, provides grain at a low price, and more easily supplies the other necessities of mortal life. They claim that with good money, however, everything becomes more expensive, and farmers and those burdened with annual taxes are weighed down more than is usual. 

Those who have hitherto been granted the right to mint coins will praise this opinion out of hope for private gain. Perhaps merchants and craftsmen, to whom nothing is lost thereby, will not disapprove of it either, since they sell their goods and wares according to the value of gold; the more <term>debased<note>original: "exilior" — literally "thinner" or "leaner," referring to a coin with a low precious metal content.</note></term> the currency is, the greater the number of coins they receive in exchange for their goods. 

But if they look to the common good, they certainly cannot deny that excellent money is beneficial not only to the state, but also to themselves and to every class of people, while debased money is harmful. Since this is clear enough for many reasons, we also say it is true through experience itself—the mistress of all things. For we see that those lands flourish most which have good money, while those which use worse money decline and perish. 

Prussia certainly flourished in the days when one <term>Prussian mark<note>original: "marcha pruthenicalis" — a unit of account and weight used in the Teutonic state and Royal Prussia.</note></term> was worth two <term>Hungarian florins<note>original: "florenis ungaricis" — a stable gold coin often used as an international benchmark for value.</note></term>, and when, as previously mentioned, two Prussian marks and 8 <term>schots<note>original: "scoti" — a unit of currency and weight equal to 1/24th of a mark.</note></term> were exchanged for a <term>half-pound<note>original: "selibra" — half of a Roman pound, here equated to one mark of weight.</note></term>, that is, a mark of pure silver. But in the meantime, as the currency becomes more worthless day by day, our homeland is declining; by this plague and other calamities, it has been brought almost to its final destruction.

It is further evident that those very places which use good money...

--- Page 283 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicholas Copernicus’s argument against the debasement of currency. Having just summarized the arguments used by proponents of "cheap money"—who claim it helps the poor—Copernicus now refutes these claims by contrasting private gain with the public good and citing the historical prosperity of Prussia as evidence.</meta>

<page-num>65</page-num>

[Good] money, on the contrary, makes everything more expensive; it overburdens farmers, <term>rent-payers<note>original: "censitaires" — individuals who paid a fixed annual rent (cens) to a landlord, often in a feudal context.</note></term>, and all those who have payments to make." This opinion will please those who would be deprived of a significant profit if the right to mint coins were taken away from them. Perhaps it will also be the opinion of merchants and artisans, who suffer no loss in selling their goods and products regardless of the price of gold; for the more the currency is <term>debased<note>original: "avilie" — referring to the reduction of precious metal content in a coin.</note></term>, the more of it they demand for their wares and their labor. But by looking toward the common good, they cannot deny that sound money is advantageous, not only to the State, but also to themselves and to people of every status, whereas defective money is greatly harmful.

A great number of proofs make this evident, and experience—that most reliable guide—fully demonstrates the truth of it. Indeed, we see those countries flourish that possess sound money, while those that have only bad money fall into decay and perish. Prussia, too, was flourishing when one <term>Prussian mark<note>original: "marc pruthénien" — a local unit of currency and weight.</note></term> was worth two Hungarian florins (<term>ducats<note>High-quality gold coins widely used in international trade.</note></term>), and when—as we said above—two Prussian marks and eight <term>scotes<note>original: "scotes" — a small denomination; there were 24 scotes in one mark.</note></term> were exchanged for half a pound, that is, for one mark of pure silver. But the increasing debasement of our currency brings about the decline of the fatherland, which, struck by this plague and other calamities, is reaching almost to the gates of the grave.

It is undeniable that countries which use sound money shine through the arts, possess the best craftsmen, and have an abundance of everything. On the contrary, in those States that use a degraded currency,

<summary>Copernicus argues that while debased currency may seem to benefit certain merchants in the short term, it ultimately destroys the state's prosperity. He uses the historical economic decline of Prussia as a direct example of how losing a stable silver standard leads to national ruin.</summary>

<keywords>monetary debasement, common good, Prussian mark, inflation, economic history, ducats, scotes, silver standard</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page continues Nicholas Copernicus’s argument in "Monetae Cudendae Ratio" (Treatise on the Minting of Money). Having established that sound money leads to national prosperity, he now contrasts this with the social and moral decay caused by debasement, using the historical economic shifts in Prussia as his primary evidence.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

[We see countries with sound money] thrive with arts and excellent craftsmen, as well as an abundance of resources. On the other hand, where <term>base money<note>original: "vilis moneta" — currency that has been debased by reducing its precious metal content.</note></term> is in use, through laziness, sloth, and a state of stagnant leisure, the people neglect the cultivation of both the good arts and their own talents, and even the abundance of all things perishes.

It has not yet passed from human memory that grain and provisions were bought for a smaller amount of money in Prussia when <term>sound money<note>original: "bona moneta" — high-quality coinage with consistent weight and purity.</note></term> was still used. Now, however, as the currency becomes debased, we experience the price of all things pertaining to food and human use rising. From this, it can be clearly seen that "light" money feeds laziness more than it assists the poverty of the people. 

Nor can the <term>restoration of the currency<note>original: "monete exaltatio" — literally the "lifting" or revaluation of the money back to a higher standard of purity.</note></term> greatly burden the <term>rent-payers<note>original: "censuales" — individuals who paid a fixed annual rent (cens) to a landlord.</note></term>. For if they seem to pay more than usual to their lords, they will also sell the fruits of the land, their livestock, and similar goods for a higher price. A proportionate measure of money will compensate for the reciprocal change of giving and receiving.

If, therefore, it is finally pleasing to restore Prussia—which has been calamitous until now—by the restoration of its money, we must first and foremost avoid the confusion arising from a variety of different <term>mints<note>original: "officinarum" — literally "workshops," here referring to the facilities where coins are struck.</note></term> in which the money is to be produced. For a multiplicity of locations hinders uniformity; it is a much greater task to keep several mints in the duty of integrity than to manage just one. Therefore, two locations at most should be designated: one in the lands of your Royal Majesty, and the other in the domain of the Prince. In the first, let money be struck which is marked on one side with the royal insignia and on the other with the signs of the lands of Prussia.

cowardice, laziness, and indolence reign; the arts and the cultivation of the mind are neglected, and people suffer the most miserable poverty. We still remember the time when grain and provisions were cheaper in Prussia, back when sound money was used. Now that the currency is debased <note>original: "avili" — reduced in value or purity of precious metal.</note>, we can observe every day how the price has risen for everything used for food and human upkeep. It clearly follows that weak money feeds laziness far more than it alleviates poverty. A currency of higher quality <note>original: "meilleur aloi" — refers to the "alloy" or fineness of the metal.</note> would not even cause any prejudice to those who pay an annual <term>rent<note>original: "cens" — a fixed annual payment made by a tenant to a landlord, often fixed by custom or contract.</note></term> for their estate; indeed, they would also sell the fruits of the earth, livestock, and every kind of produce at a higher price. Exchange involves giving and receiving in turn, and money restores a proportional balance by acting as the compensation.

If we wish to finally remedy the misfortunes of Prussia by reforming the currency, we must first prevent the confusion that can result from a diversity of <term>mints<note>original: "ateliers monétaires" — the physical locations where coins are struck.</note></term>. This diversity, in fact, prevents an equality of value, and it is more difficult to keep several mints within the line of duty than just one. Therefore, two locations in total should be designated: one on the lands subject to the Royal Majesty <note>The King of Poland, who held sovereignty over Royal Prussia.</note>, the other on the lands under the power of the prince <note>The Duke of Prussia, a vassal to the Polish King.</note>. In the first mint, coins would be struck that, on one side, would bear the royal insignia, and on the other, those of the land of Prussia. In the second <term>mint<note>original: "officina" — a workshop; in this context, the physical location where coins are struck.</note></term>, however, let the coin be struck with the royal insignia on one side and the prince’s image on the other, so that both currencies remain subject to the royal authority and, by His Majesty's mandate, may be in use and accepted throughout the entire kingdom. This arrangement will carry no small weight in reconciling the minds of the people and fostering a commonality of trade.

It will be worthwhile, moreover, that these two currencies be of a single <term>standard<note>original: "grani" — literally "grain," referring to the fineness or purity of the precious metal.</note></term>, value, and estimation, and that they persist perpetually through the vigilant care of the <term>authorities<note>original: "prematum reipublice" — the leaders or magistrates of the state.</note></term> according to the ordinance now to be established. Furthermore, the princes on both sides should derive no profit from the minting of the money; instead, only so much copper should be added that the face value <note>original: "estimatio"</note> slightly exceeds the intrinsic value <note>original: "valorem"</note>, so that the <term>production costs<note>original: "impendiorum jactura" — the loss or expenditure required to manufacture the coins.</note></term> can be covered and the opportunity for melting down the money is removed.

Likewise, so that we do not fall once again into the confusion of our current time—which the mixing of new money with old has produced—it seems necessary that as soon as the new currency is issued, the old be abolished and entirely removed from circulation. It should be exchanged at the mints for the new money according to the proportion of its actual value. Otherwise, the labor of renewing the currency will be in vain, and the subsequent confusion will perhaps be worse than the first. The old money will indeed once again infect the dignity of the new; for when mixed together, the total sum will fall short of the <term>just weight<note>original: "justo pondere" — the legally required weight of precious metal.</note></term>, and the same inconvenience mentioned above—resulting from an excessive quantity of poor coins—will follow. ## On the comparison of silver to gold.

It was stated above that gold and silver are the foundation upon which the value of money rests. And what has been explained regarding silver currency can, for the most part, also be applied to gold. It remains for us to explain the ratio for exchanging gold and silver against one another. First, therefore, we must investigate what the <term>valuation ratio<note>original: "ratio appreciationis"</note></term> is of pure gold to pure silver: so that we may descend from the general to the specific, and from simple elements to those that are composite. 

Furthermore, the ratio for uncoined gold and silver is the same as for that which is minted at the same degree of purity; and likewise, the ratio of minted gold to uncoined gold is the same as that of minted silver to uncoined silver, assuming the same degree of alloy and weight. 

The purest gold minted currency found among us is the <term>Hungarian florin<note>original: "floreni ungarici" — high-quality gold coins widely used as a standard in Central Europe during the Renaissance; they were prized for their consistent purity.</note></term>; for these have the smallest amount of alloy—perhaps only as much as was necessary to cover the costs of the <term>mint<note>original: "monetariis" — the facility where coins are manufactured.</note></term>. Thus, they are rightly exchanged for pure gold of the same weight, with the prestige of the official stamp making up for any slight deficiency in the florins. It follows, therefore, that the ratio of pure uncoined silver to pure uncoined gold is the same as the ratio of that same silver to Hungarian florins, provided the weights remain unchanged. 

Now, 110 Hungarian florins of correct and equal weight—specifically 72 <term>grains<note>original: "grana" — a traditional unit of weight for precious metals.</note></term> each—make up one <term>pound<note>original: "libra" — Copernicus specifies here that his "pound" equals two marks.</note></term> (by pound, I always mean that which contains two <term>marks<note>original: "marchas" — a common unit of weight for gold and silver, typically weighing about 8 ounces or 230-250 grams.</note></term> of weight). By this argument, we find commonly among all nations...

<summary>Copernicus defines the fundamental exchange ratio between gold and silver, using the Hungarian florin as the standard for pure gold. He establishes that the value of minted coins should ideally match the value of their raw metal weight plus a small allowance for production costs.</summary>

## *Comparison of silver to gold.*

We have stated that gold and silver are the base upon which the value of money rests. What we have put forward concerning silver money can also, in large part, be applied to gold money. It remains for us to explain the method of the mutual exchange of gold and silver. In order to move from the general to the specific and from the simple to the composite, it is first necessary to know the ratio of the price of pure gold to that of pure silver. It is known that the same proportion exists between pure gold and pure silver as between gold and silver minted at the same <term>fineness<note>original: "titre" — the proportion of pure precious metal contained in a coin or alloy.</note></term>; just as the same proportion applies to minted gold and gold bullion as to minted silver and silver bullion, provided they have the same degree of alloy and represent the same weight.

The purest gold that is found minted among us is that of the Hungarian *ducats*. <note>original: "ducats" — also known as the Hungarian florin, these were prized across Europe for their consistent high purity.</note> There is indeed the least amount of alloy in them, perhaps only as much as was necessary to cover the costs of the minting. Thus, they are usually, with good reason, exchanged for the same weight of pure gold, the guarantee of the official stamp replacing what they lack in weight. It follows that a similar proportion exists, at equal weight, between pure silver and pure gold, and between this same silver and Hungarian *ducats*. 

*One hundred and ten ducats, having the legal weight of 72 <term>grains<note>A traditional unit of weight based on the mass of a single seed of cereal; in this system, 72 grains equaled the weight of one ducat.</note></term>, make a pound. (I always mean by pound the weight of two <term>marks<note>A "marc" or mark was a standard unit of weight for precious metals, typically equal to 8 ounces; Copernicus specifies a pound as two marks (16 ounces).</note></term>.)* 

We thus find among all nations that one pound of pure gold is commonly worth twelve pounds of pure silver. (8) <note>The 12:1 ratio was the prevailing market rate in early 16th-century Europe, though Copernicus would later argue that this ratio was being disrupted by the debasement of silver.</note> But eleven pounds of silver were formerly worth one pound of gold. ### *Epilogue on the Restoration of the Currency.*

Regarding the restoration and preservation of the currency, these points seem worthy of consideration:

*First*, that the currency should not be changed without the mature counsel of the leaders <note>original: "procerum" — the high-ranking officials or nobles of the Prussian estates.</note> and a unanimous decree.

*Second*, that only one place be designated for the <term>mint<note>original: "officina monetaria" — the factory where coins are struck.</note></term>, if possible. There, the money should be struck not in the name of a single city, but in the name of the whole land with its own insignia <note>The coats of arms or official symbols of the territory.</note>. The Polish currency demonstrates the effectiveness of this policy, as for this reason alone it retains its value throughout such a vast extent of territory.

*Third*, that upon the issuance of the new currency, the old be prohibited and abolished.

*Fourth*, that inviolably and unchangeably, perpetually...
