...after mature deliberation, to determine the choice that will seem most advantageous to the State. What I have just said regarding the restoration of the currency should suffice to make clear how the value of the coinage has been degraded, and how it can be restored to it.

### *Epilogue on the Restoration of the Currency.*

To succeed in restoring and preserving a sound currency, several things must be considered:

1. It should not be modified except after mature deliberation by the <term>notables<note>The high-ranking officials or representatives of the Prussian estates.</note></term> and by virtue of their unanimous decision.

2. A single location, if possible, should be chosen for the minting of the currency. It must be struck not in the name of a city, but in the name of the country, bearing the insignia of the State. The effectiveness of such a measure finds decisive proof in the Polish currency, which thus maintains its value throughout the vast extent of the kingdom. <note>Copernicus argues that a unified state currency is stronger than various competing city-issued coins.</note>

3. Upon the issuance of a new currency, the old must be demonetized and suppressed.

4. It must be held as an inviolable and immutable rule to strike only 20 <term>marks<note>A unit of weight used for measuring silver; Copernicus is advocating for a fixed, honest ratio between the weight of silver and the face value of the coins.</note></term>, and no more, from one pound of silver, deducting only the amount necessary for the costs of minting. In this way, the Prussian currency will be brought into alignment with the currency...

<summary>Copernicus summarizes his technical arguments and lists the first four essential rules for monetary reform, emphasizing state-wide uniformity and the withdrawal of old, debased coins. He insists on a fixed ratio of 20 marks per pound of silver to ensure the currency's intrinsic value.</summary>

<keywords>monetary reform, Hungarian ducat, minting, demonetization, silver standard, Royal Prussia, Polish currency, economic stability</keywords>

--- Page 296 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Epilogue on the Restoration of the Currency" from page 77, specifically detailing the fourth rule regarding the weight and value of the new Prussian coinage relative to the Polish standard.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>

...it must be observed that exactly 20 <term>marks<note>original: "marche" — a unit of weight and currency used in Prussia and Poland.</note></term> and no more are to be produced from one pound of pure silver, after deducting what is necessary for the expenses of the <term>minting<note>original: "opificii" — the costs of the labor and the minting process itself.</note></term>. In this way, the Prussian currency will be proportional to the Polish, so that twenty Prussian <term>groschen<note>original: "grossi" — a standard silver coin of the era.</note></term> as well as twenty Polish groschen shall constitute one Prussian mark.

Fifth, one must beware of an excessive quantity of money. <note>Copernicus identifies that over-issuing currency leads to inflation and devaluation.</note>

Sixth, the currency should be issued in all its denominations at once: that is, <term>skoters<note>original: "scoti" — a unit of currency equal to 1/24th of a mark.</note></term> or groschen, <term>shillings<note>original: "solidi" — a smaller denomination.</note></term>, and <term>small coins<note>original: "oboli" — the smallest denomination, similar to a half-penny.</note></term> should be minted together.

*As for how much alloy the mixture should contain—whether groschen and shillings are made, or even silver <term>denarii<note>original: "denarii" — pennies.</note></term> worth a <term>quarter-mark<note>original: "fertonem" — a weight or value equal to one-fourth of a mark.</note></term>, a half-mark, or even a whole mark—this is at the discretion of those concerned; provided that a standard is set and decided upon so that it remains permanent for the future.*

*Consideration must also be given to the small coins, which are currently worth so little that an entire mark of them barely contains the silver value of a single groschen.*

A final difficulty arises from contracts and obligations made both before and after the renewal of the currency. In these cases, a method must be found so that the contracting parties are not overly burdened. This is similar to what was done in former times, as is evident from the records described on the other side of this leaf.

<summary>Copernicus outlines the final rules for monetary reform, emphasizing the need for a fixed silver standard proportional to Poland, the danger of inflation, and the necessity of addressing pre-existing debts after a currency change.</summary>
<keywords>monetary reform, silver standard, groschen, inflation, debt obligations, currency denominations, Prussian mark</keywords>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific rules for the restoration of the Prussian currency, concluding the discussion on the relationship between Prussian and Polish groschen and addressing the practicalities of minting and legal obligations.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>

Polish [currency], so that 20 Prussian <term>groschen<note>original: "gros" — a standard silver coin of the era.</note></term>, as well as 20 Polish groschen, will constitute the <term>Prussian mark<note>original: "marc pruthénien" — a unit of currency and weight used in the Prussian territories.</note></term>.

5º One must avoid an excessive multiplication of the **money supply**. <note>The author argues that too much money in circulation devalues the currency, a core principle of early monetary theory.</note>

6º All subdivisions of the currency shall be issued at the same time; that is, one shall simultaneously mint <term>skoters<note>original: "scotes" — a unit of currency equal to 1/24th of a mark.</note></term>, groschen, <term>shillings<note>original: "sous" — here referring to the "solidus," a smaller denomination.</note></term>, and <term>obols<note>original: "oboles" — the smallest denomination, often containing very little silver.</note></term>.

As for the proportion to be maintained, it will depend on those who strike the coins; they will decide what quantity of groschen and shillings to strike, or even *silver denarii*, which will be worth a <term>ferton<note>original: "ferton" — a unit of value equal to one-quarter of a mark.</note></term> (9), or a half-mark, or even the whole mark, provided that they maintain the same proportion and remain faithful to the rule once established.

One must also take into account the **obols**, whose value is now so low that an entire mark [of them] contains barely as much silver as a single groschen.

A final difficulty arises from past contracts and obligations agreed upon before and after the **reminting of the currency**. It is vital to find a transitional method that prevents the contracting parties from being too greatly harmed. This was provided for in the past in similar circumstances, as shown in the attached document (10).

<summary>This page concludes the author's six rules for currency reform, emphasizing the need for simultaneous minting of all denominations and warning against inflation. It also identifies the legal challenge of how to honor existing debts and contracts following a change in the currency's value.</summary>

--- Page 299 ---
<meta>This page provides editorial commentary and historical annotations for the preceding treatise on monetary reform, specifically clarifying the terminology used for various denominations and their weights across different European systems.</meta>

# NOTES.

---

(1) It might perhaps be more accurate to say: **CONSIDERATIONS ON THE MINTING OF MONEY**; we have preferred a more concise title.

(2) *Skoter <note>original Latin: "Scotus. Monetæ species." — A type of money.</note>* — DUCANGE <note>Charles du Fresne, sieur du Cange (1610–1688), a renowned scholar of medieval history and Latin.</note>. "The petitioner, hoping to become an acquired good friend of Great John, offered to lend him three *skoters* or *testars* to help pay his loss." (*Ibid.*)

The *skoter* <note>original French: "scote"; in Polish: *skojciec*, *skojec*, plural: *skojce*</note> was an ancient weight, worth one-third of an ounce. Since the mark was divided into eight ounces, the skoter formed one-twenty-fourth of the mark. Its weight in fine silver was therefore slightly less than ten grams, the *Cologne mark* <note>A standard unit of weight for precious metals in Northern Europe, weighing approximately 233.8 grams.</note>, taken as the standard in the North, weighing 233 grams.

<margin>¹ Memoirs of the Academy of Inscriptions and Belles-Lettres, Vol. XXI, pp. 348-352.</margin>
The *testars* mentioned in the quote by Ducange as an equivalent to the *skoters* were an English coin. In France, they began striking coins under Louis XII in 1513, on which the king's head was represented, and which were given the name *testons* <note>Derived from the Old French "teste," meaning "head," because the coin featured a realistic profile portrait of the monarch.</note>. These offer an almost complete equivalence with the *skoters*. We find in the learned Memoir of Mr. Natalis de Wailly, regarding the *variations of the livre tournois <note>The "livre tournois" was the standard account currency of the Kingdom of France.</note> since the reign of Saint Louis*¹:
April 6, 1513. Teston, mintage rate <note>original: "taille des espèces"</note>, 25 1/2.
Value of the coin in Tournois currency, 10 shillings <note>original: "sous"</note>.
Value of the minted mark in Tournois currency, 12 pounds <note>original: "livres"</note> 15 shillings.
Price of the mark of raw material in Tournois currency, 12 pounds 10 shillings.
March 5, 1533. The value of the minted mark in Tournois currency was 10 pounds 6 shillings.
That of the mark of raw material, 13 pounds, 7 shillings, 9 pence <note>original: "deniers"</note>.
The intrinsic value of the teston at the legal standard was, in 1513, 2 francs [at] 0.001466978. The monetary standard <note>original: "pied de la monnaie"</note> for silver, at the legal standard, 52 4/47; the intrinsic value, at the tolerance standard <note>The "titre de tolérance" is the minimum purity level allowed by the mint before a coin is rejected.</note>, 1 franc 99.436: the intrinsic value descended, in 1533, to 1.91629.

This was, very nearly, the value of the *skoter*.

<summary>This page contains editorial notes defining historical currency units like the skoter and teston, providing weight comparisons and exchange rates from the 16th century.</summary>
<keywords>numismatics, skoter, teston, Ducange, Natalis de Wailly, livre tournois, Cologne mark, silver weights, monetary standards</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial commentary on European currency systems, specifically focusing on the relationship between the Roman testone and the monetary standards of Poland, Lithuania, and Prussia, such as the scotus and the mark.</meta>

The *testone* is also a currency of the Papal States. The Roman *testone* is worth 1 franc 84 centimes.

Czacki <note>Tadeusz Czacki (1765–1813), a prominent Polish historian and numismatist.</note> states (*Laws of Poland and Lithuania*, Vol. I, p. 144): "When the Teutonic Knights occupied Prussia, they pledged to strike coinage similar to that produced by Saint Louis <note>King Louis IX of France (r. 1226–1270), whose gold "écu" and silver "gros tournois" set high standards for European coinage.</note>, that is, 60 pieces to the mark of fine silver." (Article XL, *Of the Culm Privilege* <note>original Latin: "Privilegii culmensis"; the Chełmno Law granted to the city of Culm in 1233.</note>, 1233-1251.) He adds (Vol. I, p. 181): "The *scotus* or *skojec* is the twenty-fourth part of the mark." And, investigating the origin of this division, he explains it as follows: "It is known that there was a *Slavic* mark, which consisted of twelve *solidi* <note>original Latin/German: "Wichbild, Magdeburgense" — Municipal Law of Magdeburg.</note>. I believe that the *skojec* (*scotus*) was half of that fraction."

> "The *scotus* is the twenty-fourth part of a mark," <note>original Latin: "Scotus est vigesima quarta pars marcæ"</note> repeat the historian Kromer, Braun (*Mintage* <note>original German: "Müntzwesen"</note>), and Hartnoch (*On the Prussian Monetary System* <note>original Latin: "De re nummaria Prussiæ"</note>).

The *scotus* was not known in Germany, but it is frequently mentioned in ancient Polish and Lithuanian documents. Czacki cites (Vol. I, p. 148) a privilege from Bolesław V the Chaste <note>original French: "Boleslas le Pudique", High Duke of Poland from 1243–1279.</note> dating to 1260, which reads: "They are held to pay a fourth part of a *scotus*, namely of common silver." <note>original Latin: "Quartam partem de scoto solvere tenentur argenti videlicet usualis."</note> And (Vol. I, p. 181): "A tax of one silver *scotus*" <note>original Latin: "Censum unius scoti argenti"</note> (1306, Władysław I the Short <note>original French: "Ladislas le Bref", King of Poland.</note>). Lithuania also calculated by *skojce*: these pieces were used until the reign of John I Albert <note>King of Poland from 1492–1501.</note>.

According to a table compiled by Czacki, here is the successive degradation of the *skojce*, the *gros*, and the *mark*, calculated in grains of fine silver:

| Years | Skoiec (scotus) | Gros | Mark |
|-------|-----------------|------|------|
| 1300 | 153 8/12 | 80 | 3,688 |
| 1350 | 131 10/14 | 68 8/14 | 3,161 2/14 |
| 1378-1418 | 115 4/16 | 60 | 2,766 |
| 1505 | 57 20/32 | 30 | 1,383 |

We see a proportional reduction in intrinsic value, but the *scotus* always remains, more or less, the equivalent of one-twenty-fourth of a mark, whether the mark was heavy or light.

(3) Braun (*Report on Polish and Prussian Mintage*, Elbing, 1722, in-4°) says that this right was devolved in 1454 to the cities of Thorn <note>Toruń</note>, Dantzig <note>Gdańsk</note>, Elbing <note>Elbląg</note>, and Kœnigsberg.

(4) The King of Poland.

(5) Since the Treaty of Cracow, concluded in 1525, Margrave Albert had obtained the title of Prince <note>Duke</note> of Prussia.

<summary>This page analyzes the historical weight and value of the scotus (skojec) in Polish and Prussian currency, demonstrating its consistent ratio as one-twenty-fourth of a mark despite the overall devaluation of silver over two centuries.</summary>
<keywords>testone, scotus, skojec, mark, gros, Tadeusz Czacki, Teutonic Knights, Prussian coinage, Polish history, monetary devaluation</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues the editorial commentary on the history of Polish and Prussian monetary reforms, moving from the definitions of specific units like the scotus to the legislative history involving Nicolaus Copernicus and several Polish kings.</meta>

(6) The collection of ideas presented here by Copernicus served as the starting point for the monetary regulation of 1528, which Sigismund I <note>Sigismund I the Old (r. 1506–1548), King of Poland and Grand Duke of Lithuania.</note> finally established after lengthy discussions with the privileged cities.

Already, following the deliberations held at the Diet of Piotrków, King Sigismund had promulgated a monetary regulation (Given at Kraków, Monday the fifteenth of October in the year of our Lord 1526) <note>original Latin: "Datum Cracoviæ, die lunæ quintadecima octobris anno Dni 1526"</note> intended to introduce a more stable order in the manufacture of coinage. (Czacki reports this in Vol. I, p. 158).

The concern of the Polish kings was directed toward this issue. Sigismund Augustus <note>Sigismund II Augustus (r. 1548–1572), the last monarch of the Jagiellonian dynasty.</note> clearly expressed the intent to maintain a good currency in an important document, a treaty made with Ferdinand I of Austria (1549, *Codex diplomaticus* of Dogiel, IV, 219): 

> "Since, finally, it is of the highest importance to us and to our kingdoms and provinces, that we have in our kingdoms and domains a currency that is both good and just." <note>original Latin: "Cum denique nostra regnorumque ac provinciarum nostrarum maximopere intersit, est in regnis et dominiis nostris, bonam eamdemque justam habeamus monetam."</note>

The great king Stephen Báthory <note>King of Poland and Grand Duke of Lithuania (r. 1576–1586).</note> rejected the idea of debasing the currency, a practice so common in Europe at that time. In a letter addressed to Chodkiewicz <note>Jan Hieronimowicz Chodkiewicz, a high-ranking Lithuanian noble and military officer.</note> (March 9, 1570, Załuski Library, no. 454), he wrote these noble words to reject a proposal apparently made in this regard by a German:

> "We do not wish to make ourselves base by using base money. Let that German go to those who seek relief for their poverty through the tears of the wretched." <note>original Latin: "Nolumus vili moneta nos viles reddere. Eat ille Germanus ad quærentes levamen inopiæ, lachrimis miserabilium."</note>

(7) This entire passage is most remarkable.

(8) Following the discovery of rich silver mines, the ratio shifted in favor of gold by more than a quarter.

(9) **Ferto**, a fourth part of a mark of silver. — DUCANGE. — **Ferto**, is the fourth part of a mark. — CZACKI, I, 180. — **Ferto** is the fourth part of a mark. (SCHLEGEL, *On Ancient Coins*.) <note>The original text provides these definitions in Latin and Polish: "Ferto, quarta pars marcæ argenti" and "Ferto, jest czwarta czesc grzywny." The term "ferto" or "wiardunek" was a unit of weight and currency equivalent to 1/4 of a mark.</note>

(10) Copernicus reproduces here a regulation established at Malbork in 1418; this decree determined the method by which the payment of principal and interest due should be carried out, taking into account the change in the monetary standard. In this regulation, one sees a constant effort to restore the balance violently broken by the fluctuation of the currency; it tends to manage the transition by bringing old obligations back to their original rates.

<summary>This page details the historical application of Copernicus's economic theories through the monetary reforms of Polish kings Sigismund I, Sigismund Augustus, and Stephen Báthory. It emphasizes the royal commitment to "just" currency and explains the "ferto" as a fractional unit of the mark.</summary>
<keywords>Nicolaus Copernicus, Sigismund I, monetary reform, Diet of Piotrków, Sigismund Augustus, Stephen Báthory, debasement, ferto, Malbork regulation, currency stability</keywords>

# [DECORATIVE ILLUSTRATION]

->**Marbled Paper Endpaper**<-

This page features a full-page **marbled paper** design, a traditional technique used in historical bookbinding to decorate the inner covers and flyleaves of significant volumes. 

The pattern displayed here is the **nonpareil** <term>nonpareil: from the French word for "incomparable," this is a classic marbling pattern created by raking a fine-toothed comb through floating pigments to create tiny, uniform scalloped waves</term> or **comb** style. It is characterized by a multitude of small, interlocking crescent and scalloped shapes arranged in dense, rhythmic horizontal rows.

The color palette is vibrant and complex:
*   A **rich red** serves as the primary base color.
*   Repetitive accents of **blue**, **yellow**, **green**, and **white** are woven throughout the design.

The overall effect is a highly detailed, wavy texture that covers the entire surface of the leaf. <note>In the 19th century, such high-quality marbling was often used to signify the value and scholarly importance of a book's contents, particularly for works by thinkers like Oresme and Copernicus.</note>
